On Tuesday the sixth it comes up in **forty-one minutes**: a bellwright's core, four pieces of worked glass in a band of lead in the waist, a clapper with its tongue half worn through, and **it answers this harbor's own tide bell through the ground a hand's width of a second late, in Calder's hand, in front of four people**, and Bevin Rook writes a line with two things in it for the first time in four months. Mara Venn gives four things and will not give a date or a ceiling, and states the two limits: **the core may not be put into any shed in this harbor**, and it is not to be lifted again by any hand that has not been in the water with it, and the first of the two is why it will be hung. The bellwright will not open it in the wet and says fouling is not the enemy. Read out to the tide gang by the boy, twice, at the reeve's order.

### Chapter 47 — Three Foot Nine

Thursday the 8th of December. The piece of oak that came up on the fall on the sixth is entered by Bevin Rook on the seventh: a stern board, six foot with two foot of it left, rabbetted, three letters under the fouling, *A*, *s* and most of a *t*. The fragment comes up at two o'clock — a hand's breadth and a half of worked glass in two keyed portions, with **a ground band along its lower cut face, curved**, and the yard enters the *form* of it, quotes the conservator's own words of the second of October underneath it, states that no person in this harbor has compared it with the object in the custody room, and will not draw a line. Then the grating comes up and the work is done: **the clear opening inside the coaming, measured with a steel rule in the hand, is three foot nine by two foot four**, and the grate has been out of its seat and in its seat, worn bright on the inboard side only, with a stowage board that is not on the survey and a hasp broken at the stem. Bevin reads out the one thing all of it says, and **it takes three steps, because the chapter now carries the two it was missing**: *the room is under a grating in a deck and the hatch is the only way anything has ever gone into it or come out of it; the case cannot go through the hatch in or out; and this office's own file of the wreck, in a harbor's hand, in the year she went down, says number four, port side aft, grating in place, not cleared, contents not removed. So nothing can be taken out of that room, so it has not been cleared, and a paper that says the room stood empty is not describing the room at that moment.* The entry of the eighth and the reason in the reeve's book both now carry the file's citation. The reason goes into the reeve's book at eight in the morning, unedited. **The anchor is the stern board and not the brass bell**, because the bell is his brother's and will not go into a public record, and because the ship was in the room and is not a person. The scene: low on a loading space, a case on the shoulder, **the man with the paper behind him and the paper upside down**, a phrase with one word in it read twice and the word *empty*, four men and the count right, a tag with the third figure lost, a hand on the collar, and **the boy looks at the man and not at the case**, which is the only choice there was and the one Calder dislikes. Eleven minutes inside a limit of forty, surfaced on the tender's word, and on the boards he answers Nessa Pike in Oren's call mark and she goes white and holds a mooring post at the far end of the platform, and Bevin Rook writes *not written as a voice* and says aloud that he will not write one word about what came out of the man's mouth.

### Chapter 48 — The Tenth of December

The morning of the 9th, the 10th and the 13th of December. **The cost of the public route, felt in the body and not summarized**: Calder cannot find the end of the call — not the words, not the length, not the place in the middle where a boy's breath went, but *the lift*, and there is nothing there to remember, and it is his, and nobody can check it and nobody ever will. Nessa Pike reads the whole of the eighth's entry back and then says the price in one sentence: **a boy on a deck in a public record with no name on him, and that is the only way anybody will ever know he was there and also the only reason nobody will ever know which boy.** On Saturday the tenth Bevin Rook goes back in the water at nineteen minutes with his hand in his pocket on a cradle in eight feet and Nessa Pike says **Enough** and he comes up angry and she does not apologize and does not look away, and she says the difference of seven minutes is the difference between a man who is fit and a man who is not. On Tuesday the 13th the fragment is carried to the Morrow circuit custody room and entered as the **third** item under a third number, as *not compared by this office with any other item*, under the same seal line, and a municipal claim is entered against it **for the purpose of a record and not for the purpose of possession** — and Kest tells a hearing room on the twentieth that a claim that asks for nothing is the only kind that can be entered without a countersignature and **cannot be withdrawn without a request, and the yard will not make one.** Then Nessa reads out the money, makes the boy read it back twice, and tells him the word *wages* is coming out of the book on Wednesday and *claim* is going in, because he asked and not because anybody told her to.

### Chapter 49 — What a Record Can Carry

The 17th to the 21st of December. The public sheet goes up on the market wall in three parts in four hands, with the limits written inside the rules, including the four lines that say no office has ruled on any part of it and that the circuit office has custody of three items and has given a reason for none of them. There is no money to print it: Elias Voke carries one to the herring skippers, Harl Dace reads it at the net loft, Sella Brigg reads it at the head of her own boat, and **Mara Venn certifies the method for nothing and has the yard write *nothing* on the back of it in Calder's own hand** — and says the query comes off her file on the fourth of January. On the twentieth a paper comes from the circuit office which is not a request: the three items are re-entered from *presumed Crown material until a recognized witness or a municipal claimant is entered against it* to *municipal claim entered, contested*, with the same seal line and **no reason**. Kest reads it out in the hearing room on the 21st and says four things, of which the fourth is a fact about a file: **there is no paper anywhere that states what a municipal claim does to a presumed Crown material, and the condition in the order of the fourteenth of October was satisfied on the thirteenth of this month and the order has not been lifted, and the *Vigil* is the second office in this harbor that has never been told.** The officer asks for the harbor authority's own chart and Kest refuses in eleven seconds with the whole of his method in the open — a reeve's request is a pressure — and records that the yard has no right to ask for that chart and has not asked. Then Sera Mott, at the mole head, says the other half of the new-volume question out loud: there was more of them out there, she has heard this one answered from the westward and always late, she was asked by archivists and said no and believed them, and **the ones she knows about stopped because the men who kept them stopped going out**, and asks for nothing to be written — and Bevin writes one line saying she asked for that.

### Chapter 50 — Slack Water

Monday the 26th of December. The core is hung on **Sunday the 25th** on a harbor shackle, a harbor chain and a short moor in eight and a half feet, and the clerk reads off a chart notation in Kest's own hand that has never been taken off: *maintained by the Free Keel yard, not a mark and not a light and not a danger, a signal kept by hand, at the cost stated in this office's hearing record of the twenty-eighth of November.* On the 26th the tide bell goes and the core answers, and under the answer there is **a call — three marks and a flick, in a grown man's voice, unhurried — with a lift on the end of it**, and it is not a boy's and it is not his, and Bevin Rook writes *knock, delayed, with a call in it. Three marks and a lift. Adult. Not written as a voice* and says he will have a view on Thursday and will not have it tonight. Mara comes out to the mole to say she is out of standing on the fourth of January, refuses to say out loud the thing she has believed since the fourteenth of October, and says the best thing in the chapter: **a record that says a boy was there and does not say who he was is the only honest form of that page, and it exists because the man who made it cannot bear to write his brother's name in an office where a seal with a senior examiner's name under it can be read by a man he will never meet.** Calder takes down the unverified note, sees that two words would make its one false line true, and **puts it back on its own shelf with the false line in it**, because a true slate with a boy's name on it is a document in a yard any man may read. Kest says out loud, in the open air, that the charter may be suspended after an inspection, that he has no cause and will not suspend it, and then names the **nineteenth of January** for the mole's toe — because the run does not stop at the mole or the shoal, it is under the town, and this office has no chart of what is under the town because the town was built on it. **A provisional charter is a leash, and a longer one than the leash he had.** Ends at slack water on the mole face, the first of March nine weeks and two days off and a Wednesday against a Monday, and the three of them signing the year out in a shed with the stove going while outside the tide is making on the mole face and the bell is answering.

## Volume 02, Batch 0001 (Chapters 51–60) — complete. Movement 1, the leash opens.

### Chapter 51 — Five Marks in a Bag

Wednesday the fourth of January. The yard's position is read back figure by figure and the number comes out the same as it has since the first of December, with one addition to the book: the fact that the yard's own count of the weeks on the chandler's note is a figure in Calder's head and not a number anybody can check, entered by Bevin Rook on his own motion. **Mara Venn's query comes off her file and she says the arithmetic out loud in the yard**: a conservator with nothing left to refuse is a conservator an office will find expensive to employ, and a certificate takes three weeks that nobody can hurry. Calder asks the chandler — for the first time in four months — to write a new figure, and the chandler writes **nineteen marks at twenty-two weeks**, with a line under it in nineteen years of a ledger that has never held one: *entered at the asking, with a request that it be paid as the first money that comes into the yard and not out of a bag that cannot.* **The promise of the second of December is broken out loud and named as broken**, and the boy reads the money back twice. Ends in the road outside the market wall, where a man off a cutter's boat asks, pleasantly and in front of eleven people, whether the crew means to be working inside the line this season, and gets a one-sentence answer, and the boy enters the question, the words, the hour, the date, and the fact that it was not asked twice. Cost: the yard's position is legible off a wall of its own making in four minutes, and the only gain of the day is a true figure that is worse than the old one. The second request from the circuit office is in Mara's bag, unopened.

### Chapter 52 — The Second Request

Thursday the fifth, in the Morrow circuit office copying room, the only room in the batch. The second request is read out with the door of the room's own business open: it asks the yard to deliver the public sheet and all ten copies of it into the custody room so that the record of a contested material may be single, and **a party declining must state its ground in writing within fourteen days or is treated as not having declined, and a request returned without a ground comes back with a second seal, and the second seal is a seal of delay.** Mara Venn is not the addressee and is named in the copy as a person who has already declined a related request, and says so in the road in four sentences and is not thanked. Calder buys nothing and refuses nothing and pays **three marks for the request to be entered and dated** — the cheapest thing an office sells and the only thing in the room that is a fact — and reads the date back into the crew's book in his own left hand. The copying clerk, filling in a box, says what an unanswered request costs in weeks, and the office adds one line nobody asked for: **the fourteen days run from the date of the paper and not the date of receipt, and six of them are gone, and six remain, and the fourteenth is the thirteenth of January** — and the clerk's *fifteenth day* and *twenty-ninth day* are given as dates as well, **the thirteenth and the twenty-seventh of January**, so that the day-count cannot be read as the twenty-ninth of January. Calder asks Mara a question he has no right to ask and is given the figure — a certificate of method is **six marks and three weeks** — and does not pay it and does not resent her. **The bag is two.** Closes with a decision with a bill attached: a date bought for three marks, a fortnight in front of them that the yard cannot earn anything into.

### Chapter 53 — The Warp on the North Wall

Friday the sixth to Saturday the seventh. Elias Voke moves his ring and warp one berth along the north wall because the north berth has been out of service since the first of December, at **twenty-four marks for two days** — a bad rate, agreed before the work, with the eighth and the share named out loud before it is done. The chapter states the only reason the yard has money: **ordinary harbor work landward of the third seam is outside the closure of the notice of the thirty-first**, and a herring skipper's own ring on his own account needs no permit, because that notice closes the mole's toe and inner face from the third to the eleventh seam and no notice has ever closed water. Bevin takes the capstan bar on one hand and works it out himself, and is told the true thing in the road rather than being helped. Out of twenty-four: the harbor's eighth three, the harbor's share three, rope and victuals three, yard's share fifteen, and **the first mark on the chandler's note since the eighteenth of November**, put on the day it was earned and not on a Monday. Neale, the tide gang's foreman, comes down the mole steps to say three things: the ninth seam is still out with three men stood by and nowhere to put the days, the coast office's word *capacity* is still unanswered a month on, and **the first of March is a Wednesday seven weeks and five days off with forty boats coming in and one berth short, and the north berth is the one that takes a northeast blow.** Ends on a cost named by somebody else: a man who works under the leash says out loud what the leash is for.

### Chapter 54 — What a Boy Is Owed in June

Monday the ninth in a front room behind the fish market, Tuesday the tenth in the shed. The mother asks for the truth in daylight with the book open and is told the second sentence and not the first. She produces the surgeon's original out of her apron, holds it up, and does not read it, and asks one question that is not about money: **whether the plate at the fifth seam was worth her son.** The hand is four months on; the bonesetter's word in January is a different word from November's, and she gives it in front of the boy because he is twenty-two and is owed a forecast — *it will close, it will not be worth what it was, and I will not promise you June; what is in my book is a boat hand in June, and that is not a promise and it is not a lie.* Nessa Pike says the true thing in front of the boy about what a hand and a book together are, and is not gentle. **The turn is the boy's own**: a claim is a thing you can hold and a wage is a thing you can spend, and he asks for a *worse* bargain than he could have asked for — a hand at four a week from the first of February, on a day the yard can name and cannot pay until it raises something, in addition to the forty marks, which stand and are not reduced. He worked out **by himself that the first of February is a Wednesday** — from the first of March being a Wednesday and a February being four weeks, checked a second time off the Mondays — and said so, and Calder Marr did not explain it to him. Entered in the yard's book in his own right hand and countersigned by Pike, with the date, and the word *wages* is not put back. Cost: the yard has named a wage it has no money for, and the mother has been told once and is not to be told again in a room.

### Chapter 55 — The Going Out

Wednesday the eleventh, a fine day, which is the point. **The bellwright is named in a person's mouth** — Nessa Pike: *Corvin Sarn sent for a woman on the sixth of December and nobody in this yard has asked him a question in four months* — and Calder Marr asks him a question with the book open, and Sarn chooses the record. He states the cost of a signal in his own trade's terms: a rope a year, a man's day every four or five years for the tongue, and a person in a boat in the weather the rest of the time; the fouling cannot be cleaned and must not be, and he will not open a bell in the wet, and the whole of the craft is the going out. **The crew's share of the day is offered and refused in one sentence** — a man who sells his going out is a man whose going out belongs to somebody — and the offer and the refusal are both entered. The delayed note is logged a ninth time in the flat form, the span from the twenty-sixth of October read aloud, **and the answer is the note with no call in it, for the first time, and the boy enters the fact and nobody asks him a question about it.** Then the mechanism, in Sarn's trade words and not as a mystery: the core's seaward face carries a pale scour where thirteen years of fouling has been taking gravel off it every flood, and the plate under the mole's root has had nothing moving over it since the year the Cut happened, and **a bell only speaks while somebody keeps the weather working on it.** Cost: eight marks, a day of a boat, a hired warp and a hired pump, and the knowledge that it will be the same again in a year and the year after.

### Chapter 56 — Thirteen Minutes Past the Mark

Thursday the twelfth to Saturday the fourteenth, in a rising southerly. The reason goes into the harbor authority's book at eight, in Calder's own left hand, unedited, with the clerk present, and **it names a hand and a lead and does not name a diver, because the notice of the thirty-first names Calder, Pike and Rook in its third clause and stops all three from the water on the authority's work.** The diver is Tam Slee of the *Grey Widow*, hired by Elias Voke on his own account. At nineteen minutes the chain comes tight, the fall comes down the line, and Bevin Rook turns his body and puts the arm already in the sling into it. **Nessa Pike calls *Enough*, does not apologize, does not soften it and does not look away**, and then says the thing that belongs in the chapter: it is the sixth overrule since the twenty-fifth of October and the first one that has cost somebody a hand, and she wants it in the book in her own words. Kest's own entry under the reason is the worse one: the ground, the stone, the shackle, the mooring and the fouling are all the authority's, and the reason the fouling is not being narrowed is a notice the office did not write naming three men. **The reason also carries a sentence nobody had put there before — that the first clause of that notice closes this ground to all work and all approach by all persons including this office, that a permit of this office cannot lift a notice of the circuit office, and that this office is putting a man inside that closure and is entering the fact of it — and Calder puts the reeve's own November ground to him in the room, that this office will not put a person inside a closure its own notice created, and Kest orders the clause written on the sheet and read back and not softened by a word.** The stone is carried on the Saturday with **a hand crane hired and paid for by the harbor authority** out of its own repair account, and the yard's list does not carry it twice. The bonesetter gives a different word from November's and will not promise June. The money: nineteen on the board, thirteen out, the yard's six, a bonesetter's mark, **the bag is thirteen.** Ends on the fact that the yard has been in the water on every date the authority has named since the sixth of December and has not been paid for one of them.

### Chapter 57 — The Bill for Thirteen

Sunday the fifteenth to Monday the sixteenth. Recovery, and a supply problem understood rather than announced: **a cracked housing is not a cracked housing, it is a pump that cannot be hired out, and that is four months of a bad winter.** The harbor's crane and barge are the harbor's and the harbor pays; the harbor's rate is what a man with no pump is charged. The boy's mother comes to the shed and asks for the entry about a bonesetter changing her word in front of her, and it is written down in Nessa Pike's hand. In the net loft **Harl Dace of the *Grey Widow* asks the question the yard cannot answer** — *who is it for* — after establishing that the sheet binds nobody and consents to nobody, which is what he needed to know, and the yard has no answer and says so. **The change: Calder stops asking for a charter question and asks for a specific thing on a specific date, and asks Nessa Pike what the yard can do, and takes her answer, and it is smaller than what he wanted and he does not argue once.** The bench is reassigned in writing in her hand alone, including the *Low Lantern*'s cover entered as a fact with a date — the third thing she has signed away in four months. **The tinnitus is named once**: he loses the harbor's crane, asks for a sentence to be said again, and is told it is the fourth time since the fourteenth of October and will be the twentieth. Money: air two, rope one and a bonesetter's mark one make four, so the bag is nine; **the week's bread and oil and candles, five, goes onto the note at the chandler's and is paid out of the bag the same afternoon, because a figure written on a note is not a figure paid** — so the note is at thirteen and **the bag is four, smaller than the figure on the sixth of December.** The pump is not fitted before the spring, **the eighth of March is a Wednesday**, and the day ends with a wage that starts on a Wednesday in a fortnight's time.

### Chapter 58 — What the Chart Costs

Tuesday the seventeenth, in the reeve's barge on the west basin. Kest pays the harbor's part of the twelfth out loud and in his own book — Neale's gang one day on the wall and a hand crane for two days, **seven marks for a day of work this office did not get done** — and says the part he cannot pay is the boy's wrist, and will not pretend it is his to pay. Then **he says the price of the nineteenth of January before he grants it**: a fourth set of moorings, the ninth seam's, and the herring fleet's winter warp bought out of the authority's own account for the season, cut and laid by Neale's gang in the fortnight before the first of March. The cost falls on the tide gang, on Elias Voke's claim in practice and not on paper, and on forty boats, and **the nineteen winters stay disputed and unpaid and the money goes on warp instead.** The ground is named in the paper in Kest's own hand and the naming is read back, which is the thing Bevin found the purpose of the twenty-first lacked. **And Kest says out loud what is not on the paper: the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first closes the ground he has just named, he named ground his own notice closes, he did the same on the twelfth and entered it, this is the second time, and he wants the yard to be holding it as well as he is.** He also says the first of February **is a Wednesday**, and that he has not told the boy the fourteen marks are coming and is not going to. **The nineteenth is paid as a day: eighteen marks out of the authority's own account, the eighth and share off anything raised and nothing to be raised, in the yard's hands on the first of February.** A proper eight-clause plan for the fifth seam is lodged with a fisher's hand on the delivery and is not answered in January, and the chapter says plainly that it will be refused again, correctly, and that the mechanism is a change of politics in an office and not a better argument from a shed. The reeve's own man reads out three of the men who are going to pay for it.

### Chapter 59 — The Day Before

Wednesday the eighteenth. Not a room. **A man on a market stall who is owed nothing asks for a copy of the public record to keep** — Amos Pryde, a coaster's master with a hand hired out of the market for nineteen years, who wants it for a boy of nineteen who cannot read a manifest — and the yard gives him one, and **the count of the printed crew copies goes from ten to eleven and both numbers are true of their own dates**, with the count entered out and in and the place recorded, and the naval rating's slate now holds a number that is a week out of date, and that is the consent question's first cost and it is free. The reason for the morrow is written: **two hundred and sixty-nine words with no diver in them**, read out, read back, entered. **Bevin Rook finds the sentence nobody else found**, and counts it — *the run of the bedded material does not stop at the mole, and the party does not know where the run goes and is not asking this office to know on the nineteenth*, eleven words, and the only sentence in the reason that is not an instruction — and explains what it will do to a reeve and to a ledger in Veyr in four years. Nessa Pike says what the reason does not contain and why: no purpose, because of the eighth of December and the twenty-sixth, and **the omission is entered in the yard's own book as an omission, with a date, in the boy's own right hand, the first thing he has entered this month that nobody asked him for.** The run's near end is not at the mole: it is in the yard's own record since the twenty-eighth of October, forty feet of bedded seam running north under the rubble, of which the yard has used the south end as a position.

### Chapter 60 — Under the Town

Thursday the nineteenth, the top of the flood, in the slop. The reason goes into the reeve's book at eight in Calder's own left hand, unedited, with the clerk present, and it **names a hand lead and a slate and does not name a man going in**, and Kest adds a line saying so and that the office does not require it to name one. The harbor's crane, barge, clerk and tide gang come out on the authority's account; the harbor's man stands four minutes without speaking. Four hundred feet of figures on a slate: **nine feet at the south end**, which is the tender's count of the twenty-eighth of October and whose number the boy gives as the hearing record's, with his own count of it as seven; **ten and a half forty feet north at the north end of the bedded material**, the tender's count, with his own count of that as eleven given aloud on the rule that goes with it; **seven and three-eighths at the mole's root**; and then **five and a quarter, sounding landward, under a stone quay**, sounded four times. **Nine and a quarter is the *Aster Gannet's* ferry plate beyond the eleventh seam and is a different object, and the two are not fused.** Kest writes a **second** line under the reason, in his own hand, saying that the first clause of the notice closes this ground, that he named it on the seventeenth and knew what he was doing, and that a man in Veyr will find two such entries in one ledger. The delayed note is logged a tenth time in the flat form, the span read aloud, and **the answer is the note again with no call in it, the second time running, entered as a fact because it is a fact** — the eleventh of January was the first, and every earlier logged occasion had a call in it. Calder stops the day at twenty past eleven at a figure that is not the figure the yard went out for, **and says out loud in the boat why: a route that no paper has ever been written for cannot be asked about in a hearing room, and a man who cannot ask is a man with no instrument** — and the boy writes it down and reads it back and adds, in his own hand, that the man who calls the day is the man who does not get to be the man who goes on. The day is paid as a day at eighteen marks, payable on the first of February, and the bag is four and the tin is empty. The leash is named as a leash, the first of March is named as a Wednesday in six weeks less a day with forty herring boats and one berth short, the coast office is still unanswered, the notice is still on the wall, and the run goes under a town that was built on it and that the harbor authority has no chart of.

## Volume 02, Batch 0002 (Chapters 61–70) — complete. Movement 2, weather and the capacity of a harbor.

**Actual word count, re-measured by the Volume 02 audit after the Batch 0002 review repair: 31,300 words across ten chapters** (Ch61 3,024 · Ch62 3,502 · Ch63 3,195 · Ch64 2,752 · Ch65 3,498 · Ch66 3,275 · Ch67 3,062 · Ch68 2,790 · Ch69 2,636 · Ch70 3,566). **The figure of 30,995 and the per-chapter figures for Chapters 61, 63, 65 and 68 that stood here were measured before the review repair was applied and were never recomputed; the chapters are the authority and the corrected figures are above.** **Six chapters inside the 2,200–3,200 guideline and four past it**, the largest (Ch70, the batch's one hearing) about eleven per cent over, and no chapter anywhere near Volume 01's Ch31 or Ch40. **No chapter in the batch is two rooms**, and the batch's pressures alternate: supply understood, a rival crew, ordinary paid work, an office, money and a boy, a closure and a record, aftermath, labour in weather, a recovery that pays, and a sitting. **Edric Pringle's pronouns in Chapters 62 and 69 were repaired mechanically by the volume audit; see `reviews/volume-02/volume-02-close.md` Part 2, item 1.**

### Chapter 61 — The List of Things That Exist

Friday the twentieth to Saturday the twenty-first of January, in the shed and on the hard. **The supply problem is understood and not announced**: a pump with a web cracked in the housing and no new one on this coast before the eighth of March, which means it cannot be worked *and cannot be hired out*, and hiring the pump out is how a yard in this harbor pays for a bad winter. The air pot a third gone, the *Kittiwake* on the hard with her bilge blocked in and no cradle, the hand lead, the slate, the boat that is not the yard's, the twenty marks of iron that are the charter's bond and not capital, the bell's eight marks and a day of a boat a year, and a boy. **Bevin Rook refuses figures in the yard's own book** — a list with figures is a list of what a man is worth — and writes nine heads with no figures and the date, and the hearing room's slate wall is wiped on the Monday the thirtieth at eight and the ledger is the record. **The turn is the boy's**: the yard is not three and a half hands, it is three men and a book's worth of hands, because a notice names the three of us and that is worth more to a man who wants to hire a crew here than the price of the hire. Ends with **Noll Tarner of the *Sea Wren* on the steps with a dredged pump housing in each hand**, who says the rate out loud before he can be cheated, and who has a claim window on the twelfth seam. No money moves. The bag is four.

### Chapter 62 — A Pump That Works

Monday the twenty-third to Tuesday the twenty-fourth. **The rival crew, and it is not a villain.** Noll Tarner, thirty-four, of the *Sea Wren*, has a working pump, two and a half hands, one of whom cannot read, **a claim window on the twelfth seam for the twenty-sixth, which the notice of the thirty-first does not close**, and no rescue cover, because Sella Brigg's went off the slate on the twenty-ninth of October and the *Sea Wren* is off the Free Keel's slate since November for taking a job at Ashtide without telling his own crew first, which he says in one sentence and does not apologise for. He cannot write a reason, and **Bevin Rook refuses to write it for him and explains why in the yard's own history** — the reason of the nineteenth has a hole in it that a boy entered with a date on it — and offers instead to come down to his boat and take it in his words, which is a bigger thing. Tarner asks the question nobody has asked: **whose book is the day in, and can a man come and read my dive in your book, and what is it for** — and the boy answers it in two columns, the checkable and the not, and says that a man who can read him has to be able to read himself. **The turn is the reeve's**: Kest reads the reason and does *not* write under it for the first time since the twelfth of January, and says why out loud in a room, and then writes one sentence anyway, that this yard has been sent to him for work because its own ground is shut. Money: none.

### Chapter 63 — The Twelfth Seam

Thursday the twenty-sixth to Friday the twenty-seventh. Ordinary work in good weather, and the first lawful ground in six weeks. A herring boat's ring, shackle and forty feet of fouled warp come out from under the mole's foot at the twelfth seam, on Edric Pringle's own account, with no diver and nothing raised. **The diver sets his own limit at the boat, out loud, and the boy writes that the limit was set by the diver, which is the first limit in this yard's book not set by a man in a coat.** The boy is asked to take a heaving line and says no in front of five people, in his own words, about his own hand, and Nessa Pike enters the fact and states that there is no rule and she is not making one. Tarner asks for his own count of twenty-one to go in, and it goes in the second column with a reason, and the first column holds nineteen because a man on a boat can check nineteen. **The tide gang stand at the twelfth, which is not in the clause, and write on their own slate that nothing in the water was glass.** Money: **twenty-six gross, the eighth three, the share three, air two, rope one, the *Sea Wren's* pump a mark — sixteen out, the yard's, and the bag is twenty.** The yard copies the day's page for Tarner to keep and not return, and Tarner says the thing nobody in the Free Keel says: *if a man in Veyr ever comes up that road, the first thing he will learn about this harbor is that a yard put its own errors on a public sheet and asked a fleet to check them, and I have never in my life seen a fleet check anything.* Ends with the boy entering, unasked, that **the Free Keel yard's book is now the book of another crew's work.**

### Chapter 64 — The Second Seal

Friday the twenty-seventh to Saturday the twenty-eighth. The circuit office's second request arrives on the twenty-seventh, **which is the twenty-ninth day from the thirtieth of December, and the twenty-ninth is a day and not the twenty-ninth of January.** It is the same four paragraphs with **nineteen words in the office's own type, set before anybody in this harbor was born: *That no ground having been received, the said request stands unanswered in this office, and this office awaits the same.*** A delay that costs the office nothing and the party the count of the days. **The yard enters the arrival and does not answer and does not refuse**, because a dated refusal is an answer and an answer chooses the office's next move. **Mara Venn prices it in the order Calder asks for — three figures in that order, as a figure and not as a decision — and says the words *do not make me useful*, and says she is a trade and a trade does not say no, and asks for the cost of her own nothing to be written in the yard's book so she can point at a page instead of a memory.** She asks for six marks on the seventh of February and does not ask twice. **The boy finds the line nobody else read: the office's own type still says ten copies, and the count is eleven, and the second seal was affixed today by somebody who had to write that count.** Three facts entered, no opinion, and the third is that nobody in the shed knows whether the office has not been told, has been told and has not corrected it, or has been told and has left it standing. Money: air two, rope one. The bag is seventeen.

### Chapter 65 — Four Marks on a Wednesday

Monday the thirtieth of January to Tuesday the seventh of February, and then forward to Tuesday the twenty-first. The whole of the first of February in the shed. **The harbor authority's fourteen marks arrive on a Wednesday out of the office's own account**, in a canvas bag, read out of the receipt and not out of the bag, and **the boy's first four marks are paid on the same afternoon** — and the boy asks that the source be entered, because *a wage I do not name the source of is a wage a man can be quietly proud of, and I would rather it was a wage I could say out loud in a market.* **Sella Brigg's entered third of the iron, a true figure of six marks, eight shillings and tenpence, is paid at the round figure of six marks at the counter — she counts it twice, names the round six, and says she will not be handed a tidy sum and be told the rest of it is understood — the eight shillings and tenpence is entered in her name on the same line and is not paid, and she says the yard is the party and she is the crew that keeps the party solvent**, and she names the two men in this harbor a yard can hire off the wall in a blow, one of whom she has had a falling out with. **On the twenty-first the fourth week falls due, there is one mark in the bag, and the boy enters the unpaid week in his own right hand and states that he will not ask and that the book is the wrong place to ask**, and that four marks is not a thing a man asks for twice, and that a man who can do his own arithmetic is a man nobody has to lie to about money. **Mara Venn asks for six marks for a certificate of method and cannot be paid, and says in daylight that the plate in the mole is the only thing a conservator's certificate could ever put a hand on, that she cannot write one because a certificate is an act of an office, and that in about two years somebody will say she stood on a wall and knew the ground, and it will be worth nothing.** Cost: the note to four, the bag to one, Mara's forty unpaid, Sella's cover gone since October.

### Chapter 66 — The Man Who Is Not Allowed

Wednesday the fifteenth to Friday the seventeenth of February, at the ninth seam, in a south-easter and rain. **The first clause of the notice of the thirty-first closes the toe and inner face of that mole from the third seam to the eleventh to all work and to all approach by all persons, which includes this office, and the crew stands at the crown above the marker at the eleventh and does not go down the face.** Voke cannot write a reason, so **Calder writes Voke's reason in his own left hand at eight in the morning with the clerk there**, and it is the first reason this office has had from a party in four months that has no hole in it — and the sentence Calder wanted in it is about the fouling on the ground line between the third and the ninth, in the fourth paragraph where a clerk will not skip it. **Kest writes the fourth acknowledgement under it in his own hand.** The stone comes off in nine minutes and the shackle is a sleeve of the mole's own foundation stone cast round it, so the work takes two goes on the same day and Neale is the named tender with the cutoff. **At the top of the crown Calder Marr says he was the diver and the thing that stopped him is a piece of paper, and that he does not know whether that is justice or a waste, and has no right to an opinion in front of the clerk, and asks that it be entered that he did not have one — the only line in four months he has entered in a municipal book that says he did not have a right to an opinion.** **Bevin Rook then asks the harbor's clerk for the book and writes the whole of the afternoon in the authority's own ledger in two columns in his own right hand, with the clerk initialling the foot of it, and enters that he was not in the water and was not on the face and was not allowed to be, and that every figure in the first column is a figure he took off a man's slate and not one he saw.** The mooring of the ninth seam is in service from four o'clock and it is a mooring and not a berth. The yard is paid nothing and has lost a day it could not sell. The fouling on the third-to-ninth line is untouched and is now a fact in a reason in an office's book.

### Chapter 67 — The Bill for the Ninth Seam

Wednesday the twenty-second to Friday the twenty-fourth of February. **The tinnitus is named again**: he loses the harbor's crane, asks for a sentence to be said again, and is told it is the fifth time since the fourteenth of October and that no paper in this harbor or in Veyr takes it out of a man's head. The fouling goes into the authority's store and not into any shed, because the second clause has never once in five months been argued with. **Neale says the three men are off on the twenty-fourth and will not be on again until the fourth of March, and that the reason is a harbor authority that spent its own repair account on four sets of moorings and a season of warp in the fortnight before it promised them in a hearing room, and one of the three is a man whose brother went down twice on the seventeenth under his eye and has not said one word about it.** **Noll Tarner hires out Wend Crale at two marks a day and two days, a hire and not a kindness, and the yard enters in advance that if the yard has nothing on the day the hand is not paid on the day and the reason goes in the book** — the first time the boy has written a promise to somebody outside the yard about money that has not arrived. The arithmetic goes up on the slate in the boy's own right hand and comes true, and Calder enters that he would not have hired him and would not have found him, and that he has been treating a crew of three as a crew of three and a half and the arithmetic has known it since the fifteenth of January. **On the mole steps in the wind Calder comes within four inches of asking the boy what came out of a man's mouth on the eighteenth of January and does not, and the boy enters that neither of them asked, and that two of them keeping a thing out of a book is twice as safe and half as honest, and that he would rather it was half as honest than twice as safe.** The bell is heard from the top step and it is not an occasion, because an occasion is a thing a man goes out in a boat to find out.

### Chapter 68 — Tarred Marline

Sunday the twenty-sixth to Monday the twenty-seventh of February, on the north wall in a hard north-easter. **The going out, entered for the first time out of the yard's own pocket and not paid, eight marks and a day of a boat under a rule because it is the same figure as the eleventh of January and the whole of the point of a signal is that it is the only line in a ledger that is the same on every page — and the yard cannot pay it, having eight shillings and tenpence of a woman's third standing on its own slate and a boy's four of the first of March in the same thirteen marks, so it stands owing with the date on it and is the first figure the yard has entered this winter knowing it would not be paid.** Corvin Sarn comes because the ground and the gear are the harbor's, thumb on the ground line, and says the line is good and the waist is the same and the scour is clean, and then says the only new thing anybody has said about it in five months: **he has heard the answer nine times and has never heard a voice in it, and on the twenty-seventh there was one, and he is a man who works with bells and does not know what it is, and it goes in the book in his own words.** **The delayed note is logged an eleventh time and the answer has a call in it** — longer than the twenty-sixth of December's and further off, a grown man's, not written as a voice, no source — **and the boy enters that there have been two occasions logged with no call in them, the eleventh and the nineteenth of January, and that this is not one of them, and that the count of those is two and it is a fact and it is not a question, and nobody in the boat asks.** Ninety fathoms of the authority's tarred marline go on the north wall and the boy calls the time and the tide and the state of the water eighty-one times without being told he is doing it wrong. **At twenty past twelve the harbor authority's own shackle, out of its own moorings gear and nine years in salt water, opens and forty feet of the authority's marline goes over the wall and out into the harbor in about two seconds** — and the cause is entered in the first column because every part of it can be checked by asking a man who was on the wall or in a barge, and the forty feet goes in the second column with the man who lost it, because it cannot be checked. Fifty fathoms laid by four in the afternoon, where ninety were to lie. Money: **twenty-six gross, the eighth three, the share three, air two, rope two, a hand four — fourteen out, the yard's twelve, and the bag is thirteen.**

### Chapter 69 — Forty Feet of Warp

Monday the twenty-seventh to Tuesday the twenty-eighth of February, on the north wall. **Edric Pringle comes down at seven with the *Anna Beth*'s ring gone and forty feet of her warp gone, and asks that the cause be said first by the yard and not by her, and the yard says it: a shackle through a stone post that never had a shackle in it, in a wall worked on the twenty-seventh, changing the run of the water at the head — and Kest had said in his own barge on Thursday that there was no better iron and would not buy iron in the last week of February, and the yard accepted that and worked on it anyway, and both of those are the yard's.** Fourteen gross, the eighth and the share two and two, air two, rope one, and **Wend Crale's second day, which he says at eight in the morning he is not fit for, and is paid for, and is given the crate of fish the mutual account put up, and Nessa Pike enters that a hired hand told the yard he was not fit and was not asked to be and that a man who does not take the two marks makes the book a lie.** There is no permit and no diver and no reason in any office's book, and the yard keeps the practice in its own book anyway and will not have the two confused. The ring is found on the fourth sounding, under a coping stone off the wall's head, and is got out with a boat warp and a chain in nineteen minutes on the ebb. Money: **nine out of fourteen, the yard's five, and the fortnight's food is four this week because a third of a crate of fish is a thing and not a figure.** **The fortnight's bread, oil and candles goes onto the note on the twenty-eighth and comes out of the bag, three, and the note stands at one.** Ends with the boy who cannot read saying that a man called Neave Tarrant in the third lay is out in the blow and is not back till Wednesday, and that there are forty boats coming in and somebody counted forty this morning and somebody else counted forty-one.

### Chapter 70 — A Statement of Capacity

Wednesday the first of March, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store, which Kest has called himself for the first time since the twenty-eighth of November. **Forty-one boats in the basin**, the forty-first anchored in the roads, and the difference of one boat between two figures in the same harbor in the same week is the whole of the difference between a berth short and a harbor that is short. **Two documents.** The first is the harbor authority's own statement of what it has spent since the twenty-eighth of November — four sets of moorings, ninety fathoms of warp, a day of gang and a hand crane for work it did not get done, the ninth seam worked on the seventeenth by a hired diver, forty feet of its own marline in the sea, a ring and forty-one feet of warp belonging to a herring skipper, three men off from the twenty-fourth, **and nineteen winters of dues untouched** — and Kest reads it out because a reeve who writes a clever version has to be clever again in four years. **The second is Elias Voke's, written at his own table on Saturday night, for nothing, in his own hand, and read out by him.** Its first line is that he does not know what the word *capacity* means and has had eleven weeks to find out. It names two berths and a mole, the north berth out of service since the first of December and the one that takes a north-easter, the ninth seam's mooring in service **and a mooring is not a berth and he will not let them be the same thing in a piece of paper**, **the hard at Ashtide applied for on the ninth of November and unanswered in eleven weeks**, and the *Kittiwake* on the hard with her bilge blocked in and no cradle. **It names Bevin Rook.** The boy stands up in a hearing room and says that three of the four weeks are paid, that the fourth fell due on the twenty-first and was not, that he did not ask, and that it was paid at seven o'clock that morning out of a column he worked out himself on a slate on Saturday night — and the clerk says he has not got a box for that, and Kest orders it into the margin in the clerk's own hand, initialed, unsummarized, the fourth thing this quarter to go into that office's book in something other than a box. **Mara Venn says before she is asked that the room will not get a conservator's opinion out of her, because she has no standing and is named in a copy of the thirtieth of December as a door already tried, and nobody in the room asks Calder Marr whether he wishes to have her asked, and he notices that it was not asked.** Kest then reads the second seal of the twenty-seventh of January into the record on his own motion. **The turn:** the coast office's word is answered, and the answer is a document the yard did not write and cannot fix — and a one-season provisional working charter runs to the last of March and whatever comes back from Veyr decides whether there is a second season, and the yard's part in that is nothing. The bell answers the harbor's tide bell through the ground a hand's width of a second late and **the twelfth occasion is not entered anywhere, because the boy was in a municipal room with a ledger that was not his and would not put a line of his own into it.**

## Volume 02, Batch 0003 (Chapters 71–80) — complete. Movement 3, the paper that asks for the book.

**Actual word count: 26,358 words across ten chapters** (Ch71 2,679 · Ch72 2,527 · Ch73 2,348 · Ch74 2,535 · Ch75 2,333 · Ch76 3,302 · Ch77 2,639 · Ch78 2,356 · Ch79 2,261 · Ch80 3,378), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the 2,200 floor; eight are inside the guideline and two are past it** (Ch76 at three per cent over, the batch's one hearing room, and Ch80 at five and a half per cent over, the batch's one complete day). The batch's pressures alternate: a decision and its price, a confiscation, a journey and a counter, a supply problem paid, an argument between two people who both have a right, a hearing, a market, labour and a missing man, a refusal and a market question, and a ledger. **No chapter in the batch is two rooms.** These figures are the post-repair ones; the Chapter 80 staging change is recorded in `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0003 review repairs*.

### Chapter 71 — A Ground and Not a Refusal

Thursday the second of March, in the shed. **The yard decides to answer the circuit office with a stated ground and not a refusal, because a dated refusal is an answer and an answer lets the office choose its own next move, and because a delay is free and the file is what a coast office and a naval officer will both read.** The ground is four paragraphs in Calder Marr's own left hand: what the sheet is; the count told twice in public with both figures and neither struck out; **the party cannot deliver what is not in the party's hand — of the eleven copies, two on the yard's own wall, five in the bench chest, three on the walls of crews who are not parties and whom the yard has no power to ask, and one with Amos Pryde at his stall, given at his own asking on the eighteenth of January to be kept and not returned**; and, found at eleven o'clock by the boy and read back four times because a thing found afterwards has to be proved was there, **the fourth paragraph: the yard's own book contains the twenty-sixth of January, which is the *Sea Wren's* day and not the yard's, entered at his own asking, and the *Sea Wren* is not a party, has not been asked, and cannot be asked, so the party cannot deliver a leaf either.** **Mara Venn prices it — three marks and two days — and refuses the asking of whether the ground is a good one, and says a woman with no office behind her who is asked whether a ground is good is a woman being made useful.** The two days go in as two days and not as a figure, with Nessa Pike's objection signed against it, and Calder Marr's statement that he does not know what a day is worth. The seven copies the yard does hold are counted twice out of the chest and the date goes on it. Cost: three marks, two days out of a shed of three men and a boy, and a fourth job refused in consequence. Thread moved: the second request and the second seal, from unanswered to answered once.

### Chapter 72 — A Form and a Signature Line

Saturday the fourth of March, in daylight, on the fish dock, in about two hours, and nobody lays a hand on a man. **A working boat is confiscated by a form.** The officer of the *Vigil* comes off the tender at half past nine and asks for the master by name, and the form is read twice, and Nessa Pike makes him read the endorsement box, and Tarner writes four true facts into it and then a fifth in his own words, and makes the office's type carry a master's assertion with the master's name against it. **The four facts are that the vessel is a working salvage boat of no crew of any association, that she carries no transparent material and none is alleged, that her master is a member of the Free Keel association and has been off its slate since November, and that no person of this yard has been served or named — and the twenty-ninth of October is Sella Brigg's cover and not Tarner's name, which is what Chapter 62 has him say in his own mouth.** **The ground of the holding is *entries concerning a contested material entered in the fish market of Morrow Quay*, and it does not name the entry, and the office is not asked whether the record is the vessel's business and the reeve is sent for and comes back with one sentence: this office has no power over an instrument of a vessel of the Crown Navy and will not pretend to any.** The pump — the only working pump in this harbor not the harbor authority's — goes into a lighter first, then the leads, the warps, the gear, the tool box, and then the papers; **and Tarner reads his own logbook out on the boards in a market first, in two columns, and says out loud where he got the two columns from and that it is not a naval form and that the yard put it on a wall for nothing, and that sentence goes on the endorsement.** The hour is twenty-four minutes past ten and is written down by a boy on the dock before anything else. Cost: a boat, a pump, two warps, four men who came to hire a crew and two of whom had money, and a crew that may go ashore twice a day, which is neither a punishment nor a mercy. Tarner to Calder Marr: *if you had kept it in a drawer I would be afloat tonight and I would hate you, and I would be afloat.* Thread opened: a working pump in a lighter, and the cost of it dated ten days before a man went missing.

### Chapter 73 — The Counter at the Morrow Circuit Office

Monday the sixth to Tuesday the seventh of March, on the coast road and at a counter in the west of the city. **The ground is entered for three marks and the office re-asks on the same morning.** The clerk at the grille reads all four paragraphs before he answers any part of it, and says he has entered that crew's day on this file before now and does not remember doing it, and that a counter is not a room. **The third seal is the same nineteen words with the first clause changed: *That a ground having been received, the said request stands unanswered in this office, and this office awaits the same*,** dated the sixth of March, the fourteen running from the date at the foot, so the twentieth — a Monday. The clerk says the line has been in the office's type since before the year turned, and that a clerk copies a file when the office decides to, and the copy is what gets quoted, and the copy will have three seals on it set out in the order they were affixed. **The bag goes to six, and the two days cost a job: Sella Brigg comes down on the Tuesday at eight with twenty-two marks and two men of her own and there is nobody and no boat, and she says the cause out loud, which is the truth and not the boy, and goes up the hill.** The boy writes the loss under the Monday and not the Tuesday because the day we lost is Monday. Cost: three marks, two days, one refused day, a third seal.

### Chapter 74 — The Second Seam on a Moon

Wednesday the eighth and Friday the tenth of March. **The pump is fitted.** Nineteen marks of iron go onto the note and five out of the bag, and the bag goes to one, and Nessa Pike makes the boy write all four figures in one line. **The fifth week of a wage at four marks a week fell due on Tuesday the seventh and is entered unpaid in a column with no adjectives and is not discussed.** On the tenth he asks for the last sentence of the reason to be said again, and it is the sixth time since the fourteenth of October; **the condition itself is not named in this chapter and is not named anywhere in this batch.** Sella Brigg hires the pump and a day at the second seam for twenty gross — the eighth three, the share three, air two, rope one, a diver out of the *Grey Widow* at two, the pump at four, the yard's five — on her own account on ground the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first does not close, with the reason in the yard's own book and not in any office's, because no permit was needed and no instrument of that office was used. **The diver sets his own limit of thirty minutes at the boat, the tender is Nessa Pike, the cutoff is hers, and the entry goes in two columns and is read back by four and by Sella Brigg as the fifth, who gives a third thing nobody asked for: put in that she read it before she paid, and do not put in that she was pleased.** And the boy's last line is not money and is not read back, and it puts the fourth of March and the housing that came up on the Wednesday in the same book and says the yard knows which one made the four. Thread: the pump closes as a supply problem and opens as the yard's only hireable gear.

### Chapter 75 — What the Yard Will Not Strike Out

Sunday the twelfth of March, in the shed, and then on the dock at one in the afternoon. **Noll Tarner asks the yard to strike the twenty-sixth of January out of its book and off its wall, for nothing, in his own words, and both men have a right.** He gives the cause first and asks for it to be entered: he said a sentence out loud in a market on purpose on the fourth of March and it has become a paper. **Nessa Pike refuses for the yard and tells the story of the hole in the reason of the nineteenth of January, because a scratched-out line is a hole you have invited a man into, and an instrument of the Crown Navy put a boat into a lighter on the strength of a sheet of paper in a market this month.** Calder Marr refuses in a different way — your day is true, your nineteen is in the first column and your twenty-one in the second and neither is scratched out, and if we strike yours we strike ours, because a book with a hole a party can fill by asking tells a man in four years exactly which entries this yard gives up when it is asked nicely. **Tarner's counter-offer is the yard's leaf, not his own logbook, because his own names him and not Crale and not Marrow, and a boy of fifteen who cannot read is a boy who was in a market and cannot be proved to have been in a boat.** The boy copies it out and four men read it back, one of whom is a rival master and not on the yard's wage column. Then Calder Marr walks up the dock to say that a fortnight ago the best answer he had to *who is the record for* was *a man with a book can check it*, and that there is no clause anywhere in five months of his own arguing that says *by us and not by them*, and Tarner will not sign a thing of his to be polite. **The turn of the movement begins here:** a record is now in the keeping of a man whose boat has been taken, and none of the four other keepers came to the yard for it.

### Chapter 76 — The Officer's Figure

Wednesday the fifteenth of March, in the hearing room, and the slate wall is blank on purpose because Kest chose it. **A naval officer uses the yard's own public record, correctly, in a room the yard cannot walk out of.** He produces a slate of the *Vigil's* own count of **ten**, dated the eighteenth of December, and the clerk enters it as the vessel's figure, and the yard's two dates go in beside it, and neither is scratched out and the difference of one is entered as a difference and not as an error. The officer asks the harbor authority to note that a document produced for a contested material, amended in public, with a fresh count published while the first is still standing, is not a record that is single, and to say so to the circuit office. **Kest answers: yes to the note, no to the compulsion, and the second half as the vessel's opinion and not a finding of this office.** The third request asks that the harbor authority note that the party permitted a crew not of its association to have its day entered, that the party has declined to strike it out on that crew's asking, and that the master has been given a copy of the leaf; Calder Marr asks for the fourth fact in a different form — the crew *asked* — and the clerk enters it as he says it. **The fourth request is whether a request for a paper is an inspection under the charter, and Kest says no, and says the word *suspend* out loud nine times in a room where a naval officer is writing, and says he did it because this office does not have secrets about its own instruments, and it is the price of saying a thing where four people can hear it.** The fifth request is for a conservator's opinion and **Mara Venn refuses before the question is finished, in her own words, unsummarized, and asks to be named on the page rather than named at it.** Elias Voke defends the yard in the cost of his own document: he put the book's figures and a boy's name in it on purpose and would again, and if a document is not good enough because a yard's figures change, then every skipper on this coast will learn that the way to be safe is to write nothing. **The officer then says out loud, and Kest enters as heard, that he does not know whether there is a second season either.** The clerk says his page has needed two sides for the first time and that he is keeping it whether anybody tells him to or not. The boy is not in the room, is not a party, and is not sent for.

### Chapter 77 — The Eighth Thing on the Wall

Saturday the eighteenth of March, on the fish dock and in the market. **A fourth request of the circuit office, dated the sixteenth, and the count in it is eleven** — corrected in the office's own type in three days off a paper of Kest's, and nobody in the yard told them anything, and the naval slate still says ten. **The yard decides at about nine that morning, in nine minutes, not to answer it, because a repeated ground is a finished file and a delay is free, and because it cannot pay the three-mark counter fee: the bag has two, and three marks is four days of the air.** The eighth thing goes up on the market wall in Calder Marr's own hand, naming all eleven copies and where each of them is, and says in four lines that the party has not answered and has not refused, will not deliver the copies in its own keeping, will not ask any holder of a copy to deliver it, names the four holders so that any man entitled to ask may do so without coming to the yard, **and will deliver to the circuit office the entry of any single day, on the written asking of the crew whose day it is, unshortened, with its reason and its two columns entire — a refusal dressed as an offer, and the yard says so on the page.** **Amos Pryde says no in the street and says it is the best thing that has been said about him in nineteen years, and will give it to a magistrate on a written asking and not to a party and not to an office that wants it single, and pays a shilling for a copy because a thing a man gets for nothing is a thing a man is given.** A man of the *Low Lantern* keeps his wall up and gives the best argument anybody has made for a sheet of paper in five months. A herring skipper of the third lay says out loud that what will be read is not the yard's figures but the shape of them, and Calder Marr says the sheet is up and is not coming down. The boy of the *Sea Wren* comes up the hill and says there is a man asking about the conservator.

### Chapter 78 — The Third Lay

Tuesday the twenty-first of March. **Harl Dace hires the pump and a half day for a shackle of his own at the first seam, fourteen gross, and says out loud that he is hiring on the strength of a sheet of paper the yard put on a wall — the answer to his own January question in a net loft, arrived at by a customer and not by the yard — and asks that the figure be read back before he pays, and that it be entered that he has had two men drowned on ground somebody else called his own and the second one was on a day nobody wrote down.** The shackle comes up with a foot of the mole's own rubble in its eye. The pump, the wall's figure and the half day are in the first column and are all checkable. Then **Piet Marrow says a man of the third lay with nine winters of his own went out in the blow and has not come back, and that the *Sea Wren* was the boat that would have gone. Nessa Pike says no to a boy of fifteen and says it is her and not the law. Calder Marr says no to himself, and says he checked the notice before Piet was up the hill and asks that the checking be entered with the date and no reason, because a man who says no and does not look is hiding behind a clause, and gives three ordinary reasons: sixteen days of a charter, four weeks of an unpaid wage, and a boat already in a lighter this month over an entry.** What the harbor actually did is on a notice nailed to the tide-gang store door on the night of Saturday the eighteenth: nine fleet boats out, two of them out on the eighteenth, nineteenth and twentieth, four third-lay boats doubled, a light on the mole head at dusk on the eighteenth taken in on Monday the twentieth, because the harbor authority's repair account bought four sets of moorings and a season of warp in January and there was no money in it for oil, and the third lay dark from the night of Sunday the nineteenth. **The boy enters that there is not one line about Neave Tarrant in the yard's book, having looked twice, because a year is long enough for a man to convince himself that a book has a hole in it that somebody filled.** And his last line puts the fourth of March and the twenty-eighth of February in the same entry. Thread: Tarrant opened, untouched, not resolved.

### Chapter 79 — A Trade Is on a Shelf

Wednesday the twenty-second of March, in the shed, and **it is the fourth time in five months that Calder Marr has come within four inches of asking Mara Venn what is in the anchor and has not, and the first time two other people in the room have been able to see the four inches.** She came at seven and had been there ninety minutes. She discloses that a man with a slate, not naval and not from a court and not from the office that has her file, worked through a fish market on **Saturday the eighteenth of March** asking four men the same four questions, and that she told him the fourth of January because it is on a wall, and said no to the other three, and that **he wrote down that she said no, which she says is a different and a better thing for him than a refusal, and that she has not told anybody for four days and that four days is the whole of what she has to say against herself.** She refuses Calder Marr's request for a figure and says **the price of the fourth of January is that the one thing the circuit office asked for cannot be done by anybody, because the person who could have done it was working in that office on the day she signed it.** She asks to be named on the page rather than named at it. And Calder Marr enters, in his own hand, that he has not asked her anything else, that she saw him not ask, and that in about two years there will be a man in a room who says a woman with no standing knew what was in that anchor, and that he will be right, and that there is now one page anywhere in this harbor that says she was never asked. She prices herself at six marks a day, lives on hulls she looks at for nothing, does not ask for the forty marks in a month in which a form cost this harbor a pump, and says the arrangement is not a friendship. Cost: nothing, and everything.

### Chapter 80 — The Last Day of the Charter

Friday the thirty-first of March. A herring skipper comes up the dock at half past seven with twenty marks for six hours of flood at the second seam, and **Calder Marr refuses because the instrument runs to midnight and does not go into April, and tells him the plain reason, and then tells him the answer to *who do I ask* is that he does not know, and that it is the sentence, and that he is not going to hand a man a bigger one on the last day.** Kest comes up the hill on foot, the first time in four months he has been inside the shed, and enters a statement in his own hand that the charter ends at midnight today, that this office cannot renew it and holds nothing that renews it, and that a second season is a date this office neither controls nor has been told of — and that he burnt the version that said *I regret*. His second thing is the cost: **the man who sent the yard's own counts up to the circuit office is him, it cost the harbor authority something, and he sent them anyway rather than be the only fool in a file in Veyr**, and he says he sent them now and not in January because in January he did not know what a record was going to be used for, and that the yard can stop watching him for a favor because he has not got one. **The chandler comes down off the hill to the yard, unasked and unsent for, with his page and a paper twist of candles, offers a fortnight in his own hand for nothing, says the yard not coming is worth more than the fortnight, and the three counts of the weeks are said out loud at the head of the dock for the first time: the creditor's twenty-two, the yard's thirteen, and the last figure ever written in the page says eleven — and Calder Marr refuses in his own mouth, in the open, and gives the reason.** The ledger is read out to the mark: **five marks in a bag, twenty-seven on the note, two crowns in a tin; forty marks to the boy and forty to Mara Venn and eight to the bellwright and eight shillings and tenpence to Sella Brigg, all standing; sixteen marks of unpaid March wage in a column with no adjectives in it.** The going out's eight marks are still owing and cannot be found before October. The bell answers the harbor's tide bell a hand's width of a second late in the road and the boy does not put a pencil on the page. **The last day of a one-season provisional working charter in Morrow Quay was Friday the thirty-first of March and it ended at midnight the way a date ends.**

## Volume 02, Batch 0004 (Chapters 81–90) — complete. Movement 4, the forged seal. **THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT.**

**Actual word count: 25,683 words across ten chapters** (Ch81 2,647 · Ch82 2,569 · Ch83 2,673 · Ch84 2,456 · Ch85 2,258 · Ch86 2,702 · Ch87 2,579 · Ch88 2,574 · Ch89 2,605 · Ch90 2,620), measured with `wc -w` including headings, re-measured after the Batch 0004 review repair, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor and every chapter is inside the guideline** — the first batch of Volume 02 to come in that way, and eleven to fourteen per cent under the ceiling on the two longest. **No chapter is two rooms.** The hearing room is not used at all in this batch; its slate wall is wiped twice and is named both times, and the reeve's own page is carried forward. The batch's pressures alternate: poverty and a refusal, paid work, a discovery, an office, a refusal asked once, material proof, an argument, action on the water, publication, and a ledger. **A reviewer pass over Chapters 81–90 returned FIX; the reviewer's own log is not kept in this repository and every finding is disposed of in `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0004 review repairs*. Five required repairs and ten prose, precision and file-reference repairs were made and no chapter was restarted, no scene was cut, no cost was dropped and no planned plot was altered. The two that change what a later batch inherits are the panel's dating, which is withdrawn so that no later chapter may date the work or restore the thirty-year reading, and the fifth of April, which had a person in the water and now says so on the page.**

### Chapter 81 — A Job Without a Paper

Tuesday the fourth of April, in the shed. **The ninth week of a wage at four marks a week falls due and goes into the column with no adjectives in it, and the column is five lines deep, because the column holds the unpaid weeks and not the weeks served.** Two men come up the hill in one morning and get two different answers, and the chapter's whole subject is the difference: **a job on the second seam, on a fisherman's own account and landward of the third seam, is lawful without a paper and is taken, and a job on the mole's toe at the third seam is inside the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first and on the authority's own ground and is refused, and the yard says so in two sentences, the second of which is about Calder Marr's own decision on the afternoon of Friday the thirty-first rather than about the law.** Iven Toft is quoted the same refusal he was quoted on the last day of the charter and says he will take a yard that refuses him for a reason it does not like. The second man argues the yard should do it as private work on his own account and is refused again, and **says out loud, in a market, in the first column, that he thinks this yard only works when a man cannot go to the office.** Money: nothing moves; the bag is five, the note is twenty-seven, the tin two crowns. Alden is at the far bench, not asked for anything, and Calder does not go. At four o'clock **Harl Dace comes up the hill with a slate and asks to put a coast in the yard's book**, is told the second column exists for what a man on a mole cannot check, and asks for a reason to be written against himself. Thread opened: the coast road.

### Chapter 82 — Six Hours of Flood

Wednesday the fifth of April, on the second seam's face, in the water. **The one paid day the batch has, and the reason it is the only kind of day available.** Iven Toft tries to argue the harbor's eighth and the share out of the yard on the ground that it has no charter, and **Nessa Pike refuses in a trade argument rather than a legal one — the mole, the tide gang and the wall are the harbor's and a yard with no charter is not a yard with no customs** — and the reason goes into the yard's own book in the first column with the man who asked named against it, because any man on the dock can check it. The reason is in the yard's own book and not in any office's, and its third line is read aloud twice on its own and says out loud that **there is a person in the water and he is not of this party, that the limit is his and was fixed by him at the boat before anybody was wet, that the tender is named and is not the diver, and that the cutoff is hers to pull without asking anybody — and the yard keeps the practice in its own book and will not have the two confused.** The first column reads **no person of this party in the water at any time, the diver being of another boat**, and Chapter 88's refusal to invent a cutoff for a man on a lead is a refusal that only means something because this day had a real one. Tam Slee, hired out of the *Grey Widow*, twenty-six minutes of a limit he set himself, a shackle and nine feet of warp, four read-backs and Harl Dace as the fifth. The two-column convention is used again, and **Nessa Pike refuses to make a cutoff out of a man on a lead and has it written in the second column that the slate of limits was carried for nothing.** Money: twenty gross, thirteen out, the yard's seven; **the bag is five in the morning and twelve at night.**

### Chapter 83 — A Line of Marks and a Bottom

Thursday the sixth of April, at a table in the fish market, in daylight. **Harl Dace, master of the *Grey Widow*, sets out a coast road in his own order and not the reader's.** Three marks — a cairn of ballast stones on a point the men call the Old Custom House, nine fathoms of a ship's stem on a sandspit called the Nine Foot and called the Accountant, and a rock that dries at low water springs called the Kettle — a bar of old gravel that comes up to two and a half fathoms, and the Rooks, a creek mouth that is the only shelter on five miles of that coast in a north-easter, to be entered on the flood and never on the ebb. **The harbor authority's own sounding sheet has no line inshore of the headland for five miles north, because there is nothing there to moor on and nobody to moor, so the check can only be a check of a man.** Dace has been right for thirty-one years and has never been asked, and he wants the figures in the yard's book in two columns whether they hold him up or hold him down, and the boy writes the reason against the second column in Dace's own words. **The object arrives:** a listening panel of worked glass, made in a bellwright's shop on the quay at Ashtide out of glass recovered, by a shop that says the word *willingly* the way a man says a word when he means it, bought out of a shop at Ashtide in the autumn by Dace, with Corrie Ash of the *Anna Beth*, a boat hand of working age laid up in the winter with his chest, as the witness who put a hand on it and looked out of the shop's front window at the water. **A Registry claim seal is struck on its face and the custodian on it is a boat**, and Dace has been three times to a shop and twice to an office to find out what his own boat is allowed to do with it. **The yard will not have it in a shed, and does not argue the notice — it argues that anything which stays in a shed is in the yard's keeping, and a yard with no paper has no business keeping a contested material.** Money: nothing moves; the bag is twelve, the note is twenty-seven.

### Chapter 84 — The Fifth Entry
