Friday the seventh of April, in the reeve's barge on the west basin, and **lock L24's acknowledgement is entered for the fifth time, and for the first time at the asking of a party.** The claim window is for **one panel of worked glass of undetermined provenance, brought into the harbor by a master of no crew of this association, held by him and not by the party, claimed by nobody, entered in no custody room, and expressly not one of the three objects entered on the fourteenth of October.** Kest asks four questions and takes four answers, and rules that **there is no bond, because a bond is pledge of the party's own and the party's own is nothing** — and says out loud that it is a nicety and that it is the whole of the yard's right to stand on that mole that month and that neither of them earned it. Dace stands on the mole steps because a man who is not a party is not put in a barge, and Kest enters the one sentence of the morning that he did not write, and enters whose mouth it came out of. **Kest then treats the whole of the mole from the third to the eleventh seams as inside the first clause for the purposes of the instrument, whether the crown is or is not, and says that a conservative reading of a notice is not a narrow one** — and enters, in his own hand and at length, that this office has no power to lift the notice, is not lifting it, and is putting a party, an object and a clerk inside a closure it did not create, and that a man in Veyr will find five of these in one ledger and that the fifth is the only one a party asked for. **His warning is the batch's political pressure: the circuit office will want the glass, a party with nothing in its hand cannot refuse in a way that reads as a refusal, and the yard's protection is never having had it.** Money: air two out of twelve, so the bag is ten.

### Chapter 85 — The One Question

Sunday the ninth of April, one front room on Coombe Row, and **Movement 4's one private scene.** Calder Marr goes alone and is told so by Nessa Pike first, in about forty words. He asks Sera Mott one question — whether there has ever been a man who went out in a boat to keep one of them and came back and told her what it was like — and is refused, flatly, and is told that the refusal is not concealment, and that an archivist and a man from a coast office have both been refused and believed. **She holds to the December sentence that she would say no one more time in about two years.** He asks for the page: the entry says she was asked and refused, and **her name is not written on it at her asking, and the boy does not write it and is not asked to and does not ask** — the first line in the yard's book with a hole in it that is not a hole, and Dace, reading as a man outside the crew, says so. She then gives the one practical thing she will give, and gives it for nothing: **two men who have been out on that coast inside ten years — one of fifty-eight in the south lay who will call it a tale in a market in front of people, and one of thirty-one in the second lay with a bad wheel who will say nothing at all until the water is sounded and will then say so out loud in front of anybody.** And she tells him why he must not put her half and his coast in one book: **one is a road of water and one is about men, and a page that holds both can be read either way, and it would be the first false thing anybody ever said about either of them.** Money: nothing moves; the bag is ten, the note is twenty-seven.

### Chapter 86 — Nine Joins in a Ring

Monday the tenth of April, on the mole's crown between the sixth and ninth seams, in daylight, in a wind, above the face and not the toe. **The claim window of the seventh is worked, the fifth acknowledgement is read out loud on the stones so a man on the steps can hear it, and the slate wall was wiped at eight that morning.** Mara Venn states four conditions before she touches it — no diver, no water, no air, no ship; **she will not describe what is in the panel and has not been in it; what she says will be about the glass and the mark, in that order; and she has no office, so it goes in a yard's book and nowhere else** — and the clerk enters all four. **The object is a listening panel, made to be hung in a boat's rigging and listened to from and not entered. Its age is not established and she refuses on the page to give a figure for it, and gives the reason in her own mouth: a date is a thing you take out of a shop's book and not out of an edge, and she has not seen the book. What she offers about the making is that the edge is dressed the way a working hand dresses an edge and not the way a man in a hurry dresses one, and that this is a statement about care and not about time. She refuses to name a shop and a man and says so.** The seal reads: *Custodian, the sloop Grey Widow of Morrow Quay. Provenance, one panel worked at the quay of Ashtide. Limit, route use.* **The proof is material and institutional and it is two findings.** Material: **the ring has nine joins in it, and a Registry striking is cut once into steel and its ring is one continuous curve, so this mark was cut in something else and struck into the glass by hand** — and there is **a ridge of lifted glass standing on the outside of every one of the nine joins and every one of them running the same way round the ring, which is a hand and a hammer and not a machine, where a Registry striking is pressed down and not hit and has no ridge anywhere.** What the material half cannot do, in her own words, is give a date or a man: it is a man's hand and a man's hammer and a man working faster than that office would have wanted the work done, and it is the whole of what a thing like that will ever give anybody. Institutional: **a custodian must be a named person or a named assembly, because either of those can be told yes or told no and can be found again next year; the custodian on this seal is a boat, and a boat cannot be asked anything, cannot consent, cannot refuse and cannot be found.** So the seal is false in the one clause that would let anybody use it and right in every other particular a man would check, including the spelling of the office's name and the order of its clauses. **The hinge sentence comes before anybody asks it, unprompted and unsoftened: she can say the seal was not struck by that office, and she cannot say who held the tool, who made the glass, or why a man wrote his own boat down as the thing that owns a record of the sea — and she says *I am not being careful, I am telling you the exact size of what my trade can do*.** **She refuses the asking for the third time and the principle is identical and the detail is new: twice he wanted a number for a wall, and this time he wants a number that would let a man in a city write *a conservator of Morrow Quay certifies*, and she will not be certified by a party.** The clerk says the office has no box for it; Nessa Pike refuses to witness it because a party does not witness; the object goes back into Dace's own hands. Money: nothing moves; the bag is ten, the note is twenty-seven.

### Chapter 87 — Instrument or Weapon

Tuesday the eleventh of April, in the shed, and **the tenth week of a wage falls due and three marks of bread, oil and candles go onto the note in the creditor's own hand and come out of the bag the same afternoon**, so the bag is five and the note is thirty, and for the first time in the winter the boy's own arithmetic and the creditor's page do the same thing on the same day. **Dace comes to ask for his glass back and Calder Marr says no.** The argument is the longest that shed has had since the twelfth of March and it turns on a sentence Nessa Pike makes Calder Marr say in his own mouth: that he wants to turn a man's question into a yard's asset while the man who asked it is in the room. Kest's Friday warning is that a claim to a real object is worth having and that a party with nothing in its hand cannot refuse without the refusal looking like a refusal. **The turn is given up on purpose: the glass goes back to Dace on the steps, in the market, in front of the market, and the yard keeps no instrument, no claim and no paper of any kind.** Nessa Pike argues it on the yard's own ground — *the road is still there whether you claim it or not, and they can take a boat's gear off a man, as they did on the fourth of March, and they cannot take a bar of gravel off a man* — and Mara Venn gives the reason in one go without stopping anywhere, which she had not done in that shed in five months: **a boat cannot be asked anything, and a seal that is false in one clause and held by a party becomes false in two clauses the day it is held, and the second lie is the one that goes in the file.** Dace sets the terms of the check and the terms of the wall: the account in Calder's hand and not the conservator's, because if it is in a conservator's hand a man in a city will ask her about it and she has no office to be answerable to. The boy, unasked, says the thing nobody asked for: **a wall with a nail in it is not a seal, and a man who wants records single will not be able to tell which of the yard's pages is worth taking.**

### Chapter 88 — Two Marks and a Bar

Thursday the thirteenth of April, out past the headland in the *Grey Widow*, and **the batch's water.** The whole of the coast is inside the harbor line and inshore of the inner shoals and nobody in the boat is near the four-hundred-yard bar off the outer marks. **No permit and no instrument, because no office has anything to do with a bar of gravel, and Nessa Pike refuses to invent a cutoff for a man swinging a lead and has it written in the second column that the slate of limits was carried for nothing.** The cairn is where Dace said, eleven feet by pole at half-tide, the top three courses black; the stem called the Accountant is where he said, nine fathoms, four casts; the bar comes up **two and three-quarters at twenty past two on the flood off a spring**, which is the figure Dace gave as his own before the boat came out, and two and a quarter on the ebb, which is the shoulder. **The lead fouls at four fathoms and a hand's width and comes up a knuckle of kelp, a shoe and four feet of herring warp gone hard — somebody's loss, and not the road — and the boy puts it in the second column and a man in a boat says what it means.** **The third mark is not checked, because the Kettle dries three days out of a month and this was not one of them, and the date for going back is left to the tide and not to the yard.** Dace forbids the word *survey* out loud and says a man who chooses his own tide comes home with whatever he went out for. **The condition is named once, in this volume, for the sixth time since the fourteenth of October: the ringing in both ears, the second sound under the first arriving late, and the inability to tell reliably which sound is in the boat** — Calder gives it to Dace on Dace's own deck and makes him put it in his own book and get it back out, and Dace answers as a working man, that he has a man of fifty-one with it who is better on a lead and is not put on a lead, and Nessa Pike says the sentence to the wheel and not to him. Two columns, four read-backs, and Dace's entry that a Tuesday's work is not a survey. Money: two marks of victuals out of five, so the bag is three, the note is thirty.

### Chapter 89 — The Ninth Thing on the Wall

Saturday the fifteenth of April, at the foot of the market wall, in daylight, forty people, and **the ninth thing goes up in Calder Marr's own hand with a nail through the corner.** It carries the three marks and the bar in Dace's words, the check's figures and the hour and the tide, what was not checked and why, both columns with a reason against every line of the second, the conservator's finding in her words and her own hand **with the word *unsigned* under it and the reason why — no office in this harbor has entered it and the reason is the fourth of January** — and four lines that begin *the party makes no claim*, *the object is in a master's keeping and never was in the party's*, *this sheet is not a seal, is not a claim, is not a survey, binds nobody, consents for nobody, and may be copied by any man who can read*, and *the count of the copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October is eleven and is eleven still and no holder has been asked for any of them and will not be*. **Oswin Trask of the south lay says in the market that the whole of it is a tale, and asks for the whole of it to be entered, and is entered, and is entered again as not having been proved wrong either, and Calder Marr does not argue with him in the street.** **Coll Ferrow of the second lay, thirty-one, with a bad wheel, compares two figures on the ninth thing against his own slate in the market and says his own are the same, out loud, in front of anybody**, and is entered. Noll Tarner reads the whole sheet and says the first thing anybody has put on that wall that says what it is not for, and answers his own month-old question: **a record of a dock is not for the people who keep the ground, it is for the people who come after you.** Amos Pryde says four men in that market would each have paid a mark for the line about not being asked, **and Calder Marr says in the market that the second copy is not going to his stall and gives the reason, which is that he has been named on a wall since September and would want to have been asked, and that asking him is asking him, and names a shilling and the reason there is one, which is that a thing that is free is a thing that is owed.** The copy goes to a coaster's master at the foot of the dock on the Monday evening for a shilling. **Mara Venn asks for her answer to a shop at Ashtide to be read out at the foot of her own wall**: offered four marks in writing for an opinion, and wanted a document; there is no document and there is not going to be one; **the shop wrote back on the Friday that an opinion given in a market is not a thing it can pay for, and that is the first time in five months anybody has declined to pay her for something she actually did** — entered in her own column, and she says the forty marks are not one mark smaller for it. Two copies, because there is no money to print any and two is not eleven: one on the wall and one to a man who has not been asked, at a shilling he will pay.

### Chapter 90 — Two Facts in One Book

Monday the seventeenth of April, in the shed. The slate wall was wiped at eight and the clerk says his own page wants a side and a half and that the ledger is the record and the wall is a convenience. **The whole of the last fourteen days goes into the yard's book in two columns and is the longest entry in it: the first column is everything a man in this harbor or any man who can sail can check, and the second column is everything nobody can, and under them four lines read by four men.** The fourth is the volume's own argument: **that the seal on the object is false and the finding is a conservator's and is unsigned, and that the finding is about a seal and the account is about the sea, and that neither of the two is a reason to believe the other, and that they are in the same book in the same week because both are true and neither helps anybody** — and the third is that **the object is in a master's keeping and never was in the party's, and that this yard holds no instrument, no claim and no paper of any kind at the hour of writing, entered as a fact and not an opinion.** The boy says he has counted nine read-backs on the page and has been told to count none of them. **The money: three marks in a bag, thirty on the note, two crowns in a tin; twenty marks in the last fourteen days, every mark of it on the fifth, out of one day that paid; twenty-four marks of wage unpaid in a column with no adjectives, the first four weeks paid and the fifth through the tenth unpaid, the eleventh falling on Tuesday the eighteenth.** All the standing debts are read back whole — forty to the boy, forty to the conservator who was refused four marks on the twelfth of April and whose forty is not one mark smaller, eight marks and a day of a boat to the harbor's bellwright with nothing in this harbor before October, eight shillings and tenpence to Sella Brigg. **The dated positions go in as a set with no adjectives**: the charter gone since the thirty-first of March; the *Sea Wren* at the pile with her logbook in a naval file and a copy of the yard's own leaf in his coat; the fourth request unanswered since the sixteenth and eleven copies in the office's own type and ten on the *Vigil's* slate and both standing; the notice whole, and the harbor authority now inside its own closure five times and the fifth at the yard's asking; the north berth out, the ninth seam a mooring, fifty fathoms of warp where ninety were, forty feet of the authority's marline in the sea, three tide-gang men off, the mole-head light not on and not to be, the third lay full, the hard at Ashtide unanswered since the ninth of November, the *Kittiwake* on the hard with no cradle. **Neave Tarrant is one line and is not opened.** The false line on the unverified slate is still false; the boy has not asked; the conservator has not been asked and did not ask. The bell has not been looked after since the twenty-seventh of February, the rope is a year old in January, and nobody in that yard has said out loud what happens in June. **The pull: a coasting master asks at half past five whether this harbor has a room for a man off a boat, and the honest answer is that the yard that owes a boy four marks a week and a bellwright eight will not put him in it, and he goes to the tide-gang store, and the ninth thing is still on the wall in the dark and its second copy goes into a coaster's master's hands at the foot of the dock for a shilling he paid and would not be given.**

---

## Volume 02, Batch 0005 (Chapters 91–100) — complete. Movement 5, the rescue and the writ. **VOLUME 02 IS CLOSED.**

**Actual word count: 33,327 words across ten chapters** (Ch91 2,791 · Ch92 2,529 · Ch93 2,972 · Ch94 3,211 · Ch95 3,931 · Ch96 3,435 · Ch97 3,978 · Ch98 3,524 · Ch99 3,136 · Ch100 3,820), measured with `wc -w` including headings after the review repair, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Four are inside the guideline and six are past it**, the largest at Chapter 97 at about twenty-four per cent over and Chapter 95 at about twenty-three, and the four others past are Chapters 100, 98, 96 and 94. **Every chapter past the guideline is one complete room or one complete day, and three of them carry more than one room on that day — Chapter 92 is the shed and the market, Chapter 98 is the net loft, the mole's steps and the shed, and Chapter 100 is the boards and the dock — which is recorded here as a shape fact instead of being denied.** The hearing room is used twice, in Chapter 94 on Thursday the twenty-seventh for the office's own ledger and in Chapter 97 on Monday the first of May for the one sitting, and the market wall is used once, in Chapter 100 on Wednesday the third of May, and no two of the three are the same day. The batch's pressures alternate: a fire and a warping, a hand and a market, weather, an office and a paper, the water and a rescue, money and four causes, a sitting, a bell, a going out and six hands, and a wall. **The batch is a closure batch: it pays the bellwright's eight marks, sixteen marks of a boy's wage out of other men's pockets, a Kest payment that costs a man his warp and a berth, and says out loud that the chandler's note is not re-written, and it ends on the volume's own image.**

### Chapter 91 — A Wharfinger and a Handbell

Tuesday the eighteenth and Monday the twenty-fourth of April, in the shed, on the hill, at the head of the dock, and in a road. The eleventh week of a wage falls due on the Tuesday and goes into the column, and the column is **seven lines deep at twenty-eight marks with the fifth of March the oldest of the seven**, and it goes to eight lines and thirty-two on the twenty-fifth. **On the Monday a wharfinger of the second lay comes in off the water and asks this yard for a pump and a man, and the yard has nothing, and says so in nine sentences.** The chapter's crisis is a fire in the coal store at the second lay, and the two irreversible things it does are these: **Neale's fire party cannot get its hose cart round the third lay's boat, which is lying against that wharf with forty of her men in it, and takes about four hundred feet of herring warp off the second lay's wharf to do it; and the hose, which is the yard's own and was re-made twice since November, goes in six minutes and the fire party has no pressure at all in the second lay for the rest of the season.** Edric Pringle names the cause herself, in her own mouth, at the head of the dock, and asks for it in the yard's book and not in the market, and will not be thanked. **The fire party asked the boy for the third lay's strength and not the yard, and the boy gave a number and not a name, and the number is nineteen, and one of the two men missing from it has been out since the twenty-eighth of February and has not been found, and the yard has not gone and has asked nobody anything.** Nessa Pike refuses to put one of the yard's men in that fire, out loud, in the yard's own voice. The far bench moves under the drip at nine on the Monday and the man of sixty-one moves it himself and nobody speaks to him. **The nine hours in one room are the twenty-sixth, not the twenty-fourth.** Money: nothing moves; the bag is three, the note is thirty.

### Chapter 92 — Eight Marks in the Market

Tuesday the twenty-fifth of April, in the shed and in the market. The twelfth week falls due and the column is **eight lines deep, the deepest it has been**, at thirty-two marks. **Nessa Pike says Calder Marr's own reason about the chandler back to him in nine sentences and then refuses to take it, and tells him she is going to ask for a line of credit, and the chapter is about the difference between a fortnight and a line of credit: a yard that goes to its creditor with no date in its hand has admitted it has nothing, and a man who is given bread in June is not a man who has been given nothing.** The middle of the day is the boy and a needle at the bench, mending the third pair of trousers six times, and the list of what the yard would buy if it had money, which is eleven feet of hose, a hand lead, a stove, a lamp, line, a strop, a needle, thread, wax, and two pairs of trousers, **and the boy's wage is not on the list and he will not put it on it.** **The market is a Tuesday market and a Tuesday market is not a Saturday market.** The pump's missed hire on the twenty-second is entered against the yard and nobody is to be hurt by it. Then a stallholder's boy wheels a handcart up the boards with a slate nailed to it, and **eight shillings is taken for the second lay's hose in about four seconds out of one tray, and Edric Pringle puts four marks in on the top of it and will not be thanked and asks that a market not be told a word about her**, and the number she insists on is eight shillings out of that tray and her own four marks on the top of it, and the second column says that no man in this harbor can check what eight shillings and four marks is worth. Four read-backs and no fifth man on the boards. **Nessa Pike's run to the chandler's shop on the Thursday of the twenty-second is a line of credit she did not ask for and half a pound of candle ends she did not offer for.** Money: the bag is eight, the note is thirty-three.

### Chapter 93 — The Green Water

Wednesday the twenty-sixth of April, in a shed, in nineteen hours of north-north-east. **A glass storm, and it is written in the yard's book in the second column with a reason against it, and nobody may name a cause for it.** The boy goes out at four in the morning and comes back with one sentence: there is a green sea inside the harbor and the second lay's store roof is being salted. **Three things go wrong in the weather and all three are in the second column and none of them is explained:** a boy of twenty-two on a roof hears his own voice about a yard behind him in a yard where nobody has said anything, at about half past two in the afternoon, in the worst of it; a man of fifty-one with a bad wheel of his own is told twice to come his wheel round, out loud, flat, while he has it in both hands; and **Calder Marr cannot place a sound, asks for a sentence twice, is given it twice, and cannot say which of the two he got — and the boy enters that in the second column with a reason, and nobody in the crew asks him about it, and it is not named.** The first column is the ordinary labor of a gale: the mole steps awash twice, the spray over the coal store at four in the afternoon, the pole against the ninth seam's mooring not moved, the fouling on the ground line untouched for the fourth time, forty men of the third lay standing in six inches of water with buckets, **the harbor's own hose laid out in eleven wet lengths on the second lay's boards and unusable for the rest of the season**, the *Anna Beth* on the slip with her pump stopped, and a hand of no crew of this association pumping her for nothing and refusing a shilling. A wharfinger stands in the doorway for six hours because he has a man who cannot stand up and no way to move him, **and the yard does nothing, and the chapter says on the page that the reason is that the yard has nothing to do anything with.** The boy goes out at first light to take the bell's delay and **cannot take it, and does not write a figure, and says he will take it on a flat calm with nothing else going on and is not going to take it in a hurry.** Money: a mark for a lamp and oil and a hand of coal, out of eight; the bag is seven.

### Chapter 94 — Friday, the Twenty-Eighth

Thursday the twenty-seventh of April, in the shed in the morning and on the mole from ten o'clock. The clerk comes **up** the hill at eight with two instruments, and the writ's fee is read out before either of them because it is the only one of the three that is a fact: **one shilling and sixpence, in the office's own schedule of charges, made by somebody who is dead, not returnable, and the only money into that office's account in this harbor since the middle of February — and the tin is opened in front of three people to pay it and it is the last thing in that shed not touched since the first of February.** The air is the argument: a pot bought on the seventh of April standing three weeks, **Nessa Pike insists on saying so out loud on the mole before anybody is wet, in the yard's own words, and the word *once* is entered, because that is what she does.** The claim window of Friday the twenty-eighth names two divers not of the party, **each of them fixing his own limit at the boat**, the tender as Neale of the harbor's own tide gang and not a diver, the cutoff his to pull without asking anybody, nothing under the bedded material, and **the party's own sentence that the party is not on this office's work and is standing on the mole as the owner of hired gear and not as a workman, and that if the office requires a different reading the office's reeve will say so now.** Kest enters **the sixth of his own acknowledgements**, in his own hand, unshortened, saying the office has no power to lift the notice, is not lifting it, and is treating the whole of the mole from the third to the eleventh as inside the first clause whether the toe is inside it or not, **and that this one was asked for again by a party, the second time, and that the cost is his and is never free.** Kest also commits, out loud and into the ledger, that the yard is having the best of it — its air, at its own rate, hired by the authority, on ground the office has closed. The two go over at eleven and the reason is read out twice on the boy's own. The delay cannot be taken on the mole and the boy says why. The work goes for two hours and the sea gets up. At four o'clock a handbell is rung twice on the north wall and again by a man with a bad wheel, and a chain is found on the ninth seam's mooring pole, which is not a bollard, and Ewan Tristram gives the tide — **the half-tide at ten past six** — and Calder Marr says the hour and not the tide and goes down the steps at two minutes past six with the two Slees and Neale and the clerk, and there is a boat between the second lay's slip and that pole with the boy of twenty-two in her and a knife and a shackle, and he did not finish the shackle. **The clerk reads the reason into the office's own ledger in the hearing room over the tide-gang store at half past nine that morning, with the slate wall bare as it has stood since the fifteenth of March, and the day rate is settled on the step: six marks for the air at two and the pump at four, out of the clerk's coat, in front of three people, the hour written and read back twice.** Money: a mark out on the twenty-seventh, a shilling and sixpence out of the tin, and six marks in on the twenty-eighth; the bag is thirteen.

### Chapter 95 — The Accountants

Friday the twenty-eighth of April, from four o'clock to midnight, on the mole, in a boat, in the water. **The volume's climax, and it is a rescue.** A man of thirty-one had gone down to the ninth seam's mooring on his own account for six marks with no instrument and no permit — **ground the notice of the thirty-first closes, so he was not lawful there** — and a half sea came up on the top of the tide and he came out of the mole's shadow at four o'clock with a shackle of the mooring's own in a hand that would not open. **The first four minutes are spent in the wrong place, and the chapter says so four times over three days: a man looks in the shade of the wall he knows.** The reeve's barge is committed into a half sea because it is the only boat the harbor authority has, and Kest stays on the steps and says what that costs this office. The place is nine hundred yards inshore and two hundred south, five fathoms on the ebb, soft bottom, and the men of that water call it the Accountants and **there is no stone for them and no list and about eleven families and one of them belongs to Dace's man of fifty-one with the bad wheel.** The account is read out on the fender in twenty seconds with the pot hissing: ground, purpose, the diver, the tender, the limit he fixed, the cutoff that is not his to argue with, the pot of air bought on the seventh of April, and **the eighth of that pot is going on a hand lead in his own hand and the rest of it is on two men in a boat.** In the water, a small cold thing arrives in the leg of Calder Marr's right arm in the shape of the burn that comes back hours after a rope comes off a man's hand, and he acts on it, and the reason is in the second column: the man who could act was the man who could not speak. **The call of the *Grey Widow*'s man on the fender arrives out of the port quarter of Calder Marr's own head, three fathoms above the water, flat, unhurried and not out loud**, and the second column says the delay on it is not given and that the yard does not intend to invent a cause. **At eleven minutes Neale pulls the line and the tender's stop is not a request, and Calder Marr comes up furious and says he had four minutes and Neale answers him and does not apologize and does not look away.** Three men up, cold to the bone, with the shackle still in his hand and the cloth off the mooring pole round him, the two Slees in a dinghy they took without asking, and the loss: **the pot done, a compressor gland and about ten feet of the yard's own delivery hose, and the hand lead's line, at the same moment, at the same hand.** **It was not Dace's man** — it is a second-lay man of thirty-one, Ivo Trethewey, and Dace, who is forty-eight, says the only sentence he says that night, and Nessa Pike walks a man of fifty-one with a bad wheel of his own down the hill and not to talk. **The read-back is on the same night at about nine, not on the following morning.** The read-back has two columns, and **three lines under them, because there is no column for a thing that is both a fault and a life**: the notice did not save him, the yard will not say it did, the yard will not say the notice was not worth the paper it is on, and both of those are in one page and neither is a reason to touch the closure. **Harl Dace then says the thing about the writ — that the paper is what a man reads in a city and here it is what a man with a bad hand put his name against at seven in the morning — and it goes in in his own right hand and he reads it back himself.** The claim window is spent, worked, logged, and raised nothing. Money: the tin is emptied. **The bag is thirteen, the note thirty-three.**

### Chapter 96 — What the Market Would Not Take

Saturday the twenty-ninth of April, in the shed. **Thirty marks come in from four people and every one of them says the cause out loud before they put it down, and no one of them will let anybody thank them.** Edric Pringle, six marks, for four hundred feet of her own warp off her own wharf and a day of her boat, and she names the fire as free and says she has not come to be paid for it, and says the man she was thinking of is not the man you have got. **Harl Dace, eight marks, for a night's work and a boat's loss, and he asks for it to be entered that he was not paid on Friday and is not asking to be** — *a fire party is not a salvage crew and a form is a thing to be got back and I am too old to want it back.* Elias Voke of the *Anna Ray*, twelve marks, a day of a boat at eleven and three men at eleven at night and two hours of a man in a pole at two in the morning at four marks a man, in his own book on Friday night, **and he will not pay for a bell and he wants a clerk to check that the February warp and the Friday warp are not the same fault.** Coll Ferrow brings four marks in somebody else's hat and will not say how many men put in, **and the yard puts the figure in the second column with a reason against it and reads the reason out loud in front of four people: a man in a hat is not a man anybody can ask again in four years, and there is no office in this coast that can be asked about him by name.** Nessa Pike states that the yard's rule — air, rope, food, the harbor's eighth, the share, off the top of anything raised — **is not used today, because this is not a raising, and the non-use is entered in the first column with her name against it.** Out: **eight marks to the harbor's bellwright, being the going out of the second of May, bought at a market by men who said the cause out loud first; sixteen marks to the boy; six marks onto the note in the creditor's own hand**, the largest single payment off that page since the eighth of March and the only one in six weeks that has not been bread. The boy does his own arithmetic twice and the second time is the same, and **he takes the ninth, the tenth, the eleventh and the twelfth and leaves the fifth of March, which is the oldest line in the column, and says he is not going to take a man backwards in his own book.** Corvin Sarn comes up for the eight, and gives the yard the price of the going out in his own terms, and refuses to have it in a wage column, and says on the way out the door that a bell in the dark is a bell that is not there. The clerk reads the whole money column back and says what a box can hold and that he will keep the page whether the wall is up or not. Money: the bag is thirteen, the note twenty-seven.

### Chapter 97 — A Longer Leash With a Name on It

Monday the first of May, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store, at ten. **The wall is wiped at eight and for the first time since the fifteenth of March Kest puts a figure on it, and says out loud that it is a figure for a week and is not the record: FIVE OFF THIS MOLE, FRIDAY. NONE OF THEM ON THIS OFFICE. ONE MAN OUT. NOTHING RAISED.** **And in the ledger he names the five — two hired of a working boat and this office's tide gang, this office's foreman as a tender, and two of a yard that has not been on this office's work since the thirty-first of October — and says that the man they brought out was not one of the five: a man of thirty-one of the second lay, at the top of the make since the twenty-ninth of March, with a shackle of the ninth seam's own mooring in a hand that would not open, entered as a man found and not as a man worked.** The application is read out by the boy and then by the clerk, the same words, and the party's own list of what it owns is entered as a statement by the holder and not as an inventory, **because a court of wreck claims in Veyr will not accept a yard's list of gear as a fact about a yard.** The clerk reads the entries of the twenty-eighth, and then the cause of the warp, in the words of the woman who lost it, **and the price of the writ is named and entered and nobody is thanked for it: the ninth seam's mooring is out of service from the first of May with no end of it entered, the ground line is not to be cleared, the notice did not save the man and this office will not pretend that it did or that the notice was not worth the paper it is on — and Petch is stood off the wall from the first of May to the tenth to mind the chain and the hose cart in front of the last lay's wharf, so the mole steps will not be cleared in June.** A clerk of the circuit office attaches and reads a **reporting clause** — every finding of worked glass, whole or in part, and every raising of material from ground any other party claims, within four days, with the place, the hour, the person in whose keeping, and the limits; and where a finding is disputed the office may seat one of them and one other and take it as given; and the holder shall produce the writ on demand. **It carries three names on paper and no face in the room: requested in writing by the magistrate of the circuit, signed by the master of a cutter holding a claim in this harbor, sealed by Magister Ilya Serr, senior Registry examiner.** The yard's objection is entered unshortened in two hands and goes up the coast with the writ: **the clause is capable of being read to reach a bar of gravel and a man's account of a coast; the clause asks for findings and a finding is not a conclusion; and the holder does not object and takes the writ, because a yard with no paper cannot work and a yard with a reporting clause knows what it has agreed to.** The clerk says a fourth time that this office cannot enter an examination of an object. **Mara Venn says, before anybody asks her, that the writ cannot reach her and that she is not of it, and that she has a paper of her own going up the coast on her own account with three clauses about a false seal and a boat and three questions about a chart, and that she is not an assistant and is not going to be one this afternoon.** The fourth name on the writ is Tam Slee, who is not in the room, and Kest says the office cannot ask a man who is not in it whether his name is on a leash, **and Calder Marr says: then read it to him and ask him, and do not send the boy, because his hand is going; I will go and ask him myself this afternoon.** Slee says he is content to have it, in his own words, and the fourth read-back is the yard's own. The grant runs to the first of March next, is not the Second Bell license, may be suspended for cause by one man with a key and a book, **and its last line says that this office is not the office that can renew it, that the second season is not this office's to give, and that nothing in the instrument says there is a second season.** Elias Voke says it out loud that this is a longer leash, and Calder Marr says **that the yard is the most reliable crew in this harbor and it has cost a hand lead, a hose and a claim window, and the writ is a leash with their names on it, and both of those are true and neither cancels the other.**

### Chapter 98 — The Second Column of a Bell

Monday the first of May, in the shed, after the sitting. **Calder Marr goes up the hill and asks a man himself, and reads him the whole writ including the clause, and does not send the boy because the boy's hand is going; Tam Slee says he is content to have it, in those words, and gives ten words for it: *it is that a man in a boat is the only kind of man anybody hires, and this is the first time anybody has written down that I am one, and if I ever want to stop being one there will be a page that says I was.*** Corvin Sarn comes down the hill and reads three lines of the writ and says it is a better piece of paper than anything he has ever been handed and has cost a mooring, and says no word about the mooring. **Then the second column of a hung bell, in the order he keeps it in.** Checkable: the water at eight and a half feet by a pole with a mark cut in it in February and not looked at since; the fouling about the same, and about the same is not a figure; the mass the same and neither dropped nor lifted; the ground line fouled as it was and not touched, with forty feet of the harbor's own marline in it; the absence of a tongue on the frame; his own name as the harbor's bellwright and that he is not of this party and has not been asked a question. Not checkable: what three months and a half of water has done, which is not a figure a man can take off a pole; a scour that is not what it was in February and which he cannot say is different better or different worse; and **the frame is cut through the casting on one side where two parts of a chain in a lead plug have been swinging against it for a very long time, and the party is not going to describe the rest of it and is not going to be asked.** Calder Marr comes within four inches of asking him why and does not, and a shipwright says from the door that the holder has asked four people nothing this month and has been told so once. Four men read it back **and the fourth is the holder of the writ, which is the third time in two days the yard has read after its own crew.** Then Sarn puts a lead plug about the size of a thumb and a half on the bench: **the plug the chain was let in with, or most of it, with a piece of chain in it and a half-inch drilled hole in it clean, cut by a man with a breast drill and a steady arm, eleven inches under a bell at eight and a half feet in a stone mole in a working harbor, with a number in it — a two and a bit of another one and a fourth that is not a two — and he will not say what the number is and he will not let anybody write it on a slate.** He refuses to take it out of the room on purpose. Four men describe it, four descriptions do not agree, all four go in, and the reason against the whole is the shortest line in the book: **a thing in this yard's book that this yard has not got is worth more to it than the same thing in a drawer, and the party did not find it and cannot describe it and will not be asked.** The boy writes on the chest lid that there is nothing in it that anybody in this harbor can check. The money read out at six: **the bag thirteen, the note twenty-three, the tin empty**, six off the note on Saturday the twenty-ninth and four more on Sunday the thirtieth, and the four straight onto the page in the creditor's own hand with a cart in the market. A hand lead's line by Thursday, and the boy does not write it, and the yard has to.

### Chapter 99 — Three Columns, and the Sixth Hand

Tuesday the second of May, on the water and on the mole's steps, in a flat calm. Four men go out in the *Grey Widow* — Sarn, the yard, Dace, and Nessa Pike, who goes because the going out is a person in a boat in the weather and a person in a boat in the weather has a man in it. **The going out is done in four minutes off a ladder with a lantern and the fouling and the mass and the ground line are all about the same and he will not give a figure and will not have one given.** The tide bell goes at two o'clock and the delay out of the water off the south end is a hand's width of a second, **and it is the same figure as the sixth of December and the eleventh and nineteenth of January, and the yard expected it to have moved and it did not.** Sarn says from the ladder that he will not stand in a boat and tell them why it has not moved. **And the chapter's method change is the writ's doing: there is now a third column for what the boy said, because he is not in the boat and the four men cannot check him, and he is four days late in three weeks and his hand is going, and the day he cannot hold a pencil is the day a man in a city holds this yard's record in his own hand and writes what he likes into a space on a form. The top of the column carries the time it was read on the steps and the time it was written in the book, and the difference between the two is the whole of what is being fixed.** The third column also carries the delay figure, **taken by the boy himself at about two o'clock on the second of May in the same flat calm, in the same hour as the bell he took it from, and read back twice**, **with the reason that a hand's width on a lead line is not a fraction of a second and the boy is not going to invent a conversion for a book** — and, crucially, **that no occasion is entered in the delayed note and the count of occasions since the twenty-sixth of October stands at eleven where it stood on the thirty-first of March, because a figure taken on purpose on a flat calm with a slate and a stopwatch is not a note heard out of the weather, and a boy who has counted eleven of them is not going to make it twelve by writing a number.** Then the coast road is copied by hand six times **by six people of six different boats and no two of them is of this yard** — Sella Brigg round and fair, Harl Dace twice because he did not like the first, Edric Pringle very small and very fair, **Coll Ferrow, thirty-one, of the *Kestrel's Sister*, whose bad wheel is the boat's and has kept her in this harbor for nine days**, in pencil with a slate under it, Oswin Trask at his own table after forty years of writing nothing down — **and the sixth hand is the boy and it is a mouth and not a hand, and he insists on it being said. Five writers are named and described and the sixth is a man in this harbor who is not named; the count on the wall stands at six.** The head of the copy says that no copy of it is in this yard's keeping, one is on the market wall and one is in the harbor authority's ledger, and that the party asks no person to do anything with it. Dace carries two and Pringle carries two and neither was asked and neither was paid and both said the same thing in different words. The yard makes a hand lead's line at night and does it wrong once. Money: nothing moves; **the bag is thirteen, the note twenty-three, nothing in a tin.**

### Chapter 100 — The Tenth Thing on the Wall

Wednesday the third of May, on the market boards at seven in the morning, and **the volume closes on its own image.** The tenth thing goes up **under the naval paper that has been on that wall since the eighteenth of March**, and it carries eleven lines at the head, the ninth thing entire, the rescue's two columns, the bell's second column, the six hands and a mouth, and the head of the whole thing again at the bottom. Four men read it back at ten to eight with the market coming in behind them, **and the fifth read-back is Edric Pringle's**, who reads all of it in the street and stops at the fifth of the eleven lines and says that is the wrong way round: **four hundred feet of her own warp came off that wharf, and it was her own account, and she came up the hill and said the cause and put six marks in, and the yard has put the cause on a wall and got it the right way up for the first time in her life, and she is not going to thank them for it in a market with forty people in it.** **The sixth of the eleven lines is the volume's new question and it is a question, and it does not explain anything:** a bar of gravel that comes up to two and three-quarters of a fathom on the flood five miles north of the headland, on this harbor's own chart as a blank, which is not a wrong figure and cannot be argued with; a man of forty-eight with a slate who is a witness and not a party and is not paid and has not been asked to be right; and the yard's refusal to put a name on it, **because a party that has been wrong twice in public has no business putting a name on a thing.** Noll Tarner says the word out loud and is refused it, and produces the better answer himself: **the question is who edits the charts, and it is not a question about a network, it is a question about a man with a pen, and there are about forty offices on this coast that draw water and every one of them draws what it has sounded, and the day a man did want to moor on five miles of a shore, somebody is going to find out what his office has got.** A man of the second lay asks for a seventh copy and is told there is no charge and that his name goes on it, **which is the only thing on the sheet that binds anybody and it is a piece of paper and no more.** The count of the copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October is eleven and goes up as a slip under the tenth thing with the four holders it cannot reach named, and a shilling changes hands at the foot of the dock for it. The position is read out at half past four: **thirteen marks in a bag, twenty-three on the note, nothing in a tin**, the three counts of the note's weeks unmoved since the fourth of January, four lines and sixteen marks in the wage column with the oldest line the fifth of March, forty to the boy and forty to the conservator and eight shillings and tenpence to Sella Brigg all standing, twenty marks of unsold iron and a saw kerf in a crown shackle that a man of the south lay has offered a shilling for on its own, no gland and no hose, the ninth seam out of service, the tide gang four short, the light not on, the *Kittiwake* on the hard, the hard at Ashtide unanswered since the ninth of November, nineteen winters unpaid, **a man of the third lay nine winters out and not found, and a man of the second lay of thirty-one found on Friday the twenty-eighth, alive, in five fathoms of water off the inner shoals.** The delay is read back twice in the form the book has used since the twenty-sixth of December — **no source, not written as a voice** — and the boy says in the market that he is not entering an occasion and the count stands at eleven. And then, once, in the street, not on the sheet: **a place west-southwest of the headland at seventy fathoms, a drowned city, and a cairn of ballast stones and a stem in the sand and a bar of gravel about eleven feet deep, and the yard cannot put the two in the same sentence and will not.** The bell goes out again in about four years, the rope is a year old in January, it will be eight marks and a day of a boat then as it is now, the whole of the craft is the going out, nobody in this harbor wants it, and he will go, and the four of them are told now so that there is no argument in four years.

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0001 (Chapters 101–110) — complete. Movement 1, the first return and the first bill.

**Actual word count: 29,905 words across ten chapters after the Batch 0001 review repairs, and 26,767 before them** (Ch101 2,638 · Ch102 2,742 · Ch103 2,803 · Ch104 3,011 · Ch105 2,535 · Ch106 3,104 · Ch107 3,361 · Ch108 2,785 · Ch109 3,373 · Ch110 3,560), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Seven are inside the guideline and three are past it** — Chapter 107 by 161, Chapter 109 by 173 and Chapter 110 by 360 — **and each of those three grew because the review required material the batch did not have: a delivered cost beat for Nessa Pike, Calder Marr's interior life, and the move of the boat trip to the top of the flood. No cost was dropped, which the batch's own length rule prefers, and Chapters 109 and 110 are each one complete day in one complete place — a mole's steps on the twenty-second and a boat off the south end on the twenty-third — and no cost is paid twice in either.** **The hearing room is used once, on Wednesday the seventeenth, and the market wall is used once, on Thursday the eighteenth, and no two of the three institutional days in this batch are the same day and neither of them is a Monday.** The batch's pressures alternate: a clock and a bucket, a stone in daylight, a day rate, a casting and a handcart, a refused day, a boy off a stone, a refusal in a room, a market, a mole with a notebook, and a boat.

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page: THIRTEEN marks in a bag, thirty-one on the chandler's note, nothing in a tin, on Wednesday the twenty-fourth of May — the same thirteen the bag held on the third of May.** The bag moves for the first time since the twenty-ninth of April and it moves twice and both times are on the page: **twelve marks in and four out on the eighth and ninth of May off a day at the second seam on the harbor authority's own account, then eight marks in across the tenth and eleventh by letting the yard's own pump out of its own shed for the first time since the fifth of April, less three straight onto a creditor's counter for a fortnight of bread and a mark to a man and a handcart.** **The gross of the eighth is twenty-six on the board and twelve in the bag, and the closing figure is thirteen and not twenty-seven: the corrected chain is 13, 13, 13, 25, 21, 22, 25, 25, 21, 21, 13, 13. Chapter 110 now says the boat's money came out of a day at a seam and a pump let out of the shed, which are the only two things this yard has earned since the first of February.** **The note goes up once, from twenty-three to thirty-one: a compressor's gland at the creditor's own figure of five, in the creditor's own hand, with the carting not on the page and the five not paid, and a fortnight of bread at three paid out of the bag.** The three counts of the note's weeks do not move. The wage column closes the batch **five lines and twenty marks with the fifth of March the oldest**, one line deeper than it was on the third of May, and the fourteenth week is paid out of the day's money and the fifteenth is paid on a Monday in advance and the sixteenth falls on a Tuesday and no line goes in for it and the reason is that this yard has no reader.

### Chapter 101 — Four Days

Thursday the fourth and Friday the fifth of May, in the shed and at the head of the dock, with the clerk coming up the hill at nine with a form he ruled himself and no sitting called. **The chapter's question is asked in a doorway and it is the right one — from which hour do the four days run, and is the day of the finding one of them — and the clerk's answer settles the counting rule for the volume: the day of the finding is not counted, the fourth day after it is the day it falls due, and a return entered late is a return entered late and is not a different instrument and is not a smaller one. The finding is Friday the twenty-eighth of April and the return fell due on Tuesday the second of May.** **Return 1 is made on the fourth of May, two days late, with the two days on the page and not in a margin**, and it reports the raising of Friday the twenty-eighth: four hundred and forty pounds of old iron in three pieces, a bell-metal gun-port among them, the first piece up at twenty-six minutes past eleven, the three pieces off the bedded material at about twenty past one, and a lump of green glass in a bucket. **And in the keeper's line the yard discloses, unasked, that the object has been in a boat in a shed in this harbor since the twenty-eighth of April — six days inside the second clause of a notice this yard has not argued and will not** — and the clerk puts the two halves of the figure on the table and says the box has no room for the difference and this book has. The yard's own position is entered as a fact: it does not know which limb of the clause caught the lump and has not waited to find out, and a lump of green glass is itself a finding of worked glass, so the first limb is enough. **The boy enters the thirteenth week of a wage himself, in his own right hand, four marks, Tuesday the second of May, unpaid — and the reason he gives is no longer a circle: nobody in that yard had ever said out loud to him that a wage week falls on a Tuesday, the Tuesday came and went, and he would not write a line in on a day he had not been told was a day. It is a bad reason and it is his and it does not go in the second column, because a man in a city can find out when this yard pays a wage and cannot find out why a boy of twenty-two did not write a number — the column becomes five lines and twenty marks.** He asks whether the late return is his fault, is told no in nine words, and then asks why nobody came and told him his own week was out, and nobody answers, because there was nobody left to tell him. **And in the quiet after that he goes and stands on his own mole's steps and remembers the eighth of December, and the second thing at the end of a childhood call that is not there and cannot be put back, and the eleven times since December he has gone down those steps to look at a tide and come back up without looking at the water — the condition's cost, and not its symptom, and not its name.** Money: nothing moves. Bag thirteen, note twenty-three.

### Chapter 102 — What Is in the Bucket

Saturday the sixth and Sunday the seventh of May, on the mole's crown between the third and ninth seams, in the open air in daylight, with the tide making, the boat alongside with her bucket in the stern. **The seventh of the reeve's acknowledgements is entered at the yard's asking, the third time in three months, in Kest's own hand and the clerk's own page, and it is a person of this party standing on ground the office treats as inside the first clause, and the cost of it is the reeve's.** The object is described in a form a man on a mole can check: a lump about a hand's length, green at the fracture, cloudy through the body, a crust of the mole's own mortar on two sides, a sun edge on a board, **and a scalloped edge of eleven scallops in about four inches which is not a fracture and not a mould line and which Calder Marr cannot account for. He turns the board twice on the crown and does not say so, because a man who says out loud on a mole that he cannot name a thing is a man who has told a clerk where the hole is, and the clerk writes the holes down.** The bucket and the boat and then a tarpaulin and two stones and a spare fender on the crown put the object in the open air, which is what the second clause requires, and nobody argues the clause. **The chunk of the batch is the flatness: the lump is worth nothing to anybody the party has been able to find out about, said on the crown before anybody asks, and the second return is dated out loud — the sixth is the finding, the day of the finding is not counted, and the return is due on the tenth.** On the Sunday the yard argues for two hours about a line that goes in neither column: the age of the object goes nowhere at all, and the reason is that the party declines to give a figure and declines to give a reason for declining. Money: nothing moves.

### Chapter 103 — A Day Rate

Monday the eighth and Tuesday the ninth of May, in the shed, on the mole at the second seam, and in the office's own book. **The hearing room's slate wall comes down at eight, there is no sitting, and the clerk's own page wants a side and three-quarters.** The harbor authority orders a day at the second seam on its own account, at the yard's own rate, under the writ, and the reason goes into the office's book in Calder Marr's own left hand with the clerk present and unedited: ground landward of the third seam's mark, no ground above it, nothing under the bedded material, **Tam Slee of the *Grey Widow* and Wil Slee of this harbor's own tide gang in the water, each fixing his own limit at the boat, Neale on the lead line because the notice's third clause bars the yard's three named people from the authority's work.** The air is the office's own, off the office's compressor and the office's own hose, and the reason says why: this yard has no gland and ten feet of hose less than it had on the twenty-eighth of April. They raise a ring, a shackle and about four feet of a herring boat's warp, and the warp goes back onto the third lay's wharf in Dace's hands and is not claimed. **The column: gross twenty-six, the eighth three, the share three, the pot of air two, rope one, victuals two, a hand three — fourteen out, and the yard's is TWELVE, counted out of the clerk's coat with the hour written and read back twice, the bag being thirteen at eight that morning and twenty-five that night.** **While the two Slees are down Calder Marr stands at the top of the steps with the book shut under his arm, counts the minutes twice, finds that the second time he is not listening for the lead line at all, feels his own body lean forward before his hands have decided anything, and does not go down, and that there was nobody in that mole who would have known he had wanted to.** Nessa Pike signs her name against taking the eighth and the share on the authority's account and against the fact that the whole of the air in this harbor was spent on a Tuesday. The fourteenth week of a wage is paid on the Tuesday out of the twelve and is not announced on the Monday. **The boy says the second thing nobody asked for: nothing was raised off ground any other party claims, so no return goes up on this either — and a clause a yard can obey in silence is worth more to it this month than a clause it is afraid of.** Bag thirteen to thirty-nine to thirty-five.

### Chapter 104 — The Gland and the Hose

Wednesday the tenth and Thursday the eleventh of May, in the shed, at the sign of the Drowned Bell, in the net loft, and at the head of the dock. **Return 2 goes up the coast on the day it was due — the first time this yard has sent one of anything on the day — and it is shorter than the first, and it says the lump was looked at on the sixth on a mole's crown in daylight and is worth nothing, and the scalloped edge is in the first column and the party's own sentence is that it does not know what the edge means and a clause asks for a finding and not a meaning.** The clerk writes on his own page that the first was two days late and the second was on the day and both are the same instrument. The gland is bought on credit at **the creditor's own figure of five marks in the creditor's own hand on a page standing at twenty-three, and the carting is not on the page and the creditor says why he is not going to put it there; the five is on the page and is not paid.** A fortnight of bread at three takes the note to thirty-one **and comes out of the bag onto the counter while the creditor's pen is still wet, which is the practice of five volumes of this book and is now on the page for the first time in this one.** **The pump goes out of the shed for the first time since the fifth of April, hired to the man who asked for it on the twenty-second of April and did not get it, two days at four marks a day, eight marks in and two days lost**, and Nessa Pike signs her name against losing it. That man says the thing nobody in the shed wants said: he asked in April, was not got, did not think about it again until Wednesday morning, is not a customer and will not become one, **four marks a day is a fair figure and he would have paid four and a quarter and should not have been made to come up the hill twice for it.** On the Thursday **a man this yard did not know, paid a mark, takes the yard's own handcart** two miles up the coast road and comes back with the casting, and the yard does not fit it for nine days. The figures are read back by the boy, who has four days left on the stone, and the read-back now carries the whole chain of the week: **twenty-one after the wage on the Tuesday, eighteen after the bread, twenty-two at eleven o'clock, twenty-five at six.** In the net loft the woman says one thing about the making of a line and does not say the verdict, **and Calder Marr stands in the doorway and does not go in, because what he wants to say is that he was thinking about the boy's hand, and that is true, and it is the kind of true a man puts in a book with a reason against it, and there is no hand in that shed to put a reason in it with.**

### Chapter 105 — The Man Who Was Not on the Writ. **THE BATCH MIDPOINT.**

Friday the twelfth and Saturday the thirteenth of May, in the shed, at the head of the dock, and on the mole's steps. Noll Tarner comes up the hill with a coil and no boat, says in two sentences that he took a job at Ashtide in November without telling his own crew and does not apologize, and asks for a day. **The yard refuses the day and the work, and says the reason in one sentence: a man who is not on the writ is a man who cannot be reported, and a man who cannot be reported is a man whose accident a clerk has to write about as a stranger's — and it is entered in the yard's book in those words with his name on it, because four men have been refused on that mole since the fourteenth of October and he is going to be the fifth.** What he is given instead is a copy of the tenth thing in his own hand at no charge with his name on it, and the yard enters that this is a copy of a copy and does not alter the count of the six hand-copies. **Tarner then supplies the turn: a road that no chart carries is a thing a man can sell, and this one cannot be sold because nobody knows it is a road, and the day a man comes up this hill with a map the first question is not what is under it but who pays for the map — and the yard's answer, which Tarner calls the best sentence anybody in this harbor has said in five months, is that a party that has been wrong in public twice has no business selling anybody a map, and the six people who copied that sheet in six boats would not have copied it off a man with a map.** He also names **Lowfen**, about nine miles off, where the channels are kept by hand by men who do not want a diver, which goes in the second column with a reason. On the Saturday the yard writes the line it is not sending to anybody: no finding of worked glass and nothing raised off claimed ground since the sixth, no return goes up, and the reason, and the line is in the yard's book and in no office's. **The Saturday's last line is now a statement of what the yard is going to do on Monday the fifteenth rather than a narration of it two days early, and the Tuesday after is named as the first Tuesday in this column's history with nobody in the yard to enter it.** Bag twenty-five, note thirty-one.

### Chapter 106 — Monday the Fifteenth. **THE BATCH'S CENTRE.**

Sunday the fourteenth and Monday the fifteenth of May, at a doorway in the cottage row behind the fish market, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **A mother is told the date on the step because she has been told the truth about her son once in a front room and once in a shed and is not to be told it in a room — and she is the one who says the thing nobody has said to her twice: there is a bonesetter's book in this town with a boat hand in June in it, and she has been counting since the fourth of May, and she is telling them so that neither of them has to tell her anything else.** The boy reads the position out one last time and reads it twice, and the second reading takes eleven minutes and the first took seven. **The yard pays him four marks for the week of the ninth to the fifteenth, on the Monday and not the Tuesday, with the reason that this yard is not going to pay a boy a wage on a Tuesday with a hand that cannot hold a pencil, and the next wage week falls on the sixteenth and no line goes in for it.** Nobody asked him what he was worth and he is not thanked. **He leaves the book on the bench with the pencil on top of it and says one sentence, and says it once and will not say it again: from tomorrow the day does not get read back, the third column has two times at the head of it because he put them there, and there is nobody in that crew now who is going to be late, because there is nobody in that crew who is going to be writing it.** The clerk comes up for the last time on a working errand, reads the last page, and says it is the first time in five months he has come up that hill and been given less than he came for, and keeps that in his book in those words. Alden Marr is at the far bench and is not asked for anything and **neither brother goes over to him, which is now rendered differently from Chapter 101's rendering of the same refusal, because it is the one relationship in the book that should not sound the same twice.** **AND THEN THE CHAPTER GRIEVES, which it did not before the review.** The boy goes down the hill at four and nobody follows him. **Nessa Pike refuses to let Calder Marr read the empty third column aloud to nobody in a shed and sends him up on the mole instead, and he sits on the top of the steps for an hour and does the arithmetic of the difference between the third of May and the fifteenth: the four marks have been paid and the hand is not going to be paid by anybody, ever, and there is nothing in this harbor that can be entered against that, because a hand is not a debt and cannot be put on a page. He comes down at six and does the shed's work anyway, which is what a yard does on the evening a man leaves it.** Bag twenty-five to twenty-one.

### Chapter 107 — The Answer to the Seventeenth of January

Tuesday the sixteenth and Wednesday the seventeenth of May, in the reeve's office on the west basin and in the hearing room over the tide-gang store. **The eight-clause plan of the seventeenth of January is refused, four pages, SIGNED ON FRIDAY THE TWELFTH OF MAY AND SERVED ON TUESDAY THE SIXTEENTH — the date is corrected, since the chapter had it signed on the twelfth of January, five days before the plan it refuses and on the day Volume 01's own six-clause plan came back — on four grounds, every one of them right, and better written than that six-clause one, exactly as the reeve said it would be.** **AND THE COST NESSA PIKE PROMISED IS NOW ON THE PAGE INSTEAD OF BEING DECLARED IN THE LAST SENTENCE.** At the head of the dock on the Tuesday morning she makes Calder Marr put it in the day's line with a reason against it — a pump stripped late, a ring and a shackle and four feet of a herring boat's own warp laid out late — and she tells him to say out loud in the room, in front of the clerk and in his own words, that this party is not arguing one of the four grounds, **and he does, and the clerk writes it down and reads it back.** The fourth ground is new and is about Tarner: a plan asks this office for a place, an hour, a keeper and a limit, and the office can give a place and an hour and cannot give a keeper, because the three men the notice names are the yard's men and a keeper who is not named is a stranger's accident. Elias Voke is in the room because he carried the paper, and he carries a third paper to a coast office in the same hand. **Kest says the sentence the volume needs: the office that has to countersign it is the office that has to say the notice is wrong, and no man in that office is going to say it, and a reeve cannot make his own office say a thing.** What would move it is a change of politics in an office on a schedule nobody can name. **And he pays, and he names the price in the room before he pays: a claim window of one date, Monday the twelfth of June, for the reeve and the clerk and two tide-gang men to go out on the mole's toe and inner face between the third and ninth seams and enter in the office's own book what the notice of the thirty-first has cost this harbor authority.** That is the **eighth** of the reeve's acknowledgements, the first in this harbor no party has asked for, entered on his own motion in his own hand on the seventeenth, and the clerk and Nessa Pike both read it back. **The price lands on Elias Voke: the account goes up the coast in the same file as his statement of the capacity of this harbor, so the first thing a man in a coast office opens is this office's own account, and the nineteen winters of dues are going to be harder to dispute and not easier.** Voke does not shout, asks for his name and his boat and the nineteen winters in his own hand, and says one thing that is his and not the reeve's. Money: none moves. The plate stands in eleven feet, seen and not measured and not touched.

### Chapter 108 — A Man With a Slate and a Man With a Book

Thursday the eighteenth and Friday the nineteenth of May, in a market, in daylight, and not in a room. **A man who trades up and down this coast and is nobody's clerk — and who was in the street at the head of this yard's own dock on the third of May with that same slate — asks the same four questions he has been asking since the middle of April and has written down every answer he has been given, including the noes.** The yard answers him in public, in four sentences, in Calder Marr's own hand on the back of a slate, **and does not refuse him, because there is nothing to refuse, and says so, because being given an answer is not a courtesy.** Then he offers four marks for a clean copy with his name on it, and the yard refuses, **and cannot refuse by saying the sheet is the harbor authority's, because it is not, and cannot refuse by saying a man with a pen edits the charts, because that is a question this yard put on a wall and has not answered — so the reason given on the boards is that six people copied that sheet by hand in six boats on the second of May, one of them in pencil on a slate, and that they would not have copied it off a man with a map.** He writes all nineteen minutes of it down, says it is the first time in nine years anybody on this coast has said no and given him a reason that was about him and not about a rule, does not argue, does not come back, and does not buy. **Mara Venn is in the market, not asked, and prices six marks and two days out loud and says this yard has had two of her findings for nothing and has not been told what they cost; she repeats her own forty marks and the Ashtide shop's four marks in the refused four in writing, on the boards, and says the two are not to be got mixed up on a page.** She then says the thing the coast is built out of: a record of a refused thing is a better record than a thing that was never asked for, and a man with a slate is the first person on this coast making one, for nothing. **Her last few words — *Who reads it back tomorrow*, said to nobody in particular and looking at the place where the boy's book is on the bench — now land on Calder Marr: he had come down the hill that morning expecting the shape of a folded ledger and had caught himself two days late, doing it out of habit before he had remembered the hand, and the board behind her is a board and there is no book on it and there has not been one since Monday. A yard that reads its own figures out loud to a man in a market has decided that the reading is the job, and the reading was the boy's job, and the boy's job was four marks a week and a hand, and a hand cannot be hired in a market for four marks because there are not any.** Nothing is sold and no paper comes back out of a city. The word is not on the wall. Eleven logged occasions and two empty answers stand and none is entered.

### Chapter 109 — The Writ on the Mole. **THE BATCH CLIMAX.**

Saturday the twentieth to Monday the twenty-second of May, in the shed, in a doorway, and on a mole in the open air with the tide making. **The demand comes up the hill on the Saturday in Ambrose Sell's hand, names Monday the twenty-second at ten, names the top of the mole's steps landward of the third seam's mark, and states that it is a demand and not a complaint and that no answer is asked for — and that the officer of a vessel holding a claim is a different man from the clerk and that the two of them will not be alone with the yard.** Calder Marr asks what standing one foot lower would cost and is told that a ninth would have to be asked of a man at a sitting out of the harbor and cannot be fetched by a clerk on a Saturday. **No ninth acknowledgement is entered, the yard says where it will stand and why out loud, and the clerk writes it down and then writes a second line under it that the ground the notice closes begins below the top of the steps, in his own reading and not a ruling.** On the Sunday the book goes onto a board with two battens cut out of a seat entered at no figure, and Calder Marr writes a line under the head of the third column saying no man in this party is filling it and that the two times at the head of it are not now knowable, with the reason that there is nobody here to fill it. On the Monday the wall comes down at eight, there is no sitting, and two men come out onto the steps. **Produced: the writ with the clause and the seal line and the fourth name read twice; the crew's book, whole, because the clause's own construction carries it and nobody asked for it and the yard says so before opening it; both returns unshortened; and the tenth thing and the slip with the eleven names, handed over because they are already public and refusing them would look like a man with something.** The man in the coat asks whether there is a third return, and is told there is no third and no fourth and that the yard will say it has made two. He asks whether they looked at the lump, and is told they did, on the sixth, on a crown in daylight, because the second clause keeps recovered transparent mineral out of every shed, here is the return, it is worth nothing, and the yard is not going to say the scalloped edge means anything because it does not know and a clause asks for a finding. **He then asks who fills the third column, and is told: we do, and this yard is one man lighter than it was on the fifteenth, and the column is going to be filled late and the lateness is going in the second column with a reason.** The one thing read out of the book is the boy's own handwriting about the figure and the count — **the boy's own words, quoted by the crew for the second time in the batch and compressed on this second use, because a man reading a line out of a book is not a boy making a claim. The conservative-reading sentence is now in seven entries and not six, which is what it is by the twenty-second. And on the Sunday Calder Marr sits with the board after the others have gone down the hill and admits to himself that he does not want to hand that book to a man in a coat, and enters none of it in the second column, because the second column is for things a man in a shed can check and the whole of this yard's method is that a thing which cannot be checked goes in anyway with a reason against the line — and it was the first time he had wanted to leave one out.** Nothing went into the water and no return is due and none goes up.

### Chapter 110 — The Boat Off the South End

Tuesday the twenty-third and Wednesday the twenty-fourth of May, in the shed at seven in the morning, **in a boat off the south end, lying to off the south end through six hours of flood because the top of the flood is the only hour a man can hear anything in this harbor, and the bell goes at ten past three**, on the mole's steps at half past three, and in the net loft at half past ten at night. **THE WHOLE DAY IS RE-STAGED: the bell used to ring at two o'clock in a trip that had ended at half past nine.
