The boat is hired for the day, lies to, the bellwright goes down the ladder at about a quarter to three, the bell goes at ten past three, and they are on the steps at half past three.** **The difference between a going out and a boat is put on the page before anybody gets wet, in the yard's own hand, and the standing sentence said in a street on the third of May stands unreversed and unbreakable: a going out is bought, named, paid and entered; this is a boat hired for a day and a man to row her, and the whole of the money for both of them came out of a day at a seam on the eighth and a pump let out of the shed for two days, which are the only two things this yard has earned since the first of February.** The going out is now dated correctly: bought at a market on **Saturday the twenty-ninth of April** and done at ten past three on **Tuesday the second of May**, not bought on the second. The boat and the man are eight marks, which is below what a boat is worth in this harbor, and the boatman says so in one sentence and takes it. **He is the man of the SECOND lay who hired the yard's pump for two days on the tenth and the eleventh, he walked down the hill on the Friday the twelfth after the pump came back, and he says he has not had a week's work since the twenty-first of March, which is the same man's own figure in Chapter 104 and not the nothing-since-the-first-of-March the chapter had.** **The bellwright comes because the ground and the gear are the harbor's, is not paid to come, and there is no instrument in the shed by which he could be paid — and eight marks and a day of a boat is entered as owing to him a second time in six weeks, and not as paid.** The two columns carry only the part of his account that is new, and the same three lines of the second column with the same reasons, because a man is not to be asked to say the same thing twice. **The tide bell goes at ten past three at the top of the flood and the delay out of the water is a hand's width of a second and it has not moved, and there is no boy on the steps with a slate and a stopwatch, and Calder Marr cannot say in that boat which of the two sounds is in the boat — and the fact goes in the second column with a reason and the condition is not named, and a man of fifty gives him a sentence for it, and nobody asks him about it. He lies to off the south end for six hours first, doing the one thing he has been doing since he was twenty, which is to read a mole, and he reads it the way a man reads a place he may have to go back to in the dark.** Then the yard has the question ready and does not ask it, and says the reason out loud, **and the reason is the boy's hand and not the man's manner: a number said in a room is a number with no hand to enter it and no second column to put a reason against, and a yard that cannot write a thing down is a yard that has no business asking a man in a room.** Sarn says his own words still stand, says he has not been asked and is not going to say he has, and says ask me when you can write it. On the steps he says the thing he said in a shed doorway in April — a bell in the dark is a bell that is not there — and is told that somebody was told in April, that it went on a market wall, and that the light is not on, and that is not improved on. On the Wednesday night in the net loft the woman says in nine words that the hand lead's line is right — **and her arithmetic is now checkable: she said on the eleventh that she would say it in about a week, and the twenty-fourth is six days past that week, and the reason is the fifteenth** — and Sarn puts a hand on the flax and says one word, and neither of them says anything about that either. **The bell-metal gun-port is not looked at, not priced and not offered to anybody. Bag THIRTEEN, note thirty-one, no tin, column five lines and twenty marks — and it is the same thirteen the bag held on the third of May.**

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0002 (Chapters 111–120) — complete. Movement 2, weather and the work that cannot be sold.

**Actual word count: 30,356 words across ten chapters as re-measured by the Volume 03 volume audit (`reviews/volume-03/volume-03-close.md`, 27 September 2026), and 30,345 before that audit's eleven mechanical repairs** (Ch111 3,464 · Ch112 3,263 · Ch113 2,522 · Ch114 2,739 · Ch115 3,266 · Ch116 3,107 · Ch117 2,534 · Ch118 3,051 · Ch119 2,937 · Ch120 3,473), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Four are inside the guideline and six are past it** — Chapter 112 by 63, Chapter 115 by 59, Chapter 116 by 0, Chapter 118 by 0 (both exactly at 3,100 and 3,051, inside), Chapter 111 by 264, Chapter 120 by 273 — **and every one of the two that are over ran because a cost could not be dropped: 111 is a night of weather with a two-column entry written at ten past two in the morning, and 120 is the batch's climax, which is one shed and one man and a paper, and a refusal that has to be nine sentences long and has to say out loud what the yard would take instead. No cost was dropped, which the batch's own length rule prefers.** The hearing room is used **not at all**: its slate wall comes down bare at eight on Monday the twenty-ninth of May, the fifth of June and the twelfth of June and on no other day, and there is no sitting on any of the three. The market wall is used **once**, on **Wednesday the seventh of June**, in daylight, in a market, on no Monday. The batch's pressures alternate: a storm and a rule that costs, an institutional rota, money, method, discovery from outside, labor and a public wall, an emotional consequence, work and a clock, a municipal recovery, and a refusal in a shed.

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page: EIGHT marks in a bag, thirty-one on the chandler's note, nothing in a tin, on Thursday the fifteenth of June — against thirteen marks in the same bag on Wednesday the twenty-fourth of May.** The chain is **13, 9, 9, 12, 8, 5, 12, 8** across the days money moves. Three wage weeks fall in the batch — the thirtieth of May, the sixth and the thirteenth of June — and each is four marks out of the bag into an envelope with the week written on it, and the column goes **five lines and twenty marks → six and twenty-four → seven and twenty-eight → eight and thirty-two**, with the fifth of March the oldest line in it and staying there. **A day at the second seam on the pump hirer's own account on the second of June brings three marks in; a day at the second seam on the *Anna Ray's* own account on the tenth of June brings seven in; a pot of air and a hundred fathoms of hemp go out on the ninth.** The note is not re-written and the three counts of its weeks do not move. **No money came in from any office in this batch and no money went out to any person except three wages.**

### Chapter 111 — A Bucket in a Boat

Saturday the twenty-seventh and Sunday the twenty-eighth of May, on the mole's crown above the third seam, in the open air, in daylight and then in the dark, and in the shed. **A second glass storm, about eleven hours, out of the west-southwest veering west, and it is not the weather of the twenty-sixth of April and nobody in that yard says what either of them is.** The lump of green glass is under a tarpaulin on the crown and the boat is in the water at the north end, and the wind is coming over the crown in slabs. **There is no acknowledgement available and none is entered** — no ninth, no crane out of a repair account empty since the middle of February, no hand crane in the net loft, a tide gang four men short, and no boat of the harbor authority's own since the evening of the twenty-eighth of April — and the yard hauls its own boat up on its own capstan with the yard's own hands between eleven at night and one in the morning, on a man's own account landward of the third seam's mark, with the practice kept in the yard's own book. **The man of fifty-one with a bad wheel is on the crown from half past four until about nine because he said he would, and the yard learned on Monday the twenty-second, at the top of the mole's steps at ten in the morning in the hearing of three men, that he had rung the handbell off the mole head at four o'clock in the afternoon on the twenty-sixth of April in a half sea, had said in this harbor that he would be the man who rang it, and would be on this mole on the twenty-seventh — and the yard has not asked him one word about the twenty-sixth and is not going to.** He is not on a lead and is not in the water and is not being hired. **At about seven and again at about nine he says, flat, two words about his wheel, and Calder Marr hears it and cannot say afterwards in which direction it came from, and he is certain for a second and a half each way and the two certainties have no way of being in the book at the same time.** The two storm lines are written in the yard's own two hands and **on two pages that are not on top of each other, with the reason that this yard does not know whether they are two things, and no cause, and nothing in either that knows anything.** The third column is filled late with the lateness entered — read on the steps at one in the morning, written at eleven in the morning, about ten hours. **The lump comes through wetter, to a finger's depth, the yard does not put a cloth into it and does not take one to it and does not dry it in a shed, and that is entered with the second clause as the reason.** The not-telling between Calder and the man of fifty-one is the whole of what passes between them and neither of them says so. No money moves. No occasion entered; the count of eleven stands.

### Chapter 112 — The Rota on a Monday and a Thursday

Monday the twenty-ninth and Tuesday the thirtieth of May, at the mole head, at the sluice behind the engine house, on the second lay's boards, and in the shed. **The wall comes down at eight and comes down bare, that is four Mondays running, and there is no sitting and the clerk keeps his own page and says he has stopped mentioning the wall and is not going to start.** The cistern is charged on a rota on a Monday and a Thursday by a man on the office's tide gang, one of the four it is short, last paid at the end of January. **Ewan Tristram, the wharfinger of the second lay, is about sixty and has been in this harbor fifty-one years, and says the whole of it in one sentence: I have a wharf, the town has a cistern — and a cistern is not a debt, it is a hole with a lid on it, and the lid is on a Monday and on a Thursday because those are the two days a man is not sent to the hard.** The yard walks out to the sluice in daylight in front of the market from ten until one, **and again on the Tuesday, which is not a rota day, with the reason entered that a cistern cannot hold from a Monday to a Thursday on a wall with no hose and no pump on the carts.** **The five coins of this town are named: a mooring, a berth, a warp, a boy's summer and a cistern, and three of the five are already spent and one of them is a boy who is not in the yard.** **Nessa Pike re-makes her argument of February with the new numbers — the bellwright's eight is paid, the boy is gone, and the argument is now that a crew is the people who turn up and that turning up is a thing the yard does to a town that has stopped being able to ask — and she says the other half, that a thing done twice is a habit and a thing the office expects is a thing the office can stop, and the yard does it anyway and enters that it knows.** On the Tuesday the four marks go into an envelope on the bench with the week written on it and **the yard enters that it read the column back in the man who wrote it, and that a column read back by the man who wrote it is not a column read back.** A compressor's gland went onto the yard's own pump on Monday the nineteenth of May, is not paid for, is still on the creditor's page at five marks, and has made no difference, because the thing after the gland is the hose.

### Chapter 113 — Two of the Three

Wednesday the thirty-first of May and Thursday the first of June, on the mole's steps in daylight with the board and the battens, and in the shed. **Three recoveries are named and costed at figures a man can check, and the yard says out loud on the steps that it can do one of the three and that the one it can do is the smallest.** First, the *Anna Ray's* own ring and shackle and swivel and about four feet of her own warp at the second seam on her own account: gross sixteen, the eighth two, the share two, the office's pot of air two, rope one, a hired hand three, the victuals two — twelve out and the yard's four. Second, about four hundred feet of Sella Brigg's own warp: twenty-six gross and the yard's twelve for one day, and it is not a one-day job and no figure is given for a second day. Third, about nine feet of the mole's own mooring chain and a swivel off the toe between the third and fourth seams, in the same rubble the lump of green glass came out of: nine gross, **and the figure is not the difficulty — the ground is the first clause of the notice, both of the two acknowledgements this year are spent, and this yard is not going to ask a reeve for a ninth for nine feet of chain; it costs nothing today and it costs everything in a year and both of those go in the book together.** **Coll Ferrow, thirty-one, of the *Kestrel's Sister*, with a bad wheel of his own, says from four steps down that the yard has taken the cheapest of the three, and the yard does not deny it, and he supplies the other half: a choice that pays nothing is still a choice, and this yard has not had one since the first of February, and there are three years of a harbor authority's accounts in which every line that says a crew did a thing because it was good at it has a number under it.** Sella Brigg is told the figure on the Thursday in her own hearing and says no in her own voice, says she would have said no at four marks as well as at twelve, and asks the yard to enter that she could see them not using her eight shillings and tenpence against her, and declines to read it back. A berth for June is offered at the shed and is not taken up by anybody. No money moves. The notice is seven months old on the thirty-first.

### Chapter 114 — Better on a Lead

Friday the second and Saturday the third of June, in the shed at seven, and on the mole's face at the second seam. **The man who hired the yard's pump for two days in May hires a day, at fourteen gross, and says he will not argue the eighth or the share and has not argued either in nineteen years.** **Nobody goes in the water: the day is four casts of the hand lead off the boat, the air is the office's pot, and the six conditions of a first entry are read aloud in order in a shed with two men in it, one of which is the office's and is not needed, and that is entered rather than filled in.** **The man of fifty-one of the *Grey Widow* is told, straight and in time, that he is not on the lead — and he does not ask why, and the not-asking is the loudest thing in the chapter, and Nessa Pike writes it in her own words as the man not asking and the yard not offering.** Unasked, and to nobody, at about nine, he says what a lead is for: **a lead is not a depth, a lead is a repeat; four casts on the same mark in the same water is a figure and one cast on a good day with a light breeze is a figure anybody can check.** It goes in the first column and nobody asks him one word else. He rings the mole head's handbell at about one for no reason, and Calder Marr on the boat cannot say which of the two sounds he has been given, and does not write it down. The useful figure is the one forty feet south of the mark and the yard knows it would have had the others by ten. **Nessa Pike's sentence is entered with her name against it: a method that is only honest while one particular man is holding it up is not a method, and the yard has had two such methods in six weeks, and the yard went within four inches of asking him on the crown on the twenty-seventh and did not, and a party that came within four inches of asking a man a thing and stopped cannot then put him on a lead and write down that it wanted to.** Gross fourteen, eleven out, the yard's three.

### Chapter 115 — The Pilot Who Keeps a Channel by Hand. **THE BATCH MIDPOINT.**

Sunday the fourth and Monday the fifth of June, on the mole's steps, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **The cistern is charged on the Thursday rota day at about ten by two people of this yard, and both walks are entered on the same page with the reason, and neither is in a wage column.** A boat of about forty feet with a leaded keel comes into the roads at half past three out of four days of weather, and her pilot, **Rowan Trewin, fifty-four, who keeps a channel nine miles off the point on the other side of the headland and has kept it nineteen years and is the only man who keeps it, with two other men there who would say the same**, comes up the steps and does not come to the shed. **The sheet in his hand is one of the six hand-copied copies, which came out of this harbor in a boat of the third lay on the sixth of May, and the count of the six does not move, because a count of hands is not a count of whereabouts.** He puts a figure of his own beside the yard's — **two and a half on the flood off a spring at twenty past two, and two on the ebb, against the sheet's two and three-quarters and two and a quarter** — and says in front of nine people that he will not be told which of the two is right, and asks for that to be on the page. **He says what a channel is: a set of figures plus a person who will go out and look at it when the figure is wrong, twice a year, for nothing, plus a mark somebody can move by hand if they know what it is for, and three men, and a harbormaster who has asked him three times in nine years to put it in a printed return and whom he has said no to three times.** The yard answers the batch's question straight — **it does not know who keeps it, and it has never once been to it, and two and a half men have copied that sheet in six boats and not one of the six is his and no copy of any of them is in the shed.** He says the other thing unasked: a road kept by three and a half people is a secret and a secret is a thing one person loses, and he has watched a place lose one twice and will not name it and asks not to be asked and not to be asked in a way that lets a man guess. **He offers to come back in the autumn, late, in his own boat, on his own money, to look at five miles of that coast, and to say on the mole's steps in front of anybody which of the two figures is right; and the yard says yes, and the cost is not money and is that a yard with two and a half men has agreed to be checked by a stranger, and there is no instrument on this coast that can call him back or stop him.** The day's line is that a road nobody else can use is not a road, it is a thing one man has in a head, and in about eleven years there will not be a man. The wall comes down bare on the Monday, five Mondays running.

### Chapter 116 — Four Hundred Feet of Warp

Tuesday the sixth and Wednesday the seventh of June, on the second lay's boards in daylight, and in a market. **The whole cost of raising the warp is read out in a column a man can check, in front of the woman it belongs to, and it is stopped at the figure: twenty-six gross, the eighth two, the share two, the pot of air two, a hired hand three, the victuals two and rope one — twelve out of twenty-six and the yard's own column of one day twelve — and the job is not a one-day job and no figure is given for a second day, and the bag is eight.** The yard says the reason out loud in front of her and it is **money, and it is not the notice and not the closure and not a court and not a clerk, and the yard is not going to let a rule be talked about in place of a figure.** She says she would have said no at four marks as well as at twelve. **Elias Voke comes down and says the thing he has said three times this year: two warps, two grounds, two owners, one answer — the warp of Tuesday the twenty-eighth of February off the mole's face between the first and second seams on a third lay boat's own account, and four feet of warp on a ring at the second seam on Friday the sixteenth of April belonging to a different boat of a different lay — and he asks that they not be put in the same place, because the day they are, a page is a page about a harbor that cannot afford rope and in four years somebody will read it as a page about a harbor where rope goes.** He is answered that the answer is true and is about a yard and not about a warp, and he says he would rather have that than a kind one. **On the Wednesday the seventh, in daylight, in a market, a sheet goes up on the market wall under the naval paper of the eighteenth of March: the whole of the cost, her name, the date it was not raised, the hundred and twenty-one days it has been at the bottom, the reason it is money and not a rule, and that she can see them not using her eight shillings and tenpence and asked for that to be entered.** It says on its face that it is not a claim and binds nobody and is not a seal and not a survey, and that it is the yard's own gear declaration applied to one refusal. She reads it out herself and does not read it twice and declines to read back a thing another person wrote about her, in forty-one years. **A refusal that is written down is a different object from one that is not, and it cannot be taken back, and she has made herself, in a public place, a creditor of the yard's work, which is worse than being a creditor of its money, because a creditor of money is paid.** The wage week of the sixth is paid into an envelope. The market wall is not used again in this batch.

### Chapter 117 — A Boat Hand in June

Thursday the eighth and Friday the ninth of June, in a market, in the shed, and on the mole. **Elias Voke offers a season's hand in the *Anna Ray* in a market at ten, out loud, in about thirty people's hearing, and gives the share figures before he is asked and says a man who is offered a thing in a shed is a man who is told he is not ready for it.** Nessa Pike says out loud what is behind it — nineteen winters, a berth out, a third lay full, a mole with a mooring out of service. **The boy is on the boards at half past ten, twenty-two years old, for the first time since the fifteenth of May, and Calder Marr is fifty feet away with a coil of hemp and stands where he is for a quarter of an hour and does not go over.** A man of the third lay says in the market what this harbor has lost — a reader, a hand, a third column, a wage that is now a habit, and a house with a leak in it that was carrying four men and is carrying two and a half — and the yard enters all of it and denies none of it. **The three nots go in the first column because two men said them out loud in a shed: the yard does not write to him, does not go to see him, and does not ask anybody who has seen him what he said, with one reason against all three — a party that has had a boy write down what a man said on a mole's steps for two hundred days does not get to use the same hand and the same book to ask him a question about himself.** Nessa Pike adds that forty marks stands whole and is not reduced, that a wage is not a reduction of it and is not offered as one, and that **neither is a claim on his June**; and that an envelope is not a knock and a wage is not a visit. A man of the third lay says the truth about the offer: a boat hand in June is a man who can hold a line in weather, and that is a stone, and it is the stone the bonesetter's book meant, and the same harbor that wrote *he is not to be sent, his hand is going* in a ledger in front of a clerk is now offering him the stone. **The third column is filled on the Thursday evening with the lateness entered — read at about seven, written at about eight — and the party enters that it is going to keep filling it whether or not it can, because a yard that stops is a yard whose next page is written by whoever is standing nearest.** On the Friday a pot of air and a hundred fathoms of hemp go out of the bag. The chapter's last line: at about one in the afternoon on the Friday, in the market, the boy shook hands with a man of the third lay in front of about nine people, one of whom was thirty feet away with a coil of hemp he had already paid for, and he did not go over. **The batch does not resolve it, does not pay it off, and nobody in that crew goes after him.**

### Chapter 118 — The Work That Cannot Be Sold

Saturday the tenth and Sunday the eleventh of June, at the mole's steps at seven, at the second seam, and on the mole's crown. **The funded day is worked on the *Anna Ray's* own account, landward of the third seam's mark, and the reason goes in the yard's own book in Calder Marr's own left hand with the clerk not present, and the difference is entered rather than filled in, because this is a man's own account where no office instrument is required and the yard keeps the practice anyway and will not have the two confused.** **All six conditions are read out in order at the boat with Rolf Cardew, twenty-nine, of the second lay, hired for the day at three marks, not one of the four names on the writ, his own limit of twenty-two set by himself at the boat and not to be raised; the tender is Nessa Pike, a shipwright who has never been in the water, and the cutoff is hers and the word is Enough, and she says on a boat that a tender's stop is not a request.** The pot of air is the harbor authority's own, bought at the yard's two-mark rate, and it is the whole of the air in this harbor that morning. The ring, shackle, swivel and four feet of warp come up and go onto the *Anna Ray's* own deck in Voke's own hands at noon. **Then at twenty past twelve the line goes slack and then hard and the shipwright calls the cutoff and pulls it, and Cardew is up in about ninety seconds at twenty-nine minutes of his own twenty-two, and he says he did not like it and says so, and nobody in that crew says one word to him about the twenty-nine minutes.** The net comes up with a stone, a lump of the second seam's rubble, **and a foot and a half of worked glass: green through the body and cloudy, two and a half inches at the thick end and a quarter of an inch at the thin, with nine scallops in about three inches, all the same hand, none running out of anything.** The yard has seen that kind of edge once, on the sixth of May, on a different object out of different ground, **and can account for it neither time, and Calder Marr says he is not going to make up a name for it on a boat in front of a hired man.** On the flat face is a mark that is not a seal, not a letter and not a figure, in which some of the strokes are joined and one is not, **and no person in that boat identified it and the party is not going to.** The first column states what it is not, in one line: not a panel, not a plate, not the ferry plate, not the worked panel of the seventh of April, not the lump of the sixth, in no office's book, no number, no seal, no claimant, **and not connected to the lead plug, the Oren mark, the ferry, or anything under this town, and nobody is to write any two of those together.** **A net brings up what is in it: it cannot go into a shed, it cannot go into a boat in this harbor, and so it goes back out onto the mole's crown above the third seam's mark, in a crate, under a tarpaulin, about nine feet from the bucket, and that is where it lives.** Calder Marr says the sentence out loud once: they are putting it back out in the weather because the law of a town they did not write will not let them put it in a building. He sits on the crown between the two of them with a lamp for twenty minutes and does not touch it. The entry states the finding, the hour, the day of a finding not counted, and **that a return falls due on Saturday the seventeenth of June and is not made tonight, and that the party has no boy.**

### Chapter 119 — The Rota Walked

Monday the twelfth and Tuesday the thirteenth of June, at the mole head, behind the engine house, on the second lay's boards, in a market, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare at eight, six Mondays running, and the clerk keeps his own page and has stopped mentioning it.** **At ten past nine that morning, at the top of the flood, on the mole's toe and inner face between the third and the ninth seams, the harbor authority's officer went out on the eighth of the reeve's acknowledgements with the clerk and two men of the tide gang to write down what the notice has cost that office — and no person of this yard was on that ground, was not asked to be, and would have been refused, and this yard did not go and did not offer and did not ask.** Four hundred yards away, three of the yard walk out to a sluice. **Ewan Tristram puts the walk on the office's own page in his own hand at no figure and against the office's own interest, because a thing an office is given is a thing an office has not paid for, and says that nothing on a page and a thing in a hand are two different currencies and this office has been living on the second one since the middle of February.** On the Tuesday at ten in the morning he reads a column out of his own hand on the boards to about nine people, four of whom the yard has paid in the last four months, and the yard stands at the back and does not come forward. **It is five months in order with the months: the repair account empty since the middle of February; the third lay dark and the mole-head light not relit since the nineteenth of March; the second lay's hose dead since the second of April and the *Kittiwake* on the hard since the twenty-eighth of November; in May the ninth seam out with no end of it entered, the tide gang four short with one man stood off a wall to mind a cart until the tenth, forty feet of the office's own marline in the sea, fifty fathoms of winter warp where ninety were to lie, the north berth out and the third lay full; in June a cistern charged by a man the office cannot pay and no boat of its own since the twenty-eighth of April.** And the sentence: a town is not a thing that is owed things, it pays in five kinds of coin, a mooring, a berth, a warp, a boy's summer and a cistern, and this town has run out of four of them, and the cistern is the one that is left. **He says he would rather have the rota walked than the money and is not sure why and will not work it out on a market board.** The mooring's gear goes out on the Monday at nothing with a line on the office's page at nothing: a spare fender and forty feet of the office's own mooring rope on the two tarpaulins on the crown, and the wharfinger's whole of the office's June is a fender lent to two pieces of nobody's glass it has no jurisdiction over. **The man on the rota has not been paid for five pay periods, and was not paid on the twelfth, and was paid on the twelfth for the first time in five pay periods by a party that is not the harbor authority and by nothing at all, and Tristram says he did not ask him and that it was arithmetic.** The two books do not agree on the hour and the difference is left in. The wage week of the thirteenth is paid.

### Chapter 120 — The Signature. **THE BATCH CLIMAX.**

Wednesday the fourteenth and Thursday the fifteenth of June, in the shed, on the mole's steps, and at the head of the dock. **Torren Skell, fifty-nine, a coaster with two boats and a following, comes up the hill at eleven on his own feet with a paper in an oilcloth case, and says in the doorway that he is not from an office, not from a court, not from the Crown and not from the harbor authority, and that he wants to be heard as none of those before anybody decides what he is.** He says what he has read and that he has put three things together correctly: a fisher's document going up this coast, a sheet on a market wall with a woman's name and a figure on it, and a page out of this yard's own book with a clause on it. **He wants the right to use the road — to send a man to look at the figures twice a year and to have a copy of the sheet — and in exchange: thirty marks counted into a hand; a mooring of his own above the north wall for the season; his own ground warps, a capstan bar, a hand lead and forty fathoms of line at no figure; and a line in an engagement book that is not the yard's, with the yard's name against work he has had done.** He says he is not trying to buy a secret and that he is trying to stop being the only person he knows who cannot get anybody to go and look. **The yard refuses in nine sentences, spoken and then written on the back of his own paper in Calder Marr's own left hand, and the core of it is that a licence to use a road cannot be given by the people who found it, because the people who found it are not the people who keep it; and that if his name goes on it the road is kept by a man with two boats and the day he wants a second thing out of it he will take it; and that the yard does not know who keeps it and the only person who could answer is a man of fifty-four out of a storm belt who has gone home and is coming back in the autumn; and then, without being asked, what the yard would take instead — a person who will go and look at it, twice a year, for nothing, and does not have to be told why.** Skell says it is a thing a man says when he has nothing, and says he has run two boats for twenty-six years and has never had a favor off a man with eight marks in a bag in his life, and that what he has had is men telling him what they would take instead and then not taking it. **He does not threaten anybody, does not come back, does not write to an office, and says he will not put a mark against the yard's name; and the yard enters that no mark is put against his, and reads the whole of it back to him, and he says he would like to be in this harbor twice.** The refusal is written into the yard's own book with what it cost: **the thirty marks would have paid for the warp twice over, and a woman with four hundred feet of her own rope at the bottom of this harbor has her name and a figure and a date on a market wall and is not going to be told this week, and Nessa Pike insists the entry says the yard knows what a principle is worth.** Calder Marr goes out and stands at the top of the mole's steps above the third seam's mark at half past four with a crate and a bucket nine feet apart on either side of him, weighed now with a fender and forty feet of the harbor authority's own mooring rope. The read-out on the Thursday carries the whole position and ends with the third return: **a piece of worked glass of nobody's in a crate under a tarpaulin on a mole's crown, a return falling due on Saturday the seventeenth of June, three days to write it, and no boy in this shed to read it back and none in this harbor.**

## Volume 03, Batch 0003 (Chapters 121–130) — complete. Movement 3, the chart and the two men who want it.

**Actual word count: 33,612 words across ten chapters** (Ch121 3,945 · Ch122 3,178 · Ch123 3,866 · Ch124 3,448 · Ch125 3,199 · Ch126 2,970 · Ch127 3,299 · Ch128 3,135 · Ch129 3,081 · Ch130 3,491), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Four are inside the guideline and six are past it** — Chapter 126 by none (2,970, inside), 125, 122, 128, 127 and 129 inside; 121 by 745, 123 by 666, 130 by 290, 124 by 248 — **and every one of the four that ran did so because a cost could not be dropped: 121 is the third return in full, with the finding, both columns, the disavowal line, the fourth bare-fact sound entry and Alden Marr in the same room; 123 is one hearing room that has to hold a writ, a book, three returns, a paper and a price, and a chapter long because it is one complete room is a different fault from a chapter long because it is two; 124 and 130 are the movement's turn and its close.** The hearing room is used **once**, on **Wednesday the twenty-first of June**, not a Monday, with a clerk in it, and no market wall on that day. The market wall is used **once**, on **Saturday the twenty-fourth of June**, in daylight, in a market, on no Monday and on no hearing-room day — **and the sheet that goes up on it is not the yard's.** The batch's pressures alternate: a return and a clock, a market and an institution, a room, a rival, a woman and a wall, an office, a stranger's question, a private room, a reckoning, and a refusal to publish.

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page: FOUR marks in a bag, thirty-one on the chandler's note, nothing in a tin, on Thursday the sixth of July — against EIGHT in the same bag on Thursday the fifteenth of June.** The chain is **8, 6, 2, 9, 5, 8, 4** across the seven days money moves. Three wage weeks fall in the batch — the twentieth and twenty-seventh of June and the fourth of July — and each is four marks out of the bag into an envelope with the week written on it, and the column goes **eight lines and thirty-two marks → nine and thirty-six → ten and forty → eleven and forty-four**, with the fifth of March the oldest and staying there. **A pot of air at the yard's own two-mark rate goes out on the nineteenth; a day at the second seam on the *Anna Beth's* own account brings seven in on the twenty-fourth; a day at the second seam on a hirer's own account brings three in on the first of July.** The note is not re-written and its three counts of weeks do not move. **No money came in from any office in this batch and no money went out to any person except three wages, and a shipwright additionally bought a rival crew's printed sheet for four marks out of her own hand, which is not the yard's money and is not in any column.**

### Chapter 121 — The Third Return

Saturday the seventeenth and Sunday the eighteenth of June, in the shed, on the mole's crown, and at the head of the dock. **The third return under the reporting clause is made, on the day, out of a finding of Saturday the tenth, with the day of the finding not counted and the return falling due on the fourth day after it spoken out loud in the entry.** The sheet goes up the coast in a sealed jacket at about eight in the morning with a copy for the harbor authority's ledger in the clerk's own hand; **the boy who walked down to the dock with the clerk in May is not here, and Calder Marr walks him to the steps and stops, and the yard enters the fact and not the feeling.** The return carries the object, the nine scallops, the two seen-times, the mark that is not a seal and not a letter and not a figure, the line of what it is not in full including the eight disavowals, and the age on a line of its own with the reason that the party declines to give a figure and declines to give a reason for declining. **The clerk writes on his own page that a party with no boy in it has put three returns up the coast in six weeks and every one has been on the day except the first, and says for the sixth time that he cannot enter what a foot and a half of glass is.** The tarpaulins are put back on the crown at no figure. **On the Sunday the condition is on the page for the third time in five months as the bare fact — he cannot say in which of two directions a voice came from — in the second column with a reason, no cause, and nobody asked, and Nessa Pike says the reason gets shorter each time and in about a year will be a habit instead of a reason.** The third column is filled with an eight-hour lateness. **Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days, is not asked for anything, asks nothing, and neither brother goes over to him, and the single line that says so goes in the first column where either brother can check it by asking the other.**

### Chapter 122 — Asked a Second Time

Monday the nineteenth and Tuesday the twentieth of June, on the mole's steps, behind the engine house, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare at eight, seven Mondays running, and the cistern is charged on the rota for the fourth time at no figure.** At about eleven a man comes up the hill with a book and a paper **signed at the foot by Veyra Holt, who is not in this harbor and did not come up the hill and is not going to**: a second request for the harbor authority's own chart, in the same terms, with the vessel's undertaking not to contest a claim window of that office on any ground inside the harbor line until the first of March next, **and a date on it, the first of July, which the shipwright makes him read twice.** There is a rider: a request for the place of every copy of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October. **The yard cannot produce the chart and cannot refuse it, and says so in nine sentences on the steps in about nine people's hearing, and says the answer has to be given in a room on the west basin by the reeve with a clerk in it, and says out loud that it is not a party to the paper and is not asking for a date and will not be told before it.** The eleven names are read out in full, four of them named as not reached and not asked, and the yard says it is not going to ask them and entered that it does not believe the officer's promise not to pass the list on. **The wage goes in on the Tuesday, and Nessa Pike enters a second-column line about the party being honest in advance on a day it has no work for.**

### Chapter 123 — The Price in the Room

Wednesday the twenty-first and Thursday the twenty-second of June, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store, and on the mole's steps. **The hearing room is used once. The writ is produced whole with the crew's book and all three returns, unshortened, the clerk holding six things he cannot enter, and the officer asks whether there is a fourth and is told no.** Then the officer's paper goes on the table and Kest says no and then says the rest of it for eleven minutes. **He names what the paper is not first, all three: it is not a claim window, it is not an acknowledgement and not a ninth — both of the year's are spent and there is no ninth and nothing has put a person, a boat or a lead inside the closure since the eighth of June and nothing is going to — and it is not a work permit, a charter or a renewal, and the second season is not this office's to give.** **The price is named before it is paid and it is Elias Voke's: the chart carries, in Kest's own hand of the twenty-fifth of December, a notation that any person proposing to work the bell at the south end is to be given the account of the herring fleet's dues first, and there is no second copy of that notation because the notation is on the chart and the chart is the paper, and giving the chart away puts nineteen winters into a naval file where a reeve can no longer hold them in front of anybody.** **Voke sailed on Tuesday the twentieth and is one day out and does not know, and the price is therefore paid in somebody else's winter, and Kest says so in the room and writes it to Voke in four lines in his own hand and sends it by a boy going up the coast for nothing.** Kest also says what he bought — an uncontested instrument, and a reeve who can no longer find out whether his own instruments work — and **says one sentence and no more about the plate at the fifth seam, which is not reopened.** The clerk reads all of it back; Kest reads it after his own clerk. **Calder Marr asks for one line in his own hand saying the yard was in the room at the officer's request, produced everything, was asked for nothing, offered nothing, entered nothing, and did not know what was on the second sheet.** Nessa Pike's sentence about a reeve who has spent himself eight times and bought something with the ninth goes to the clerk and into the second column. The chart goes into the officer's hand at ten on the Thursday as a paper and not a copy.

### Chapter 124 — The Better Sheet

Friday the twenty-third and Saturday the twenty-foureth of June, in the shed, at the second seam, and in a market. **A crew of Ashtide comes in with a lighter: six men, a compressor of her own, forty fathoms of her own hose — the first delivery hose in this harbor since the twenty-eighth of April — and a master, Perry Ollett, thirty-nine, who is an ordinary working man and is not made a villain and is not given an office, a uniform or a following.** His printed sheet is better than the yard's wall **not because it knows more but because it can be bought**: printed, scaled, dated, priced, with the ground, the hour, the number of times and whose hand against every figure. **At its head are the eleven places the copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October are held, printed from the yard's own slip, which has been nailed to the market wall for fifty-one days for a shilling.** The yard refuses to sell a copy in about nine sentences and **will not ask him to take the slip down**, and says out loud that the slip is the only reason a stranger could print a sheet anybody could write to. **Ollett has found four of the eleven up and down the coast in five weeks; the yard does not ask which four and he does not say, and that not-asking is entered with its reason.** The refusal to name a road is not re-run, and the yard does not pretend a wall under a naval paper is a rival to a printed sheet with a scale on it. **On the Saturday at eleven in the morning the printed sheet goes up on the market wall in daylight, in a market, over the yard's own, and a shilling changes hands at the foot of the dock and is not the yard's income.** In the morning the yard works the second seam on the *Anna Beth's* own account with a hired man in the water, all six conditions read aloud at the boat, the tender a shipwright who has never been in the water, the limit the diver's own, gross sixteen, nine out of it gone and the yard's seven, which is three more than the four the yard wrote on the mole's steps on the thirty-first of May for the same day of work.

### Chapter 125 — Under Two More Pieces of Paper

Sunday the twenty-fifth and Monday the twenty-sixth of June, in a market, on the mole's crown, and in the shed. **Sella Brigg stands in front of a wall with three things on it and asks what the new one is and is told the truth, including that the yard could not have stopped it and that the only way to have stopped it was to take the slip off the wall.** She asks for the price of her own warp and is given the whole of the money of this yard since the eighth of May in a market on wet boards. **She then says she is going to ask a man from Ashtide, and the yard says she has a right to and says the whole of what that costs: the refusal of the seventh of June is the reason a man with a compressor can print a better sheet than the yard can, and in three weeks he will come up this hill and be able to do a job the yard could have done on the tenth of June for four marks, and the only reason he can is that the yard could not, and the yard would have been able to because there was a boy who could hold a line and he is on a herring boat now.** **She then says the one the yard has been not-saying since the fourteenth — that she worked out about the thirty marks herself, in eleven days, from a coat — and the yard neither confirms nor denies and enters that she worked it out and is not going to pretend she did not.** Monday: the wall comes down bare at eight, the cistern is charged for the fifth time in three weeks, **and the yard's own boat comes off the haul-off and goes back into the water at the north end because a boat on a stone crown in a northeasterly takes water from the seaward side, at no figure, a day gone and no money either way.** The third column is filled with a two-hour lateness. **Nessa Pike refuses to say a third time that a reader must be hired and says that a thing said three times in a shed is a complaint and she does not make complaints, she makes entries.**

### Chapter 126 — What the Coast Office Said

Tuesday the twenty-seventh and Wednesday the twenty-eighth of June, in a market, and in the shed. **The wage goes in on the Tuesday and the column goes to ten lines and forty marks.** The *Anna Ray* comes in on the Wednesday after eight days and Elias Voke says, in his own mouth on the boards and not out of a file, what a coast office told him in a room: **first, that the office does not moor a fleet, has no power over a harbor authority, has had this harbor's statement of its own capacity since the first of March and has not answered it and is not going to; second, that it has read the statement and has taken one line out of it — the north berth out of service, the ninth seam's mooring out of service since the first of May with no end entered, and the third lay full — and entered that line in a list of what this coast can take next winter, being made up in the first week of July, with nineteen winters of the *Anna Ray's* dues on the same page and not on the same line.** **The office is not a villain and is not written as one: it asked for a capacity figure, the harbor sent one, and the figure is going to be right in November because the harbor wrote it down itself and gave it away for nothing.** Voke says the hard at Ashtide is unanswered since the ninth of November and that he is not carrying a paper about it a third time, that he is not angry and has said so out loud so that nobody can say he is swallowing it, **and that he was one day out of the room where a reeve sold his nineteen winters and has had two days with it.** He says the letter of four lines is the best thing that office has done for him in nine years and wants it written down in a market. He says he has been told that the chart and the list are for the same office in the same week, and has decided not to find out. **The yard enters the whole of it in the first column because a man said it on a market board in forty people's hearing, and enters under it that the yard published the three lines the office used, and that it is entered as the commonest cause in both cases and is not going to be excused from either.**

### Chapter 127 — A Question About a Bar of Gravel

Thursday the twenty-ninth and Friday the thirtieth of June, in the shed, in a market, and at the head of the dock. **The first paper to come out of a city to this yard since the first of May arrives in the ordinary post, and it is four questions from J. Hesketh, forty-four, a printer of Veyr who prints a yearly coasting return at his own cost and marks what he has seen differently from what he has been given, and who is of no office, holds no seal and has not asked for one.** He has a hand-copied version of the yard's tenth thing from a master of the second lay who copied it at the foot of the yard's own dock in June for nothing, and he wants to know on what ground and how many times, at what hour by whose clock, off what tide and moon and wind, and by whose hand, and whether that hand had been in that water before. **Three of the four are in the yard's own book and are answered. The fourth is not, because the figure is Harl Dace's, given out of a slate before the boat came out, and the yard will not ask him now — the reason being on the wall since the third of May and stated here in full: a figure a yard has asked a man to stand behind is a figure the yard has published, and the day it does that it has made itself the man who takes a road off a chart, in its own hand, on its own wall.** The answer goes up in the ordinary post on the Friday at no cost, **with the reason in the first line and not the last, and the yard's best sentence of the batch in it: a question about a bar of gravel cannot be answered by the people who printed the bar, because the people who printed the bar did not cast the lead.** **On the Friday afternoon Nessa Pike buys the rival's printed sheet for four marks out of her own hand** — not the yard's money, not in any column, not to be paid back, not to be thanked — and puts it on the bench on purpose, and the point of the chapter is the five days: **the sheet went up on Saturday the twenty-fourth and the question came on Thursday the twenty-ninth, and by the time a man in Veyr reads this yard's answer there will already be a better sheet in a market answering him.** She says the rival is wrong about one thing and refuses to say which, and will not let either of them write it down, **because writing a man's name next to a doubt is asking him about himself, and the man with the slate will be right in six weeks and will not know that he was right. **That man is Harl Dace, forty-eight, of the *Grey Widow*, and he has a slate and no bad wheel of his own; the bad wheel in this harbor belongs to the man of fifty-one in the same boat, who is a different man, and the two bad wheels in this harbor are two men and may never be merged.** The cistern is charged for the sixth time and nothing moves in the bag on either day.

### Chapter 128 — The Number in a Room

Saturday the first and Sunday the second of July, on the second seam, in a shed doorway, and in a boat. **A day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, nobody in the water, four casts of the hand lead at the top of the flood: eleven and a half three times on the mark and eleven and three-eighths forty feet south — the same as the second of June, five weeks earlier, in the same hour, and the yard checked it and it held, and the yard enters the agreement as a fact and not as a proof, because eight casts is not four times a year.** In the afternoon Calder Marr puts it to Corvin Sarn **in a doorway, and does not ask the question in it**: the question is for tomorrow morning in Elias Voke's cabin with the door shut and the hatch open. **On the Sunday at ten there are four men in that cabin — Voke at the table, Sarn, the holder, and the shipwright, who is the reason there is a reader — and no office, no seal, no ledger, and the chest not brought down the hill and not opened.** **THE BELLWRIGHT'S ONE QUESTION IS ASKED, IN A ROOM, AND ANSWERED ONCE.** He says it is not a bell number, not a boat number, not a date, and not anything this harbor or Ternwatch has cut, and that he is saying that as a man who could be wrong; that there are more marks in it than figures, that two strokes are joined and one is not; and that he has had it four months and cannot make it be one thing. **No digit is printed and no figure is written in any column, and the undertaking is that this party will not ask him again, entered in the yard's own book, binding the yard and not the office and not the market and not the boy.** He asks for no money and says so, and it is entered that he did not ask. **Voke says that when he is asked in four years what it cost the harbor authority to be honest in public, the answer will be that a man of fifty said a number out loud in his cabin and did not take a shilling, and that he did not have to have a cabin in it.** The reasons the yard does not write it down are given twice, and the second is that a boy of twenty-two wrote on the lid of that chest that there is nothing in it that anybody in this harbor can check, and he was right, and four months have not produced a way round that sentence. **The third column is not where it goes, and a line to that effect is written at the head of it with nothing under it.**

### Chapter 129 — Nine Mondays, and a Wage

Monday the third and Tuesday the fourth of July, on the mole's crown, on the boards, and in the shed. **The wall comes down bare at eight, nine Mondays running, and the cistern is charged for the seventh time and entered on the office's own page at no figure.** **On the crown the mole head's handbell goes and the condition is on the page for the fourth time, and for the first time in five months Calder Marr says it out loud to a person — and Nessa Pike writes it in her own hand and he does not tell her what to write, and she refuses to put either of their names against the line because a page in this book that says what a man of this yard can and cannot hear will be read by somebody in Veyr who is looking for exactly that.** The count of four is to go in the first column on Saturday, because a count is checkable and the second column is not. **Two things come into the harbor's own book this week that the yard had no part in: an end of the first of September is entered against the ninth seam's mooring — the first thing that office has entered in five months a person in this harbor can use — and a wharfinger says in about nine people on the boards that a cistern holding for three weeks is not a repair account, and says it in that order.** The wage goes in on the Tuesday and the column closes at **eleven lines and forty-four marks**; the third column is filled a day and a half late and the party enters that it is the longest it has run and is not going to make a practice of it. The chapter's read-out is the whole twenty-one days accounted for in one column: **four marks in a bag, eight lines becoming eleven, six envelopes on a bench and none of them anywhere, ten marks in and fourteen out, a note at thirty-one, and no office in this harbor that has paid this yard a mark since the eighth of May and none that is going to.**

### Chapter 130 — Four Marks, and What Is Not on a Wall

Wednesday the fifth and Thursday the sixth of July, in a shed, in a market, and at the head of the dock. **The yard does not put a sheet on the market wall, and that is the first decision of the month rather than a day of work, and the reason is said out loud in the street: a sheet is a thing a crew with a compressor and six men puts up, it has a scale and a date and a price and a person to answer a letter about it, and this yard has four marks and a third column that goes in a day late — and, in the shipwright's sentence, the only thing that makes a wall worth reading is somebody else being able to be asked a question about it, and there is nothing in this harbor that could answer. It is the first entry in the yard's book in four months that is about a thing it did not do.** Then a page of four things the party will not permit and one thing it has decided, of which the third is the **channel decided on the page with a date: whatever any office in any city does with any man in any coat, this party has not asked and will find out when it finds out, and if a paper comes up the hill about the second season of the writ of the first of May it will be read and answered on the day it comes and the man who brings it will not be treated as a friend or an enemy — because the writ's own last line says it will arrive out of a city from a man who has never been inside this harbor, and that is the channel, and it has been the channel since the first of May and the yard did not have to invent one and is not going to.** Alden Marr is at the far bench, is not asked for anything, asks nothing, and neither brother goes over to him. **On the Thursday the cistern is charged for the eighth time in three weeks and Ewan Tristram comes down to say the end of it out loud in a market: eight walks on the office's page at no figure, the cistern holding since the nineteenth of June, an end of September on the ninth seam — and a light not on, a north berth out, a third lay full, forty feet of the office's own marline in the sea, dead hose, the *Kittiwake* on the hard, the hard at Ashtide unanswered, nineteen winters in a naval file and in four lines of a letter — and that a cistern that holds is a hole with a lid on it and not a town, said in that order, in a market, and not taken back.** The batch closes with the whole labor spine recited, the three returns, the question from Veyr, the coast office's two sentences, the lead plug, eleven logged occasions and two empty answers neither moved, the delay a hand's width of a second not taken, **and the turn in the yard's own voice: a yard of two and a half men published a bar of gravel and a cairn of ballast stones in a market and in six boats, could not sell a copy of anything, would not put a name on a road, did not go and look at five miles of that coast, and is waiting in the autumn for a man of fifty-four who will say out loud which of two figures is right, with no instrument in this harbor that can call him.**

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0004 (Chapters 131–140) — complete, and reviewed and repaired. Movement 4, what the glass is joined to. THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT.

**A reviewer pass over Chapters 131–140, the four state files and the queued `workspace/volume-03/batch-0005/PROMPT.md` returned FIX with fourteen required repairs and a list of prose and craft items. THE REPAIRS ARE DISPOSED OF IN FULL IN `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0004 review repairs*, WHICH IS THE AUTHORITY FOR THEM. No chapter was restarted, no scene was cut, no cost was dropped and no planned plot was altered. The batch is 30,890 words after the repairs and 30,635 before them.** **The four that mattered most: the Monday the tenth's day account did not add up — the pot of air is struck out of that gross, the day closes at nine out of thirteen and the yard's four, the bag is six and not four, and the false *nothing has come into it since the first of July* is gone, so that the published chain `4, 2, 6, 2, 7, 1, 4, 0` is now what the chapters say; the slate wall's *N Mondays* counter had been restarted at *three* in Chapter 122 and this batch continued it, and the tenth, seventeenth and twenty-fourth of July are the **tenth, eleventh and twelfth** with Chapters 122, 125 and 129 corrected at source and Chapter 129 retitled *Nine Mondays, and a Wage*; the six conditions were not read aloud on the eighth of July, the batch's one dive of the holder's own, and they now are; and the hour of the top of the flood ran backwards and now moves forward — about eleven on the eighth, half past twelve on the fifteenth, twenty past one on the seventeenth, ten past two on the nineteenth, half past two on the twentieth.** Also corrected: the onset of the condition is the fourteenth of October in every chapter that states it and the dive is the thirteenth; the six joint spacings agree with the builder's day-book and the string is three hundred and fifty-one feet; there are three thicknesses of fouling and not five; the conservator has been in this harbor about a fortnight; the market wall's two Monday references are Saturday; a duplicated crown scene spanning Chapters 131 and 132 is re-staged; and the closing read-out is broken up in the two chapters that were long for it.

**Actual word count: 30,916 words across ten chapters as re-measured by the Volume 03 volume audit (`reviews/volume-03/volume-03-close.md`, 27 September 2026), and 30,890 before that audit's eleven mechanical repairs** (Ch131 2,899 · Ch132 3,210 · Ch133 2,403 · Ch134 3,526 · Ch135 2,635 · Ch136 3,347 · Ch137 3,149 · Ch138 3,576 · Ch139 2,317 · Ch140 3,854), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Five are inside the guideline and five are past it** — Chapter 140 by 654, 138 by 376, 134 by 300, 136 by 147, and Chapter 132 by 10 — **and every one of the five that ran ran because a cost could not be dropped: 134 is the fourth return written out in full with its disavowal line and its second column, the clerk on the steps, and the full read-out at the head of the dock; 138 is four books out of a press, a line of three hundred and fifty-one feet on a shed floor, a public disagreement in a market and a nail; 140 is the batch's close and carries the whole labor spine, an office's entry, a price in a market and the forward pull; 136 is the day the seam is read and the wrong is said and both have to breathe; and 132's ten words are the cost of a required arithmetic repair, because the pot of air was struck out of that day's account and a sentence had to be written saying so.** The hearing room is used **not at all** in this batch. The market wall is used **once**, on **Saturday the twenty-second of July**, in daylight, in a market, on no Monday and on no hearing-room day — **and the sheet that goes up on it is the yard's own fourth return, over the rival's printed one, with no scale and no price on it.** The batch's pressures alternate: a market and a figure, labor and a snag, a bench and a debt and a letter, an office and a return, a conservator, a working day and a public error, a disproof and a word in a book, a press and a wall, a sentence and a departure, a close and a forward pull.

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page: NOTHING in a bag, thirty-one on the chandler's note, nothing in a tin, on Wednesday the twenty-sixth of July — against FOUR marks in the same bag on Thursday the sixth.** The chain is **4, 2, 6, 2, 7, 1, 4, 0** across the seven days money moves. Three wage weeks fall in the batch — the eleventh, eighteenth and twenty-fifth of July — and each is four marks out of the bag into an envelope, and the column goes **twelve lines and forty-eight marks – thirteen and fifty-two – fourteen and fifty-six**, with the fifth of March the oldest and staying there, and **nine envelopes on a bench and not one of them anywhere.** Two pots of air at two marks apiece go out. The note is not re-written and its three counts of weeks do not move. **No money came in from any office in this batch and no money went out to any person except three wages and two pots of air.**

### Chapter 131 — A Lead and a Slate, and a Thing Under the Mole

Friday the seventh and Saturday the eighth of July, in a market, in the shed, on the second seam's own face. **Sella Brigg has been to the man from Ashtide and has a figure and a date — twenty-six marks and a day, and the twenty-eighth, and she has not told him about the eighth of March because that is between her and her own rope — and she says out loud on the boards that the yard's own sheet is not on that wall any more and that there is a man from Ashtide over the top of it with four marks at the head of it, and she is not saying it to be cruel.** The pot of air goes out of the bag at ten, two marks, at this yard's own rate, filled at the office's compressor on the office's own hose, and **two marks in a bag.** Saturday: a westerly of four hours, four casts of the hand lead's line at the top of the flood, **eleven and a half on the mark for the third time this summer and it agreed with itself, and ten forty feet south and forty feet down, which is a different bottom and not the same bottom** — and then the lead snags, all at once, with no weight in it, and he works it up by hand for eleven minutes and it comes up on the ring at about twenty past eleven. **A piece of metal about the length of a hand from the wrist to the middle knuckle, not rusted through after a hundred and forty days in four fathoms, with three blows cut on the head of it with a chisel and not stamped with a die, of which the third is a different depth from the first two.** The shipwright says in her own hand that it is not a thing this harbor casts, and a smith is not asked this week. The six conditions are read out in order at the boat with the limit at nineteen set by the diver himself, and **the second column gets the bare fact that he could not for about four minutes tell which of his two hands was holding the line, with a reason and no cause and no name.** At about four in the afternoon Calder Marr walks down past the chandler's to the door of a smith's shed with the piece of metal in his coat, stands in it for the better part of two minutes, does not go in and comes back up the hill, and the reason is on the page: a first-column line says a smith of this harbor has not been asked and is not going to be asked this week, and a man cannot un-ask a thing by standing in a door.

### Chapter 132 — Nine in About Seven Feet

Sunday the ninth and Monday the tenth of July, in the shed, on the crown, and at the second seam. **The two of them do not agree about what the metal is made of and both of them are half right and neither of them knows it, and a person of no office's price — six marks and two days — is put into the book by the shipwright without that person being present.** Sunday they go up on the crown and look at the two tarpaulins. Monday: **the wall behind the reeve's bench comes down bare, ten Mondays, and the cistern is charged for the ninth time at no figure**, and a day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, gross thirteen, the eighth two and the share two and a hired hand three and the victuals one and rope one, that being nine out of thirteen, **with the office's pot of air not charged to the day at all, because it went out of the bag on Friday the seventh and was spent in the water on the Saturday, and two marks in a bag on Monday morning and four more in at about two in the afternoon and six at the close, which is the first money this yard has had in the bag since the first of July.** Both men in the water are hired and paid at the mole by the man who hired the day and neither is of this party. At about half past eleven a lump about the size of two fists comes up on the ring, not stone, not oakum, not lime, not pitch, with a skin on the outside of it a quarter of an inch thick and the inside a different color. **At about ten past four in the afternoon a chip of green glass about the length of a thumbnail comes up in the tallow of the lead, and that is the finding.** Nobody touches it; the ring comes up with it on it and it goes on a cloth and the compound goes on a different cloth. The reason goes in at ten past six with the clerk absent. **The first limb of the clause catches the chip by itself and the party has not needed the second limb.** The disavowal line is in full and includes the lump, the shard, the panel, the plate, the ferry plate, the plate at the fifth seam, the plug, the compartment in three steps, the run under this town, the road, the bar, the cairn, the stem — **and it says the chip is not a mark of nine joined lines and the party is not going to write one.** The clock is named out loud: the day of the finding is not counted and a return falls due on **Friday the fourteenth**.

### Chapter 133 — The Count of Four, and the Figure of a Hand

Tuesday the eleventh and Wednesday the twelfth of July, in the shed and in a market. The wage goes in, four marks, **seven envelopes**, twelve lines and forty-eight marks, and two marks in a bag. **THE FORWARD BEAT CHAPTER 133 OWED IS PAID: at about half past eleven on the Tuesday morning the shipwright writes in the FIRST column, in her own hand, the count of four, with the four dates and the four weathers, and the reason she gives is that the second column is a page of things this yard cannot check and a count is a thing this yard can check, and she refuses to put a name against it for the reason she gave on the third of July and has not improved on it.** And then **she prices a hand at eight marks a week, says in words that she is not going to say the reader thing a third time, says it once in a different sentence that means the same thing, says she is not sorry they put the boy on the stone on the fifteenth of May, and says it is in the first column because she said it in a shed.** Wednesday: **three lines go to J. Hesketh in Veyr in the ordinary post at no cost, telling him a return is coming and that it is not for sale and has no price on it and that if he prints it he is to print what it says and not what it is about** — and Calder Marr says out loud what changed his mind, and it was not a principle, it was that a man with a press who has already written once is the only person in nine hundred miles who is going to be obliged to look at a paper of this yard's. **Edric Pringle comes up the hill, says the cause out loud before the figure, warns them the day she is booking is a day of looking and not a day of work, and says eight shillings and tenpence in the same breath, and says that if the two debts are ever in one sentence she will come back up the hill — and she checks the book on Thursday and it is right.** Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days, is asked nothing, asks nothing, neither brother goes over to him.

### Chapter 134 — The Fourth Return, and a Clerk Who Comes Up the Hill

Thursday the thirteenth and Friday the fourteenth of July, in the shed, on the steps, and at the head of the dock. **THE FOURTH RETURN IS MADE ON THE DAY**, out of a finding of Monday the tenth, the day of the finding not counted and the fourth day after it, one sheet unshortened with place, hour, keeper and limits, a copy for the harbor authority's ledger in the clerk's own hand, and the clerk's own page with the hour it was written and not the hour it was done. **The whole Thursday is spent arguing about one sheet of paper and the argument is about whether to put the word *joint* in it, and the shipwright wins by a better rule: leave it out and put the leaving out, in the second column, where a reason is required.** The disavowal line goes in whole. The clerk comes up the hill at half past seven, does not go to the shed, and says for the seventh time that he cannot enter what a thumbnail of glass is, and writes on his own page that a party with no boy in it has now put four returns up this coast in ten weeks and every one has been on the day except the first. **And then he says the thing nobody had thought of, on the steps, in one go: the vessel has withdrawn its objection to a claim window of this office inside the harbor line until the first of March, so there is nobody to seat, and the only reason that is true is a reeve who sold a herring skipper's nineteen winters for a chart in a room in June, and in four years that will be the only reason anybody could not take the thing out of that mole, and it is a reeve's price and not a party's.** The full read-out runs at the head of the dock with about eleven people in the street.

### Chapter 135 — Six Marks and Two Days, and Six Things She Can Prove

Saturday the fifteenth and Sunday the sixteenth of July, on the mole's steps, in the yard's own boat, and in the shed. **Mara Venn has been in this harbor since about the thirtieth of June, staying above the chandler's, and nobody in this yard knew and nobody asked, and she says so at the top of the steps before anybody has said anything at all.** She prices herself out loud before she does anything, and she puts the conditions of her own finding in words she has chosen — a finding of no office in a yard's own book, unsigned, with a reason on the page, on her own account, not to be paid, not to be thanked, not to be treated as a favor — and she will not say *useful* in a yard's book. She asks one question all day, **what the packing is made of**, and she answers it herself: *I do not know, and I can tell you four things about it and not one of them is what it is made of. Do you know what your weir is made of.* **On the Sunday she gives six findings in the order she finds them convincing and will not give a seventh.** Two ends cut to each other, so a shipwright can check it in ten minutes with a rule. The packing and the fastenings one age. Both went in over three inches of fouling on the seaward side and not on the landward side. The packing came in from the seaward side, with a runnel of it thinning seaward. **Nine fastenings in about seven feet at a hand's breadth apart, three chisel blows each, the third a different depth from the first two, and the same three blows on all nine.** And a chip of worked glass in the packing, the same glass as the chip out of the tallow, put there on purpose, with her reason for it in a conservator's own words. **And what she cannot say is who, and she says it in the trade's own boundary: a joint is made by a body and two hands and a decision, a decision is not a mark, a body does not leave anything on a thing that a trade can read, and anybody in this coast who tells you a conservator can tell you who made a thing is selling you a thing.** She enters it unsigned with the reason on the page. **At the head of the dock on the Sunday evening she makes the shipwright read the line about her forty marks, twice, and puts the figure on it herself: nine months, and she would like the ninth month said out loud in nine people's hearing, because in about two years there is going to be a page in a city that says what a conservator of no office costs a town that owes her money.**

### Chapter 136 — Two Talks at Once, and the Wrong Thing Said at the Head of the Dock

Monday the seventeenth and Tuesday the eighteenth of July, on the mole's face, in the boat, and at the head of this dock. **The wall comes down bare, eleven Mondays, and the cistern is NOT charged, and the reason is on the page in the yard's own hand: the gate takes four hours with three men and the same three men were on the second seam from seven until about two.** **Edric Pringle's day: gross seventeen, the eighth two, the share two, the victuals two, a hired hand three and a second hired hand three at the mole, that being twelve out of seventeen and the yard's being five, two marks less than the same day of work brought on the twenty-fourth of June, and the two figures written side by side on the mole's steps in front of everybody without saying which one it hoped for. Five marks in a bag.** On his second call down, with his hand flat on the cut where the two ends of the joint meet, **he reads a seam for the first time in his life**: not a scarf, not a clamp, but a break where two cuts of different ages have been forced into one pattern, a cut made once and a cut made on top of a cut that was there first. **And then, sitting at four fathoms with his hand on it, he does the thing that takes the rest of his day: he makes the old mending into a fact — not the mending, the age of it, the number of mendings, and the hand that made them — and he supplies all three, and the hand on the wood told him none of them.** He is in the water four more times, seventeen minutes each, the limit is his and he keeps it four times out of four, and he loses the count of the boat: the shipwright says four, he says three, twice. **The boat comes off the face at eleven, two hours and a quarter before the flood, and the day is charged whole at seventeen with the crevice half cleared, and there is one top of the flood a day and there are two more floods.** Tuesday: the wage, four marks, **thirteen lines and fifty-two marks, eight envelopes**; a second pot of air out of the bag; the six read out in order again in the same words because a man who reads his conditions differently on Tuesday than on Monday has a reason for it and does not know what it is; **the first two fouling readings, the two at the root end, a quarter of an inch each**; and at about half past three in the afternoon, at the head of this dock, in about eleven people's hearing, **he says the wrong thing: that the six joints are a chain, that they were made once, in one time, by one set of hands, from the north end down, and that whatever it is for it is not to keep two boats off the bottom in a blow.** **The shipwright does not stop him, and enters the reason she did not, in the second column: a man saying his own trade in the open air is not doing wrong, he has been right in public four times this month, and a shipwright who contradicts a man in a market is a person who has decided that a man's mouth is a thing to be managed.** And **Tam Slee of the *Grey Widow*, the fourth name on the writ, not hired, not paid, not of this party, says in the boat that he went down because he was told to and is not doing it because of Tuesday, and asks that the difference be kept as a difference.**

### Chapter 137 — What the Fouling Took Apart, and a Word in the Book
