Wednesday the nineteenth and Thursday the twentieth of July, on the second seam's face, on the boards, at the foot of the dock, and in the shed. **The six are read out in order at the boat before anybody is wet on all three of the days, in the same words, with the clerk's absence entered three times.** Wednesday, on the top of the flood: **the third joint reads three inches and a quarter and the fourth reads three inches and a quarter, and the two thin ones are the two at the root end, and the holder said on Tuesday at half past three in a market that the newest was the first — and it is the last, and he got it backwards, and he says so on the mole in about nine words and asks for it in the book in the words he is about to use: fouling goes on in order, a quarter of an inch is a fortnight and three inches and a quarter is a season, and a quarter and three inches and a quarter is not a morning but at least three different days in three different seasons.** A boatwright of the second lay, forty-one years in the harbor, comes down for nothing because somebody he never met asked him, and confirms the nine fastenings and the spacing with his own rule and says the spacing is not a spacing anybody on this coast works to and that a man who says a hand's breadth is telling you about two men with two hands, and that men who lay out by hand are men working in a hurry or where there is no batten — **and says he has not said a word about where it came from and does not know who, and the yard enters all of it in the first column because a man said it in a shed and has given his name.** **Edric Pringle comes up on the Wednesday evening and says the cause out loud: the tide was there at seven and at two, the boat came off the face at eleven because the man in the water could not say whether the basket had gone up four times or three, and he is of the yard — and she will not be told it was the tide, and she was told a thing about the job and not about her money, and she is owed eight shillings and tenpence and is not going to ask, and the two debts are in no sentence together.** Thursday: the cistern is charged for the eleventh time, at no figure, and the entry gives the hour of the flood against the yard's own account. **The fifth joint reads nine inches and is the fifth of the six and the second from the seaward end, and the packing in the fifth is a different color to a depth of a quarter of an inch with the same color under it.** And at about a quarter past nine that night, in the shed, with a man of the *Grey Widow* awake on a net loft floor and nobody pretending he was asleep, **THE ONE NAMING VOLUME 03 IS ALLOWED IS SPENT, IN THIS CHAPTER, IN THE YARD'S OWN BOOK, IN HIS OWN LEFT HAND, IN THE SECOND COLUMN, WITH A REASON.** He says the word *a tide-sickness*, says he got it from and that he produced the word himself, out loud, in the *Grey Widow's* cockpit on the thirteenth of May, says it is not a cause and no cause is written, and says he will not be asked to take it out and will not fight anybody who wants it out — **and the reason he breaks his own line of the second of June, which says there is no name in it and there is not going to be one this year, is in the reason against the line: a man who can lose a quarter of a second out of an ordinary conversation in a shed is a man whose account of a seam under four fathoms is a thing that can be asked about, and the party has decided it can be asked about in this book, on this page, on this date, by anybody who comes up this hill, and the only difference between that and a man with a seal is that it can be argued with.** The shipwright reads the whole of it back and does not improve on it, and the count of the four lines above it is still four and they have no name in them and are not being struck out.

### Chapter 138 — Nine Places, and a Sheet of Paper With No Price On It

Friday the twenty-first and Saturday the twenty-second of July, in the market office press, on the mole's face, on the shed floor, and in a market. A day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, nobody in the water, gross fifteen, the eighth two, the share two, two hired hands at three apiece, the victuals one and rope one, that being twelve out of fifteen, **four marks in a bag, and it is the last money this yard has coming in before the twenty-eighth.** **The market office keeps four books in a press under a stone slab and unlocks it on a Tuesday and a Friday morning while the counting is going on, and the party has never once been in it in five months.** **A builder's day-book of the port of Ashtide, in that press since a year nobody names, with six places in six entries — forty feet, and seventy-three feet, and one hundred and forty-one feet, and two hundred and ten feet, and two hundred and seventy-nine feet, and three hundred and forty-eight feet, each at the second seam — and the six places are the six joints, and the builder's name is cut into a stone at the mole head, and the last entry says that in thirty-one years he was never once asked what the joints at the foot are for and he is not asking.** **A weir-book with nine pages in a fisher's own hand and a night in a westerly and eleven hundred and forty fish and a lost boat and a repair four years later, and the third joint is a foot and four inches below that weir's own sill stone, and the repair is signed by the man whose grandfather built the weir.** **A poor-book with one line about twenty-two children put out of a village and nine of them named, and the place is not on this coast and is not on any chart anybody in this harbor has seen.** **A tide-gang's step book whose column for the foot of this mole's face stops, and after it the name of a sand and shell lay of this coast that is not on any chart in this harbor, and the party has entered that it did not match it and is not going to guess.** And a string laid on the shed floor with two pins in it: **the six joints stand in a straight line, three hundred and fifty-one feet, with nothing in the string's way, and a man can walk it in about eleven minutes.** **And the party declines the word: nine places is nine places, a line is a line, and a builder who wrote down where he worked because he was paid by the foot is not a man leaving a message — and the word *chain* is disowned, because it was said in a market on the Tuesday by a man who was wrong in three parts out of four of the same sentence.** The second column says, in full, that the party will not join the two halves: **the account is about the sea, the finding is about the glass, and neither of them is a reason to believe the other.** **On Saturday at eleven, in daylight, in a market, the yard nails its own fourth return up on the market wall, over the rival's printed sheet, with no scale and no price and nowhere to write a letter to, and a shilling changes hands at the foot of the dock for the printed sheet and is not this yard's income. Perry Ollett reads it three times, says it is the only paper on that wall anybody in a city is obliged to look at, and says he will be standing there on Monday with a better sheet and is not going to pretend he would not be. And at ten past twelve, in the open air, in front of about forty people, Mara Venn says out loud that Calder Marr is wrong, and that the yard has made it public and cannot un-print it, and that she does not think he is wrong and that she thinks he is a man who has run out of money and a man who has not — and her objection goes onto the sheet on the same Saturday, on the same nail, unsigned at her own request, with the reason, in her own hand.** His answer, in fifteen words, in the same market: **both, and I will not be able to tell you which was right in October.**

### Chapter 139 — What She Said Before Anybody Asked, and the Calipers She Took With Her

Sunday the twenty-third and Monday the twenty-fourth of July, at the top of the mole's steps, at the market office door, and in the shed. **Mara Venn says the thing that ends her time on this crew, in her own words, before anybody asks her for it, on the steps, in the wind, in nine people's hearing, and she has written it herself the night before and hands it over.** Her sentence: **a yard that cannot pay me asks me whether I can be useful; I have been useful four times since the twenty-seventh of January, not asked once, not paid once, and the fifth time I was not asked and I was not paid and I did the work, and I am not going to do that again; six marks and two days is my price and it does not come down, and I would rather be owed at the rate I am owed than be had at the rate I am had, and I have been had since January and I did not know that was the word for it until this morning; and a trade that says yes to nothing is not a trade.** It goes in the first column because she said it out loud, and nobody disputes a word of it. **Then he tells her all of it — the day-book, the weir, the poor-book, the step book, the nine places, the string — and she calls it the best thing anybody has found out in this harbor in five months, and then says it is worth nothing to her: not because it is thin, but because she is not being paid for it, and a thing a person is given because she is owed money is not a gift and not a trade and not a finding, and she will not be it twice again.** **So the disagreement is not settled by him giving her the evidence, and it is not repaired, and the party enters it whole, and neither half was settled by anything anybody handed to anybody.** And he says the yard's half out loud, and it is the sentence the movement is owed: **a conservator I cannot pay is a conservator I have to keep asking, and asking her to be useful is asking her to be cheap, and I have been doing the second thing since the twenty-seventh of January and calling it the first.** Monday: the wall comes down bare, twelve Mondays; the cistern is charged for the twelfth time; **she is not on the mole and not in the shed and the brass calipers are gone off the bench, hers, bought out of her own apprenticeship money in her second year, and she was not parted from them for a boat and was not parted from them on the Monday.** The entry says the party will keep asking her and will not ask her to be useful, and that those are two different things, and that her finding of the fifteenth and sixteenth stands unsigned and can be used and cannot be checked and cannot be paid for and **cannot be got done again.** **And she is at the market office door on the Monday afternoon with her book under her arm and does not come up the hill, and the standing arrangement is entered: a conservator of no office in Morrow Quay is to be found on a Friday morning at about ten in a room that is open to anybody, and the party did not arrange it and would not dare to.**

### Chapter 140 — Four Days of the Third Lay, and Nothing in a Bag

Tuesday the twenty-fifth and Wednesday the twenty-sixth of July, in the shed, in a market, on the mole's steps, and at the head of the dock. **The wage goes in at about eleven, four marks, and the bag is nothing, and the party enters the figure and the reason in the first column, and the reason is not the reason the bag stood at nothing for five days in April: in April this yard was living on a creditor's page and in July it is living on a wage, and a wage is not a page, it is a man's four marks for six days' work he has not been told about and that has not been carried to him, and there is no man in this harbor who can be asked about it because he is on a herring boat. Fourteen lines and fifty-six marks, the fifth of March the oldest, nine envelopes on a bench and not one of them anywhere, and the next of them is four marks on Tuesday the first of August.** Alden Marr is at the far bench, is asked nothing, asks nothing, neither brother goes over to him. **On the Wednesday the reeve enters, in his own book and at his own cost, that the footing of the mole from the third seam to the twelfth is to be examined on the second of August and the moorings of the third lay are to be closed for the four days of it — and the repair account of that office has been empty since the middle of February and the money is not coming and the entry is made nevertheless, and the reason is on the face of it: an office whose instruments nobody contests can enter an instrument with nothing behind it, and that is worse than no instrument.** **The price lands on the third lay and a man of the third lay says the number out loud in a market in about forty people: four days, the second to the fifth of August, the tide right on the second and the third and wrong on the fourth and worse on the fifth, the north berth out, the third lay full, and the rest of it in the order that matters — a cistern that holds and a mole's footing that is going to be looked at, one of them a lid and one of them a hole, and he said the word *town* in a market on the sixth of July and is not going to say it again, and is not angry, and is not asking anybody for anything.** **And Mara Venn, on the boards, at no figure, says the one thing none of the others in that market can say: four days with a lay shut is four days with a hull's bottom out of the water and nobody on it, and if the weather comes in on the fifth of August there is a boat in this harbor with four days of fouling on her bottom and nobody able to scrub her, and that happened here twice in the year before she was born, and she knows it because the men who were in it are still standing in that market.** And at about four in the afternoon the holder asks the reeve, on the steps, in nine people's hearing, whether any of it is about the plate at the fifth seam, **and the answer is thirteen words: it is not and I will not tell you what it is about** — and the party enters both halves and does not know what the entry is about and has not asked. The full read-out runs at six with the whole labor spine, **dated lines for the seventeenth, eighteenth, nineteenth, twentieth and twenty-fourth of July saying that no finding of worked glass was made and nothing was raised and no return goes up on any of those days, and there is no fifth return and there is not going to be one out of a day that produced nothing**, the third column's two entries and the party's refusal to make a trend of them, and the autumn visit of a man of fifty-four with a figure of his own for the bar and no instrument in this harbor that can call him. **And at half past six a man of the third lay says a boat's name out loud in the street: there is a working boat with people in her lying off the outer marks, the tide gang has walked past her every day since the ninth of July and has been told nothing, her name is the *Hearthwake*, and a man of a boat of his says she is one of the nine, and he has said her name out loud in a market because if a man in this harbor has a boat off the outer marks with people in her then a man in this harbor should be the one who says it and not a man in a city.** **And the yard writes it down where anybody can check it, which is a page in a book in a shed on a hill, and does not go and look, and does not send anybody, and does not ask that man one question about her, and says out loud that the outer marks stand where the writ of the first of May says they are and that the party is not going past them this season and has not tried and is not going to — and that is not nothing, and it is not enough, and both of those are on the page.**

---

## Volume 04, Batch 0001 (Chapters 151–160) — complete. Movement 1, the notice and the seat. **THE BATCH MIDPOINT AND THE BATCH CLIMAX ARE PAID.**

**REVIEWED AND REPAIRED, 27 SEPTEMBER 2026. A reviewer pass over the ten chapters, the card file, `outline/volume-04.md`, the queued `workspace/volume-04/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` and the four state files returned FIX with thirteen required repairs, six instances of meta prose, a list of prose and craft items and five state-file findings. ALL ARE APPLIED IN PLACE. NO CHAPTER WAS RESTARTED, NO SCENE WAS CUT FOR LENGTH, NO COST WAS DROPPED AND NO PLANNED PLOT WAS ALTERED, and the full disposition is `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0001 (Volume 04) review repairs*, which is the authority for them. The four that would have cost the next writer most: **THE MONEY CHAIN DID NOT CLOSE UNDER THE BOOK'S OWN CURRENCY** — a mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, so a shilling out of one mark leaves twelve shillings and fourpence and not nineteen pence, and every figure downstream of that one step was wrong in five chapters, in this file, in `state/continuity.md`, in the card file, in `outline/volume-04.md` and in the queued prompt; all of them are corrected at source with the reason on their face. **THE POSTAGE OF THE TWENTIETH OF AUGUST WAS PAID OUT OF A BAG THAT HELD NOTHING, IN THREE DIFFERENT FIGURES**; it is now seven pence and it is the shipwright's own money out of her own pocket, owed to her and unpaid, which turns an impossibility into the batch's first cost that falls on a member of this crew. **THE ENVELOPE COUNT NEVER ADVANCED AND ONE OF THE ELEVEN WAS SAID TO HAVE NEVER BEEN MADE**; there are twelve on the bench on the twenty-second of August and thirteen on the first of September, and one week fallen due this year has no envelope standing against it. **CHAPTER 156 CLOSED THE PLATE NOTE FORTY-FIVE CHAPTERS EARLY** by asserting a removed file page and an office's document, which are reserved to the volume's close and to Movement 3; the note is now open and the reserved beat is back where the outline keeps it. Also corrected: the four wrong day-counts and the doubled *fourth Monday this month*; the market wall's paper count, which said four and enumerated three; the vigil outside the market office, which said a hundred and forty minutes where the card and the entry say eighty; the head of the third column, which Chapter 159 gave as the first; the span of the copy leaving the harbor, which was *four months* in one line and *four volumes* in another, and the book's age, which is forty-seven weeks and not fifty-one; **Tam Rundle, who gave nine days of his own watching on the twenty-ninth of July and was brought in on the first of September as a stranger;** six instances of the author talking about the book inside the book, including one in Calder Marr's own mouth and a statement about the condition being unnamed in a world where it is unnamed; a twenty-word clause repeated between Chapters 151 and 159; an aphorism said three times in two chapters; the fourth return's date, which conflated the day it was made with the day it went up; and the overclaim below about the read-out, which denied the batch's dominant shape.**

**Actual word count: 31,731 words across ten chapters as re-measured after this batch's review repairs, and 31,019 before them** (Ch151 3,074 · Ch152 3,582 · Ch153 2,690 · Ch154 2,806 · Ch155 4,666 · Ch156 3,164 · Ch157 2,669 · Ch158 2,995 · Ch159 2,932 · Ch160 3,153), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Seven are inside the guideline and three are past it — Chapter 152 by 382 and Chapter 155 by 1,466 — and Chapter 155 is one complete market office in the morning and one complete market wall at about eleven with the read-out in the middle of it, which is the one place in this book where a figure is worth anything and the Volume 03 audit's ruling is that it is allowed to run long. ITS SHEET WAS COMPRESSED WHERE IT DUPLICATED ITS OWN FRAME AND IT IS LEFT AT 4,666 WORDS ON THE RECORD RATHER THAN EXCUSED, and the next batch inherits the figure and not a defence of it.** The hearing room is used **once**, on **Saturday the ninth of September**. The market wall is published on **twice**, on **Wednesday the sixteenth** and **Friday the twenty-fifth of August**. **The two are never the same day and this is verified twice in the batch. The market office is used on Friday the twenty-fifth and Friday the eighth, and is neither of the other two rooms.**

**THE READ-OUT IS THE DEVICE AND IT IS USED ONCE IN THIS BATCH, and the two-column head-of-dock read-out is NOT used in it at all — and the two things this state file used to call one thing are now separated, because a reviewer pass showed the sentence was denying the batch's dominant shape.** The read-out — a man reading figures out of the yard's own book aloud to people who can check them — happens on Friday the twenty-fifth at about eleven in the morning at a market wall in about forty people's hearing: the holder reads a **sheet** of his own out twice, gets one of the six builder's figures wrong the second time, corrects himself off the sheet in his hand without going back to the top in about a second and a half, and nobody says anything about it, and the yard enters it against himself in the second column with a reason and without a name of any kind in it. **THE READ-BACK IS NOT THE READ-OUT.** The ship's hand reading an entry back and the holder reading it back after her, nobody else in the shed, at nine at night, is this yard's standing practice since the second of May and it runs in **eight of the ten chapters — 151, 152, 153, 155, 156, 158, 159 and 160** — and Chapter 157 opens with a read-out of the yard's own book in a shed. **None of that is a use of the device and none of it spends it; a later batch may use the read-out once, as this one did, and may use the read-back on any night it likes.**

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page, and it was WRONG AND IS CORRECTED IN PLACE: NOTHING in a bag on the morning of Wednesday the sixteenth of August, and A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE in it on the evening of Sunday the tenth of September. A mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, and a shilling out of one mark leaves a hundred and forty-eight pence and not nineteen of them, which is the one step the whole chain hung on.** The chain across the days money moves is **nothing · nothing · nothing · 5 · 1 · 1 · 12s 4d · 5m 12s 4d · 1m 12s 4d**: seven pence of postage on the letter of Sunday the twentieth, **out of the shipwright's own pocket and not out of the bag, because the bag held nothing, and owed by this yard to her and unpaid**; a day at the second seam's own face on a hirer's own account on Monday the twenty-first, gross seventeen and the yard's five, and the wage of four out of it on Tuesday the twenty-second, which leaves one; a shilling at the market office press on Friday the twenty-fifth for forty copies, which leaves twelve shillings and fourpence; a day on the mole's toe inside the first clause on Monday the twenty-eighth, gross seventeen and the yard's five, which makes five marks and twelve shillings and fourpence; **the wage of four out of that on Tuesday the twenty-ninth, which is a line in the yard's own book and not a scene and is read out as a figure in Chapter 157**; and nothing after that. **The whole of the money in since the fifteenth of August is ten marks and the whole of the money out is eight marks and a shilling, and the seven pence is in neither figure because it was never the yard's.** **The wage column is seventeen lines and sixty-eight marks after the twenty-second of August and eighteen lines and seventy-two marks after the twenty-ninth, with the fifth of March the oldest and staying there. THIRTEEN ENVELOPES ON A BENCH AND ONE WEEK FALLEN DUE THIS YEAR WITH NO ENVELOPE STANDING AGAINST IT, being the week of Tuesday the fifteenth of August, and those are two different figures and neither of them is the other.** **No wage was paid twice, no wage was paid on a day that is not a Tuesday, and the three marks owing to a man of Ashtide have not moved a single mark since the eighth of August and were named once in this volume, in Chapter 153.**

### Chapter 151 — The Two That Came Down

Wednesday the sixteenth and Thursday the seventeenth of August, at the market wall, on the boards, and in the shed. **THE WALL IS BARE AT ABOUT EIGHT IN THE MORNING AND IT HAD NOT BEEN BARE AT FIVE, and three sheets are gone — the *Vigil*'s notice of the twenty-eighth of July, the longer notice of the fourth of August with *the said vessel and every part of her contents being thereby Crown material* in it, and THIS YARD'S OWN SHEET OF THE FIFTEENTH OF AUGUST — and they went into a boat, and the copy in a city is at a press that cannot be stopped.** Underneath, still on the nails, are the *Sallow Reach's* printed sheet, a sheet of the seventh of June, this yard's tenth thing, and **this yard's own fourth return, made on Friday the fourteenth of July and put up on those nails on the twenty-second, which is the only paper on that wall any office in any city is obliged to look at and which is still on those nails and can be read from the side of it in the right wind.** **A man of the third lay, Danno Fisk, thirty-eight, thirty-one years in this harbor, saw a boat lying in against the wall at about seven in the morning with a man in a coat standing on her boards doing nothing and a second man in the bottom of her whose face he did not see, and a barge out of the naval pile two hours later, and three sheets put into her in a bundle with the third one under the other two so it went in wet. He gave his name and the hour and gave a quarter of an hour instead of a minute because he had no watch on his person, and he was not asked for a face and did not give one.** **The yard does not ask the harbor authority to do anything and enters why in the first column on the Wednesday night** — an instrument of that office which would put a boat of that office to sea carries the reeve's acknowledgement at the reeve's cost, there are eight of those, both of this year's are spent, there is no ninth, and this party entered twice in July that it did not ask for one and is not going to — **and it does not go to the man in a coat, because a man in a coat is a channel and the channel is the writ and the writ is about the second season and this is not about the second season.** The clerk of that office comes up the hill unasked and says what a box holds: his page has a line for a permit, a line for a claim window and a line for an acknowledgement, and it does not have a line in it for a sheet going off a wall, because a set of nails is not in any book in this harbor and never has been. **And at about five in the evening the two of them count the bare nails and find three more sheets gone inside about nine hours of the same two days, on a day the yard does not know, and has entered that it does not know and is not going to ask.** The shipwright asks, in her own trade's voice, whether a sheet coming off a wall is a thing a man did or a thing an office did; he says he does not know; she writes that down and neither of them improves on it. No money moves.

### Chapter 152 — A Form That Does Not Mention the Second Season

Saturday the nineteenth and Sunday the twentieth of August, in the shed, on the mole's crown, and at the head of the dock. **AMBROSE SELL, A CLERK OF THE CIRCUIT OFFICE AND THE MOUTH OF A CLAUSE AND NOT AN ANTAGONIST, COMES UP THE HILL AT ABOUT TEN ON THE SATURDAY MORNING WITH A FORM IN HIS OWN HAND AND READS IT OUT AT THE TOP OF HIS VOICE IN THE YARD AND NOT IN THE SHED, and gives his name and the name of his office before reading it.** The form is four lines: a formal inquiry is opened into certain matters touching the working of a salvage concern at Morrow Quay between the first of May and the fifteenth of August; the parties named are four and are those on the writ of the first of May; the party is required to produce its crew's book and the returns made under a reporting clause **at a sitting to be appointed**; and **nothing in it touches, grants, refuses or deals with the second season of the writ.** He states that he has read it four times on a walk and there is nothing behind those four words, and that the last line of the yard's own writ says the holder will be told when the second season has been decided and not before, and a form that said it had been decided either way would be telling him in a way his writ forbids anybody to tell him in. **The yard does not refuse the form and does not sign anything on the step, and asks for one thing and gets it: the room, which is the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin, and the truth about the day, which is that there is no day, that he does not know and is not going to guess at them about it because a man who is given a guess writes the guess down, and that when a day is appointed they will get a paper naming it, served on them and not left with anybody, and that there is in that paper a power to seat one of two disputants and take such finding as is given, which was not in the four lines he brought up the hill and which this party has not seen.** **In a doorway, unasked, and this is the reason: a yard that has put a sheet of its own figures on a public wall in a working harbor has told the office that keeps the page that those figures are checkable, and an office cannot un-ask a question that a party has answered in public.** He says he is the eighth time this year he has said a thing like the thing he cannot enter, the seventh having been on the mole's steps on the twenty-fifth of July. **In the shed on the Sunday the shipwright wins the only argument of the week in nine words — *a column is not a defense of anything and was never one* — because a man with a pen in a room writes down the lines and not the reasons, and reasons are long.** Then the two of them go up onto the mole's crown and look at the two objects of nobody's without touching them, and she says write *ninety-five* and write that we did not move them. **The counter is carried day by day from Volume 03's ninety days on Saturday the fifteenth of August: ninety-five on the twentieth, ninety-nine on the twenty-fourth and a hundred and three on the twenty-eighth, and the yard does not let it stand still across five days.** **And at about seven on Sunday night the holder writes to a circuit office in a city and posts the letter in the ordinary post with seven pence of postage that is the shipwright's own money out of her own pocket, because the bag holds nothing, and owes it to her unpaid with the figure and the date and the reason against the line in the first column in her name, asking for a copy of a record: the record of this yard's own fourth return of the fourteenth of July, of which a copy went into the harbor authority's ledger on that Friday morning in a clerk's own hand and a third copy went into a file in a city the same morning. The date is on the envelope as well as in the letter, because a thing that has not been answered in a month is going to be a thing somebody says was never asked.** No money moves except the postage.

### Chapter 153 — Three Marks, a Wage, and a Third Lay That Wants a Monday

Monday the twenty-first and Tuesday the twenty-second of August, at the second seam's own face, in a doorway, and in the shed. **THE WALL BEHIND THE REEVE'S BENCH CAME DOWN BARE AND THAT WAS SIXTEEN MONDAYS SINCE THE FIRST OF MAY, and this party has not put anything on that wall in five months and is not going to start on the sixteenth.** A woman of the third lay books a day and says before she says anything else that she will pay twelve of seventeen at the mole the way she always has. **Gross seventeen; the eighth two, the share two, the victuals two, a hired hand three and a second hired hand three, that being twelve out of seventeen and the yard's being five. Nobody in the water and the pot of air is not charged to the day, and a hand lead's line went over the side four times and did not go past the outer marks, and the reason is on the page: a hand lead's line is not what anybody wanted past the outer marks.** The five goes up on the mole's steps in daylight in front of about eleven people beside the seven the same day of work brought on the twenty-fourth of June, and **nobody is told which figure the yard would rather have had and it is not going to pretend.** At about half past two the yard asks the clerk, in a doorway, as a matter of fact, whether anything stands under the end entered against the ninth seam's mooring on the third of July, and **the clerk answers that an end of it entered is an end of it entered, that this office entered it, that this office has nothing under it, that the repair account has been empty since the middle of February and is not going to be filled this year, and that a man in a boat can check a mooring and he is not a boat.** **On Tuesday the twenty-second the wage of four goes into an envelope in the holder's own left hand, and the column is seventeen lines and sixty-eight marks with the fifth of March the oldest, and there is one mark in the bag.** Then the shipwright counts the envelopes on the bench — **twelve of them** — makes him count them once, and establishes that **one of the last three was never made, being the week of Tuesday the fifteenth of August, and that the count on the bench and the number of weeks fallen due with nothing against them are two different figures and neither of them is the other, and that a week with no envelope on it is not a week that was not paid for but a week that was not written down, and that the reason both go in the second column together is that a yard which is asked how many weeks it has paid ought to be made to say both numbers in the same breath rather than the one that is easier, and that an envelope that is not made is not a wage and this column is not the wage, because a piece of paper with a week on it is a thing the yard does so as to know at the end of a year which weeks it did, and it is not a receipt and nobody in this harbor has ever received one.** **PERRY OLETT, MASTER OF THE *SALLOW REACH*, A CREW OF ASHTIDE, IS NAMED IN THIS CHAPTER AND IN NO OTHER CHAPTER OF THIS BATCH, and only in the first-column entry.** The three marks are not paid and the reason is on the page with the figure and the date: the bag held one mark after the wage had gone into the envelope, a debt is a debt, a man is not paid a shilling of it and told it is a payment, he said six and was paid three and both of those are the arrangement and the arrangement is his, the yard will pay him the rest in one figure and not in three instalments of one, and the reason it is in the first column and not the second is that it is a figure and not a feeling and anybody in this harbor can check it. **Nobody was thanked, nobody was written to, nobody is going up that hill to see him, and the reason is that the last time this yard put a man's figure in a book and then went and asked him about it, it got a page with a limit on it and a man saying *I have thought about it for five days*.**

### Chapter 154 — The Pilot Comes Back Late, and the Yard Did Not Go and Look

Wednesday the twenty-third and Thursday the twenty-fourth of August, on the mole's steps, on the crown, and in the shed. **ROWAN TREWIN COMES IN ON THE FLOOD AT ABOUT A QUARTER PAST NINE WITH A SHEET WEIGHTED WITH TWO STONES AND THE STONES STAY ON IT, nineteen years keeping a channel nine miles off this coast by hand with two other men, in a boat of about forty feet with a leaded keel and no harbor on her anywhere, and he says he did not come for the bar and he came for five miles of coast.** He read the sheet of the fifteenth at Ninefoot on the seventh in a cabin with a lamp and read it twice, and he says out loud the thing this yard had published and not said: **a page that carries the place of every copy of itself is a page that tells a man where the copies are; he has been in nine of those boats and has asked for a copy in none of them and is not going to; and the reason he can say the next thing and this party cannot is that no authority in this harbor can bring him to stand in front of this party's wall, and he would not want to be brought.** He asks for his two and a half to be put beside the yard's two and three-quarters **on a wall and not in a column and not in a book, because a book is the yard's and a wall is the yard's and a press is a man's.** **The yard says no to the other half out loud, in the open air, and gives the reason — that the only room this party can be got into in the next three weeks is a room somebody else has arranged and is holding a form about a book in, and a thing said in such a room is said at a time somebody else chose — and says it is not going to pretend it did not choose a market, and writes all of that in the first column with the date.** **The yard also says out loud, in front of a man who came a long way for it, that it has not gone and looked at five miles of that coast, was not asked to, and is not going to, and gives the working reason: a yard of two and a half people that has spent a fortnight being asked for a book by an office does not have three days for five miles of a coast it published in June, and is not going to pretend the two are unrelated.** The block goes up in nineteen words and the pilot says eighteen and the yard says it is not going to argue about it with a man who has been counting tides for nineteen years. **He is not thanked, has not asked to be, said so in advance, and asked for that to be written down with the date and not on a wall.** Three hours of a boat's time were given and not paid for and not asked for, and that is entered as a gift and not a debt, and the reason a yard that has been given a thing can be given a thing. No money moves and no air is bought.

### Chapter 155 — The Sheet Under the Two That Came Down

Friday the twenty-fifth and Saturday the twenty-sixth of August, in the market office and at its press in the morning, at the market wall at about eleven, and at the head of the dock. **THE BATCH MIDPOINT.** The press is the market office's own, at the back of its counting floor under a slab of stone, unlocked on a Tuesday and a Friday morning while the counting goes on, and the woman who shifts the slab has worked in that room for nineteen years and her mother shifted it before her, and a shilling is what the press charges for a sheet a man owns. **A man in a coat is standing at the end of the long table in a room open to anybody, and this party did not arrange it and did not ask, and he says three things and then says *go and put your sheet up* and is gone before the slab is back.** He tells the yard that its request of the twentieth is in his office's possession and has not been given and is not going to be; **that a provisional public copy record exists in a city, a copy of a record about an object, with the provenance of the record, its limits, and an explicit statement of what it does not show printed on the face of the copy itself, which the yard may ask for a copy of at its own cost, and that it is not the object and the object is not going to be;** and two things the yard can check — **that the request of the twentieth is entered and the copy is not made and not released and no date attaches to either, and that if the copy came to this room it would not be a thing anybody in Morrow Quay could check, because the paper it would be copied from is not in this harbor and a man here cannot check a page he has never seen the original of.** **THE COPY IS NOT OBTAINED, NOT MADE, NOT RELEASED, NOT PRINTED AND NOT PUT ON A WALL, and the yard has a request with a date on it and a refusal with no date on it, and a request is a thing a party can publish.** The yard pays a shilling for forty copies — **the third shilling this book has moved that was not this yard's income, and all three are entered with their dates and none of them is income** — and there is **twelve shillings and fourpence in a bag, and the yard says that figure out loud on the boards, because a mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve and it is the only arithmetic it has ever had to say out loud.** The sheet goes up on the same nails, on the outside of whatever is there, and **what is there is this yard's own fourth return, made on Friday the fourteenth of July and put up on those nails on the twenty-second, still on the wall and still readable from the side of it in the right wind, and the four papers that were standing on top of it when the new sheet went up are named, and one of those four came off in one morning on the sixteenth and the other three came off later the same afternoon.** It carries the place of every copy of itself; six figures from a builder's own day-book with a day against each and no year; **the two figures for the bar of gravel side by side in nineteen words, and the sentence that a road nobody else can use is not a road and a road everybody can use is not this yard's, and the statement that this yard has not looked at five miles of that coast**; the fact that a case of five bells is at about five and a half fathoms inside the four hundred yards and is not on the sheet and is not for sale and went back into the water out of a man's hands on the eighth; **what the yard asked for on the twentieth and has not been given, the date, and that a copy of a record is not a thing anybody in this harbor can check, and THIS YARD IS NOT PRINTING AN ANSWER IT DOES NOT HAVE**; **and why the sheet is on a wall and not in a room, on its own face, in the words that a man of fifty-four said it should be said in a room and this party has heard him and does not agree with him and is not taking the sentence down.** **And the holder reads the whole of it out twice in about forty people's hearing, and on the second reading he says the sixth of the six builder's figures at two hundred and seventy-seven feet, and it is two hundred and seventy-nine, and he corrects himself off the sheet in his own hand without going back to the top, and the whole of it takes about a second and a half, and nobody in that market says anything about it.** The shipwright says out loud, four minutes later, in about forty people's hearing, that they are going to have to learn the difference between a book they keep and a record somebody keeps, and that they have had it the wrong way round for five months: **a book a party keeps is a thing people can be asked about, and a record an office keeps is a thing nobody can be asked about until the office decides to let them**, and the only instrument this party has ever made is a nail and a sheet in a market, and it is worth exactly what is on it and nothing else. The second column takes the error against itself on the Saturday night with a reason and without a name of any kind in it, **and the reason is that the sixth figure is a figure a man in this harbor cannot check that afternoon, and there was nobody standing in front of that wall who could have put a hand on the entry and stopped him, and this yard has built its whole practice on the proposition that a figure anybody can check is worth something and a figure only its maker can check is worth less, and the first time this yard has had to say one of its own figures out loud in a market it got one of the six wrong, and on the sixth, and on the one about a man in another place.**

### Chapter 156 — The Ninth Acknowledgement, and a Man With Nothing Left

Sunday the twenty-seventh and Monday the twenty-eighth of August, on the mole's steps above the third seam's mark, in the open air, and in the shed. **THE OFFICER OF THIS HARBOR AUTHORITY IS ON HIS OWN STEPS AT TEN ON A SUNDAY, WHICH HE DOES NOT DO, WITH A SLATE UNDER HIS ARM AND HE HAD WRITTEN ON IT BEFORE HE CAME OUT, and there are about eleven people on the crown and the mole head's light is not on behind them and the two objects of nobody's are about thirty feet further up the crown, a hundred and three days since either of them was put there, with a corner off one of them since the seventeenth of June.** He has been asked for a ninth acknowledgement and has one to give and it is the last one he has. **HE NAMES THE PRICE BEFORE HE GRANTS IT, IN THE OPEN AIR, as he has done every time: the two of this year's that are already spent were not spent by his being generous, one of them was asked for by this party and one was not, and the price of the one that was not is four days of the best of a third lay's season in the first week of August, entered at the office's own cost with the repair account empty since the middle of February.** Then the price of the ninth: **an instrument of that office which puts a person, or a boat, or a lead, inside the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first carries his acknowledgement on the page and in the record and at his cost, and it has been free for nobody in nineteen years**, and there is no boat of that office and has not been one since the twenty-eighth of April. **AND HE NAMES THE MAN WHO PAYS BEFORE HE GRANTS IT, because he has never in nineteen years granted one without knowing who pays: CORBY VANCE, OF THE FIRST LAY, HAD THE TOE OF THIS MOLE BOOKED FOR MONDAY THE TWENTY-EIGHTH FOR A DAY OF HIS OWN AT A FIGURE OF HIS OWN AND WILL BE OUT OF A DAY AND A DAY'S WORK, and he is not this party and not this office and is not going to be asked for anything by anybody, and the reeve has not spoken to him and is not going to.** Asked whether he is going up to him, the reeve says no: **because if he does, then in about four years there is a man standing on a mole in another town who says that the harbor authority of Morrow Quay told a man of the first lay he was sorry on a Monday morning, and that will be the only true thing anybody in this harbor can say about this party in four years, and it will be about a Monday a man lost and not one word of it will be about the ground — a notice does not say what a man loses, and a party that has published itself is not a party that gets to be sorry in a street and mean it.** He grants it and says he has no capacity to refuse it and does not want a man in four years to read that ledger and think a reeve weighed this. **The second half of the price, said in the open air before anybody goes in the water: this office has now been asked twice by this party and has paid twice and cannot pay a third time, and he is not going to be asked a third time.** Calder Marr asks him one question and is told the same thirteen words he used in July — *it is not and I will not tell you what it is about* — and does not ask a second one, and the not-asking is entered with a date and a reason, **because a reeve who has just given you the last thing he has is a man you do not ask a question while he is standing in the wind.** Then the holder does not thank him and says why in nine words: **in four years a man reading that ledger reads it as a reeve's price.** The reeve says he knows that and it was the only instrument he had left. On Monday the wall behind the bench comes down bare for the seventeenth time and **the work is done on the mole's toe inside the first clause with the acknowledgement on the page, for a woman of the second lay with gear of her own under the toe and no other ground for it, at twelve of seventeen paid at the mole, the yard's own five out of the same board, the hour in and the hour out entered** — and the plate was not reached, not measured, not touched and not asked about, and the two objects of nobody's were not moved. **The cost fell on a man of the first lay and the yard entered his name and the figure and did not pay it, is not going to thank him, is not going to send for him, and is not going to say sorry in a market.** There is **five marks and twelve shillings and fourpence** in a bag, and the wage of Tuesday the twenty-ninth goes out of that, and the bag at the end of the batch is **a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence**.

### Chapter 157 — The First of September Is a Friday

Friday the first and Saturday the second of September, at the ninth seam's mooring, at the mole's toe, in daylight, and in the shed. **THE END OF IT ARRIVED AND WHAT ARRIVED WAS A WORKING ANSWER AND NOT A DECISION.** The two of them read a figure out of the yard's own book in the shed at about half past seven before anybody went down to the water, because the bag is what it is: **a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence, eighteen lines and seventy-two marks in the wage column with the fifth of March the oldest, the eighteenth line entered on Tuesday the twenty-ninth out of the money of Monday the twenty-eighth, in an envelope, in the holder's own left hand, with the week on the outside, and no chapter of this fortnight is set on that day and it is a line in this book and not a scene; three marks owing to a man of Ashtide for the eighth, with a date on it, not a shilling paid; seven pence owing to this yard's own shipwright for the postage of the twentieth, out of her own pocket and not paid; thirteen envelopes on a bench and one week fallen due this year with no envelope standing against it, which is the week of Tuesday the fifteenth of August, and those are two different figures; **and Tam Rundle is recognised, not introduced, for he gave nine days of his own watching of the pump on the twenty-ninth of July in the ship's own hand with an hour against it and has not been asked one word about it since;** five marks of compressor's gland on a page that has not been re-written, unpaid and of no use whatever because the thing after the gland is the hose; thirty-one on the chandler's note, not re-written, three counts unmoved; nothing in a tin since the twenty-eighth of April — and no wage falls on this Friday because a wage falls on a Tuesday and no Tuesday has come.** Tam Rundle of the tide gang, which is four men short, and Danno Fisk of the third lay were out there since half past seven, **because on the third of July an end of it was entered in the harbor authority's book in the officer's own hand and it said the first of September, and that is the whole reason there are two of them standing in this water.** They had walked out on the sixth, the fourteenth, the twenty-first and the twenty-eighth of August on their own time, and on the twenty-eighth scraped a ring off a stone, put it back, took a turn on the eye and re-seized it with a maul, and **the office did not tell them to and would not have known they came and the repair account was not filled and they were paid nothing and the yard paid nothing and did not ask.** The lead went down at about twenty past nine and the mooring is sound and in service, and **it is not a repair. It is *looked at*, in the words of the man of the third lay, and that is the whole of the difference between the first of May and this morning.** The clerk says what a box holds, fourth time this year, and says he is not going to say it a fifth time in a market because four is enough and a fifth would only be him being interesting. **Danno Fisk then puts the other half of it in the shed himself, with his name against it and nothing wanted: he read the end of it off a WALL on the third of July, not out of the book, thought that a date is a day and a day arrives, told two other men in a market, and a week later one of them mentioned a mooring and he said the end of it is the first of September and the man said *right* and that was the end of the conversation and they had a pint. A man who does a thing with a number on it that you printed with your own name on it is not doing you a favor. He is using your sheet.** The yard enters the whole of it in the first column on the Saturday night with the hour and the two names against it, **and enters a second thing underneath against its own interest: a party that has been telling this harbor since the third of May that the only thing that makes a wall worth reading is that somebody can be asked a question about it, and on the first of September a man in this harbor was asked a question about this party's wall by nobody, answered it out loud in a market to two men drinking, and two strangers then walked out to a mole four times on their own time. This party is not going to claim it, is not going to be thanked for it, does not want to be, and has not been told that it was thanked for it by anybody.** And the yard's reason for not being there is a working reason and is on the page. **The mooring is not on the list of costs any more, and that list is read out at the head of this dock in nineteen days' time in the same words and one line of it is going to be different, and the yard has entered that it will be different and has not asked anybody to notice it.** And the two of them did not discuss it, and the reason for that is hers and is on the page and nowhere else, and the not-discussing is the closest these two have come in four volumes.

### Chapter 158 — Six Marks and Two Days, and a Man on the Steps

Friday the eighth of September, at the market office, from the steps outside it, and in the shed. **HE DID NOT GO IN.** He was on the steps **from about ten past ten until about half past eleven, which is the eighty minutes the card and the entry both give**, and about half of what was said inside came out of the door and he heard enough of it to know whose voice it was inside about a minute in. **Mara Venn is at the long table with a book of her own, a pencil and her own brass calipers, and a man in a coat is at the end of the table with a paper, and she did not ask anybody to be there and this party did not ask her for anything and did not arrange any of it.** She is asked about five bells and gives **six things and no seventh: that a case of that size in that position was in the boat when she was built, and the pins and the seizing were the only thing holding it there; that five bells of that size is not a cargo, being about a fifth of a ton against a ballast of stone, and a boat carrying stones and bells was carrying two kinds of weight not in one trade; that bell-metal is a trade in about four hands on this coast and a casting of that size would be known in a fortnight and one in nine months is a casting nobody in the county has heard; that a line cut in the lip of a bell is cut after the casting and all four the Sill have are cut, so somebody who was not a founder sat down with each of them in turn, and a man who does that four times has a method and not a message; that bell-metal cracks in a fire and a crack is a hole and a book burned is a book gone while a bell burned is a bell still in the world with a piece of it missing; and that there are four of them left of a number she does not know and she has not asked why those four and not those four and is not going to.** **Then the seventh, which is the price: SIX MARKS AND TWO DAYS, THE SAME ON EVERY COAST, WHICH DOES NOT COME DOWN, and she puts the two sixes next to each other on purpose and asks somebody in the room to notice it and be annoyed about it, because six things she can give away and the seventh she cannot, and a finding of no office carries no seal and cannot be produced on demand by anybody, which is why her finding of the fifteenth and sixteenth of July is on a wall unsigned at her own asking, and a man in a city can order the one of those and cannot order the other and does not know which of them he has got. She has not got six marks and has not got two days and has forty marks standing since the fifteenth of October, and this yard has not offered her six marks and has not been asked to and is going to say no if it ever is.** A woman of about sixty with a basket asks her about the sheet of the twenty-fifth, and her answer is the only thing Calder Marr heard in full: **a sheet carries the limits its own lines carry and does not carry a thing they do not say, and if a sheet says a yard asked for a copy of a record and has not been given one, then what is on the sheet is a fact about a request, and the record is not on the sheet and is not checkable by anybody in this harbor including her — and she cannot tell anybody what is in that boat, and the trade that can is her trade and it is six marks and two days and it will be the same sentence tomorrow.** The yard does not go in, does not send anybody in, does not ask her afterwards what she said, **and enters why in the first column with the date and the hour — that a party which has said out loud in a market that it does not ask a man one single question about a boat has got to be able to say it about a woman, and that it said it about a woman on the twenty-sixth of July, and that the four people whose bells they are have a written limit in their own words and a signature on it, and that this party is not going to be the reason any part of it is asked — and a request to strike that line out of the column is refused in the same words it is refused for a line in any column.** And then the holder does the thing he has not done for six weeks: **he writes down the sentence she told him on the seventh of August not to write down, in the second column, with a reason and no name in it, and does not tell her he has written it, and says so out loud before he does, and she says *I know you are. I have known since about eleven o'clock this morning.*** No money moves and the day is a day this yard is not on a mole.

### Chapter 159 — The Book Is Produced Whole

Saturday the ninth of September, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin, in daylight, in about nine people's hearing, and in the shed at night. **THE HEARING ROOM IS USED ONCE IN THIS BATCH, and the book in it is forty-seven weeks old and not fifty-one, and the boy's hand is at the head of the third column and not the first, and the copy's leaving this harbor is the first time in four volumes and not four months.** The wall behind the reeve's bench came down bare on Monday the fourth and that was the eighteenth Monday since the first of May and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it. **A man in a coat reads a paper with four things on it and says his name and the name of his office and does not sit down: this paper is a production and not an appointment; the party is required to produce at this room on this day its crew's book kept under the reporting clause, whole and unshortened, with a copy of it; the writ and the four returns; an answer to one question, which is the question the clause itself asks and is not his; and nothing in the paper touches, grants or refuses the second season, and a sitting to be appointed will follow and will be served on the party and not left with anybody.** **The question is: on or about Tuesday the eighth of August instant, did this party make a finding of worked glass, whole or in part; and if it did, state the place, the hour, the keeper and the limits.** The holder says out loud why he is opening the book before he opens it, in the same words he has said since the second of May: **the second column is not a defense of anything and never was, it is a page where a man writes down what he cannot check and puts a reason against the line so that a person reading it in four years can see that the man knew he was writing something he could not check; the count of four in the first column in a ship's hand with no name against it is the count of four and is not a fifth thing; the third column was made on the second of May by a boy of twenty-two and is filled late and was not filled on the eighth of August; and at the head of that column there is a line saying that a number in a lead plug in a chest in that shed is not where that goes, and he is not going to say one word about that number to anybody and never has.** **AND NOT ONE LINE OF THE BOOK IS READ ALOUD IN THAT ROOM. It took about ten minutes to go through and about two minutes to make the copy, made with a pen and not a press, and the man in the coat looked at the copy and did not open it, and said he is not going to open it here and is going to take it to a city and read it there, and told them that now so they can have it in their own book tonight and not be told about it in October. THE COPY WENT INTO A FILE, and that is the whole of what happened to it, and it is the first time in four volumes that a copy of this yard's own record has left this harbor.** **Ambrose Sell enters the date, the hour he wrote it, and not the hour it was done, and says for the ninth time this year a thing he could not enter, and names the first eight, and this time the thing he cannot hold is a line in the second column about a man in a boat on the eighth of August not being able to tell for about a second and a half whether a shout came off the water or off the deck, and says that what a box holds is that the line exists, that it is in the second column and not the first, that it has a reason against it, and that it was entered on the Wednesday night by the man whose line it is, and that what a box does not hold is what was in it.** The man in a coat asks his one question and the yard answers it in a form and not a conclusion, in the order the clause wants and in no other order: the place, about a mile and a half off this harbor inside the four hundred yards in about seven fathoms; the hour, four minutes past two until about ten past four; the keeper, Calder Marr in the water and Nessa Pike in the boat with the cutoff hers and the word *Enough* which she did not use and has entered why; the limits, seventeen minutes a call, three calls, set by the diver and not raisable; **and then that no finding of worked glass was made on that day, nothing was raised, four persons were taken off a vessel, no return goes up and none is going to, and the party told no office and will tell no office — and that he is not going to tell them which part of a clause caught that day, and would like it minuted that he was not asked to and declined to be asked, because if he starts arguing about limbs in a room he will be in that room in March.** Then the writ goes on the table face down and the book goes back in its bag and the whole of it takes about eleven minutes including the copy. **In the shed at nine the first column takes the whole of it, and it enters that the party will be at the sitting and will not be represented and will answer what is asked and not what it would have chosen; and that it is going to keep producing its book whole whatever that costs, and that the reason is that a yard which has said in a market that it produces its book whole had better be the first to produce it, and the worst available place to produce a book is a room somebody else has arranged.** And the second column, half an hour later, is about the not-reading: **that for about ten minutes this morning this party had its whole record on a table in a room in daylight in front of nine people and a man with a pen and did not read one line of it, and that there was a line in that book which the holder would have read, and did not, and that this entry does not say which line and does not say that he wanted to, and that a line read out in a room is a line somebody else writes down, and that a man writes down the line and not the reason against it because reasons are long, and that the party has entered the not-reading against itself and cannot tell yet, standing there, whether it was a rule or a fear, and that it is going to have to be able to answer that in about three weeks, and that the answer is not in any column of this book.** No money moves.

### Chapter 160 — The Seat

Sunday the tenth of September, at the top of this mole's steps above the third seam's mark, in the open air, in about nine people's hearing, and in the shed at night. **THE BATCH CLIMAX.** **A clerk of the circuit office comes up this hill at about eleven on a Sunday with a paper in his hand and serves it at the top of the steps, in daylight, in about nine people's hearing, with the two objects of nobody's about thirty feet further up the crown behind them and a corner off one of them since the seventeenth of June.** The notice is four paragraphs: **that a sitting is appointed for a day in the week following, at ten in the morning, at the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin, and that the four parties named are required to attend; that where a finding is disputed between persons the harbor authority may seat one of the two disputants and take such finding as is given without prejudice to any claim, and that the construction of that paragraph is the construction of the second limb of a reporting clause and is applied by this notice to this party, and that the party did not write it and cannot un-write it; that nothing in it touches, grants, refuses or deals with the second season of the writ, and that the writ runs to midnight on the first of March, is not the Second Bell license and does not lead to one and renews nothing, and that its ground is not widened by this notice; and a list of six things to produce, including any record of any finding made on or about the eighth of August and any sheet or paper bearing the party's own figures made by the party and put up on a public wall in this harbor on or about the twenty-fifth of August, with a copy of it.** A clerk's name is at the foot of it and the clerk is Ambrose Sell. **AND HE READ THE FIRST PARAGRAPH OUT OF THE PAPER IN HIS HAND IN THE OPEN AIR AND THE OTHER THREE OUT OF HIS OWN MEMORY, AND THEN SAID THAT HE HAD SAID IT FROM THE PAPER, AND THE YARD HAS WRITTEN DOWN THAT HE DID NOT AND THAT HE SAID HE DID, AND IS NOT GOING TO IMPROVE ON EITHER.** He says one thing before he goes down the hill: **that somebody in a city has taken a thing this yard agreed to in April and turned it round so that it points at this yard instead of at the water, and that this yard agreed to it in good faith and wrote it out in public on a market wall in July, and forty copies are in about sixty boats, and one of them is in a city at a press that cannot be stopped, and he does not know who drafted it and has not been told and is not going to find out and would not put it in his page if he did, because his page holds what he can enter and not what he can guess.** Nobody thanked him. **The yard does not refuse the notice, does not sign anything on the step, and lets nobody in that market say in a market what this yard thought it was, and at about nine that night the whole of it goes into the first column in the holder's own left hand, in advance, with the hour against it and the reason against the line** — including that the said notice contains no position of any office, and no document, and no phrase belonging to any office, and that a man reading it in five years will not find in it one word about who changed a rule or what a rule was called from; **and that whoever drafted the fourth paragraph has read a market wall of this harbor or has had somebody read one to him, and this party does not know who, and has entered that it does not know and is not going to guess, because the decision of the fifth of July that this yard does not put a name on a thing it cannot check was made about a question asked in a market and it binds the same way in the other direction**; and that the party will be at the sitting, will not be represented, will answer what is asked and not what it would have chosen, **and that this is a decision and not a virtue and this party is not going to be thanked for it and is not asking.** **And the shipwright asks him one question, in her own trade's voice: are you going to read the second column out. He says I do not know. And she says *then put that in the second column with a reason and not in the first, and if you put it in the first you will have written a decision and you have not made one.* And he does**, and the line says that the holder does not know, and the reason is that a man in a boat can check which of his two own hands was on a line at a given second and a man in a room can be checked, and that this one prefers to be checked and does not yet know what he will say, and that the party is not going to spend the three weeks reading anything, and that a man who tries to find out what he will say in a room by reading is going to find out what he wished he would have said. **And at about ten she says the thing about the box — that is the same box we built in April and it has been turned round so that we are the thing on the table and not the person reading the table — and then she says the thing that is actually going to cost them, which is that this yard has an objection in the first column in its own hand since the second of May, *a finding is not a conclusion*, the best sentence in that book, and it cannot be used, because it was written for a return and this is not a return, and the whole of the trouble is that we have been standing in front of a clause for five months without noticing that the clause was about glass.** **The third column is not filled on the Sunday night, which is the second day since the second of May it has not been filled, and nobody has been appointed, and a column nobody fills is a column somebody else fills, and the head of it keeps the hand of a boy of twenty-two and a request to scrape it out is refused in the same words it is refused for a line in any column, and the forty marks stand whole and the yard is not going to hire a boy to read a position back at four marks a week and call that the same thing.** No money moves; there is a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence in the bag, a wage of four falls on **Tuesday the nineteenth** — the fifteenth of September is a Friday and there is no wage Tuesday in that week, and the card that said the fifteenth is corrected to the nineteenth — a day at the second seam's own face has brought five and five this month and a wage is four, three marks is owing to a man of Ashtide for the eighth and has not moved since the eighth, the note is at thirty-one and not re-written, the tin has been empty since the twenty-eighth of April, no air has been bought, and the bell off the south end is not going out and is not going to go out inside four years and the standing sentence of the third of May is unreversed and nothing in this month has touched it. **No name of anything was said in that shed that night and the four bare lines in that book are still four and are still bare and not one has been struck out, which is how the condition is carried in this volume: named nowhere. No fifth return is made. No acknowledgement is entered and the ninth stands as the last and there is not going to be a tenth. Nobody in that shed goes over to the far bench.** And the party cannot answer one question, knows it cannot answer it tonight, and is going to be asked it in a room — not *who changed the rule*, which is asked in a market in August and is not this yard's to answer, but a narrower one, put to the holder in a shed tonight and written down in the second column with a reason against it and his own hand: **whether he is going to read the second column out in a room.**

---

## Volume 03, Batch 0005 (Chapters 141–150) — complete. Movement 5, the cutter and the bell-archive. **THE VOLUME'S CLIMAX AND ITS RESOLUTION.**

**No card file existed for this batch. `outline/batches/volume-03-batch-0005.md` is not in this repository and none was invented, reconstructed, or written. The batch was shaped from `outline/volume-03.md` alone — its Movement 5 section, its Volume climax, its Concrete resolution, its New-volume question, its Final chapter image, its Reveal discipline, and its twenty-eight locks — and out of the four state files Batch 0004 had just written. That is the third time this volume has been built that way and it is recorded in its own first line so that a reader of the state files is not left guessing.**

**Actual word count: 29,823 words across ten chapters as re-measured by the Volume 03 volume audit (`reviews/volume-03/volume-03-close.md`, 27 September 2026), 29,814 before that audit's eleven mechanical repairs, and 29,688 before this batch's own review repairs** (Ch141 2,666 · Ch142 2,615 · Ch143 2,743 · Ch144 2,704 · Ch145 2,363 · Ch146 2,795 · Ch147 3,640 · Ch148 2,999 · Ch149 3,213 · Ch150 4,085), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Seven are inside the guideline and three are past it — Chapter 147 by 440, Chapter 149 by 13 and Chapter 150 by 885 — and all three ran because a cost could not be dropped.** 147 is the climax: the six conditions, the approach, three calls down, the case half out of the water, the boy in seven fathoms, the choice, nineteen minutes in the water, four people off, and the second-column entry that puts the price of the choice in the yard's own book. 150 is the volume's close and carries the market wall, the sheet, the wage that is not paid, the third column, **the chandler's note said out loud as un-re-written**, and the whole labor spine of the batch. The hearing room is used **once**, on **Saturday the twelfth of August**. The market wall is used **once**, on **Tuesday the fifteenth of August**, in daylight, in a market, on no Monday and on no hearing-room day. **The *Vigil's* own notice is on that wall twice, on Friday the twenty-eighth of July and Friday the fourth of August, and neither is this party's use of it, and the chapter says so.** The batch's pressures alternate: a day of work and a Crown notice, a mole with a glass and an arithmetic, a wage and a boy and a public sentence, a grounding in four minutes, a room open to anybody and a second nail, a reeve's last entry and a cost entered in advance, the working day, a testimony with a limit on it, a man in a coat and a question in a market, and the close.

**The money, and it closes checkable on the page: NOTHING in a bag, thirty-one on the chandler's note, nothing in a tin, and three marks owing to a man of Ashtide, on Tuesday the fifteenth of August — against nothing in the same bag on Wednesday the twenty-sixth of July.** The chain is **0, 3, 3, 7, 3, 7, 7, 11, 0, 0** across the days money moves: a day at the second seam on a hirer's own account on Thursday the twenty-seventh, gross fifteen and the yard's three; a day on Monday the thirty-first, gross thirteen and the yard's four; the wage on Tuesday the first of August; a day on the dump on Wednesday the second, gross thirteen and the yard's four; a day on Monday the seventh, gross thirteen and the yard's four, which is the first time the figure has been eleven since the fourteenth of June; and then Tuesday the eighth — **the wage, four marks; two pots of the office's air at this yard's own two-mark rate, four marks; and the master of the *Sallow Reach* at six marks of his own naming, of which three is paid at the mole and three is not — which empties the bag exactly.** **Three wage weeks fall in the batch, the first, eighth and fifteenth of August. The first and the eighth went into envelopes; the fifteenth was NOT PAID, and the envelope was not made, and that is the batch's cost and it is in the first column with the figure against it.** The column goes **fifteen lines and sixty marks – sixteen lines and sixty-four marks** and stops, with the fifth of March the oldest and staying there, and eleven envelopes on a bench and not one of them anywhere in twelve weeks. The note is not re-written, its three counts of weeks do not move, five marks of compressor's gland stand on it unpaid and useless, and the tin is empty. **No money came in from any office in this batch and none went out to any person except three wages, two pots of air and a day of another crew's boat.**

### Chapter 141 — Four Days in Somebody Else's Hand, and a Sentence in Our Own

Thursday the twenty-seventh and Friday the twenty-eighth of July, at the second seam, in the shed, on a quay, and at the head of the dock. A day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, nobody in the water, gross fifteen and the yard's three; **the cistern is not charged, with the reason on the page in the yard's own hand, the gate taking four hours with three men.** **A tender comes inside the line and a man in a coat nails a notice to the market wall on the outside of whatever is there** — and the notice is quoted in full: the *Hearthwake* is claimed by the Crown as Crown material, being one of a formation of vessels described in a schedule held elsewhere and not produced to this harbor; any person who boards, works upon, takes from or renders assistance is to report in writing to the harbor authority within four days; and no assistance will be rendered by the officer commanding. **THE NOTICE HAS FOUR DAYS IN IT AND THE SAME HAND ON IT AS THE REPORTING CLAUSE, and the shipwright says that in about nine words and it is in the first column because a man said it in a market.** And Calder Marr reads his own sentence of the fifth of July back on the quay in nine people's hearing and says it is now the reason he cannot help. **Nell Ardery, of the second lay, sixty-odd, says the boat has been pumping for nine days, gives her name, says her mother was on the Sill, asks for nothing, and is in the first column in the shipwright's hand with the hour.** And a crew of Ashtide takes four hundred feet of Sella Brigg's own warp off the bottom of this harbor for twenty-six marks and a day, and she is paid by him and is owed eight shillings and tenpence by this yard, and the two figures are not one.

### Chapter 142 — A Compressor of His Own, and Ten Marks We Do Not Have

Saturday the twenty-ninth and Sunday the thirtieth of July, on the crown, in a shed, and in a doorway. **A man of the tide gang, Tam Rundle, thirty, four men where there should be eight, stops on the crown and gives nine days of his own watching: her pump going since about the time the gang started walking her, upright on the twenty-first, a foot of list on the twenty-second, two foot on the twenty-fourth, four foot and about seven fathoms this morning, and he has said nothing to nine people in his office and is saying it and is not going to say it again.** **The shipwright then says the thing the whole argument turns on and it is checkable: the outer marks are two stones, the bar of four hundred yards is a line off them on the officer's own chart, the *Hearthwake* is inside that line and about three miles inside the imperial line, and every one of those three lines is a line about paper and not one of them is a line about water.** And Calder Marr, on the crown, finds the half of it nobody had said: **there is no writ past the outer marks and no clause past the outer marks and no instrument and no keeper and no return, and if we go we go on our own account, and he has been trying to make that sentence into a reason not to go for four days and it is not one.** Sunday, Perry Ollett comes up the hill in daylight and names six marks for the *Sallow Reach*, the day and two men, the air at no figure because it is his own, and says out loud that he is not doing it because he is a good man and that he will say the figure himself if anybody gets it wrong. **The shipwright's four reasons the plan fails, given out loud in a shed with the date of her last asking of it said out loud, and a note that she did not say the fifth thing.** And the arithmetic on the back of a slate: ten marks at the outer marks, three in the bag, and the only way this yard has ever made ten marks is four days of work it is not doing.

### Chapter 143 — Thirteen Mondays, and the Thing He Was Kept For
