Monday the thirty-first of July and Tuesday the first of August, in the shed, on the steps, and at the head of the dock. **The wall comes down bare for the thirteenth time; the cistern is charged for the thirteenth time by strangers and not by this party.** A day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, gross thirteen and the yard's four. **THE RESOLUTION'S SENTENCE ABOUT THE BOY IS ENTERED HERE, IN THE FIRST COLUMN, IN THE HOLDER'S OWN LEFT HAND, AND IT IS NOT A LESSON AND NOBODY LEARNS ANYTHING FROM IT: the thing Bevin Rook was kept for is reading a position back into a slate for a man in a boat; he did it for two hundred days; on the fourth of May he read four figures and got all four right and on the eleventh of May he read two and got one wrong; he was put off the stone on the fifteenth on a date the party named before anybody asked; he has not done it since and is not going to and cannot; the party has entered that he cannot, has not asked him, is not going to, and is not going to hire a boy to do it and call that the same thing; a column in that book is not a wage; and the head of the third column carries two times in his hand and they have not been scraped out and a request to scrape them out is refused in the same words it is refused for a line in any column.** The third column is filled at about four hours' lateness, the ninth entry since the second of May, and the party refuses to draw a figure out of nine. Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days, is asked nothing, asks nothing, neither brother goes over to him. **The argument the holder loses: the shipwright says they should say her name in a market on Saturday and then says, after a full day, that the end of that is a form, and that a thing said in a street about people in a boat is a thing that can be used against the people in the boat, and he agrees and asks for the date on the rule.**

### Chapter 144 — Seven Foot of List, and a Tender That Came Alongside to Hold Her Down

Wednesday the second and Thursday the third of August, on the dump, on the crown, in a doorway, and at the head of the dock. **The third lay's moorings shut at seven in the morning on a board, and the man who pays for the four days read the date out loud.** A day on the dump — ground the writ of the first of May names in its own ground — gross thirteen and the yard's four. **And at about half past two in the afternoon, from the crown, in nine people's hearing, in about four minutes, a boat with people in her comes up half a fathom on the flood and takes the ground by the head and settles with about seven foot of list, and the *Vigil's* own tender, at two hundred yards, puts a grapnel on her anchor cable and holds her there.** The entry is in the first column with the hour, and the hour and a half it took to write it is entered, and the reason is that a party which has said out loud in a market that it is not going to ask a man one question about a boat has to be able to say it about a woman too, and it said it about a woman on the twenty-sixth of July. **The *Vigil* is sighted at anchor two and a half miles off and four minutes of glass is spent on her and one sentence is entered: a man who writes down what he has seen of a vessel of the Crown from a mile and a half with a glass is a man who has begun to keep a watch, and this yard is not going to be a party to the keeping of a watch either, and the second of those was not in the entry of the fifth of July and is now.** The cistern is charged for the fourteenth time. And at about five Nell Ardery comes to the doorway and gives the figure nobody in the shed can get: **they can pump her with what is in her until about the fourth day of August, and after that the water comes in faster than two men and a boy and a woman can shift it**, and it is a figure they have because they have been doing it nine days and can see what is left in the pump's own well. And the day booked for Friday the seventh is not cancelled, because a tide at the second seam's face at about ten past one and a tide at the outer marks at about two on the fourth of August are two different tides and a man has to be on the water at the first one.

### Chapter 145 — Six Marks and Two Days, Asked by a Woman in a Room, and a Second Nail

Friday the fourth and Saturday the fifth of August, in the market office, on a market wall, and in a shed. **Mara Venn is at the long table in the market office at about ten on a Friday morning because that office is open to anybody and this party did not arrange it, and a woman of the second lay asks her a question out loud in a room of nine people, and not this yard.** She prices herself first, says she would have said the same figure if the yard had asked, says she has said she would not do a thing at nothing again and is going to do this once and says so in front of nine people so it cannot be said for her later, and then gives **six things and no seventh about the Crown notice: it is a second original and not a copy; it is guillotine-cut office paper; the first two paragraphs are a clerk's hand and the third and fourth are a naval officer's; the first notice said *any part of her* and this one says *any part of her and everything in her*, and that is the only line in either paper that reads like a man writing to himself; the fold is a wet naval fold; and the seal is a stamp struck in ink and off by about a sixteenth of an inch.** And then the seventh, which is the one she does not have: she cannot tell anybody what is in that boat, the trade that can is six marks and two days, and there is no instrument, no money and no trade on this coast that reaches five and a half fathoms inside the four hundred yards. **A tender comes in at two and a man in a coat takes the first notice down and nails a longer second one up on the same nails, and the party does not go and read it.** Two people of the third lay bring a copy up the hill. And on the Saturday a man comes up the hill and says her pump stopped for an hour at about ten, and a lamp went up in her rigging and was answered from a boat on the inner shoals, and he does not know what three short and one long means and is going out on his own tide at twelve. **The shipwright's entry refuses to learn a code because a boat is going down, and the refusal is entered, and the sentence is that a code is the only thing a community keeps that a stranger cannot read off a book, and this yard has been here five months and has learned nine places out of four books and not one practice of anybody.**

### Chapter 146 — An Office With Nothing Left to Put In Its Book, and a Cost Entered Before It Is Paid

Sunday the sixth and Monday the seventh of August, on the mole's steps, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **The officer of the harbor authority is on his own steps at ten on a Sunday and reads two entries out of his own book and neither is softened: that a vessel of this coast went on the ground inside the four hundred yards with four persons in her while the third lay's moorings were shut on the authority of an examination he entered on the twenty-sixth of July, the two being eleven words apart in his own book and he put them there; and that a notice of the Crown is nailed over a return made under a writ this office granted, so that the paper of this office is what a Crown paper is nailed over in this market, and this office cannot take it down and has no power to. And then: there is nothing in my book that will put a boat of mine past the outer marks, and nothing in yours either, and that is not a failure of either book, it is the design. HE SPENDS AUTHORITY A THIRD TIME, THE PRICE FALLS ON THE THIRD LAY AGAIN, NOBODY THANKED HIM, HE HAS NO SECOND SEASON IN HIS KEEPING AND A SPENT LEDGER, AND HE IS NOT A FRIEND. Calder Marr asks him nothing about any of it and enters all three not-askings with a date, and refuses to thank him for the second entry and says that in four years a man reading that wall will read it as a reeve's price. Monday: the wall down bare for the fourteenth time; the cistern charged for the fifteenth time by strangers while the party is on the second seam; **a day at the second seam on a hirer's own account, gross thirteen and the yard's four, and the string laid out along the foot of the face again for no reason the party could give and did not write one; ELEVEN MARKS IN A BAG.** **THE DECISION IS ENTERED IN THE FIRST COLUMN AT NINE O'CLOCK ON THE MONDAY NIGHT, BEFORE THE DAY AND NOT AFTER, and it costs four paragraphs: that the party is going past the outer marks on its own account with no instrument of any office; that the sentence of the fifth and twenty-sixth of July is broken and that this party broke it and the breaking is entered in advance; and that the cost is the air, the *Sallow Reach* at six marks part paid, the wage of the same Tuesday, and the sentence itself, which cannot be said again, with the reason — a yard that has said a thing in a market and has not done it is a yard whose saying is worth nothing in any figure it puts on any wall in any year after this one — and that the forty marks and the eight shillings and tenpence and the eight marks are not paid either way and are not in the cost and are not mentioned in it; and that the party knows the price of the third lay's four days and is not going to look at it; and that nobody goes near the imperial line, that the Second Bell license is not granted and is not approached and would not be known what to do with, and that if anything were raised out there it would not be reported to any office and the yard knows what that will look like in a file in five years. ALDEN MARR IS AT THE FAR BENCH AND THE HOLDER DID NOT GO OVER TO HIM, and neither brother has said why.** And Nessa Pike says one thing that is not in the book and tells him not to write it down, and he does not write it down.

### Chapter 147 — What Was on the Gunwale, and What Was in the Water

**THE CLIMAX. Tuesday the eighth and Wednesday the ninth of August. A working day, not a room.** The wage goes in at about eleven, four marks, the sixteenth line and the sixty-fourth mark, the eleventh envelope. Two pots of the harbor authority's own air at this yard's own two-mark rate go out at about a quarter past twelve. **At the mole at ten past one Perry Ollett comes alongside and takes six marks off the board for the boat, the day and two men, the air at no figure because it is his, and the yard pays three of the six at the mole in the way it pays every other man at the mole, and three marks is a debt to a man of Ashtide with a date on it. AND HE SAYS THE THIRD THING BEFORE HE GOES, so that he does not have to say it at six: that if a boy comes off her foredeck he is going in after him on a line off his OWN bow and will refuse the yard's line, because a boat of his alongside a boat of theirs with a pot in it is a thing that has gone wrong in other waters.** **THE SIX ARE READ OUT IN ORDER AT THE BOAT IN DAYLIGHT AT TWENTY PAST ONE, in the same words, the limit at seventeen set by the diver and not raisable by the holder, the cutoff hers and the word *Enough*, and the stated reason entered in the yard's own book with the clerk's absence entered rather than filled in AND WITH THE FURTHER FACT ENTERED THAT THERE IS NO CLERK TO BE PRESENT BECAUSE THERE IS NO INSTRUMENT TO BE PRESENT FOR, the writ being bounded by the outer marks and the clause being on the writ.** They are on the water at four minutes past two; the outer marks go by on the port bow at about a quarter past; the shipwright says the time out loud; the two men of the *Sallow Reach* say nothing. **Three calls, seventeen minutes each, kept three out of three. The hatch is under about five and a half feet at the top of the tide and the pins on the grating are seized. There are five bells in a case about four feet by two, not a set, a line cut in the lip of each that is not a letter in any hand on this coast, the largest about a foot across the mouth, and HE PUTS HIS HAND ON IT AND IT IS COLD AND IT SAYS NOTHING AND HE DOES NOT STRIKE IT, and the reason he did not is that a bell is not a thing you find out what by making a noise on.** In about six feet of water in the dark he reads a seam on her garboard — a plank cut and let go and mended over, mendings under mendings — **and it tells him nothing about when or in what order or by whose hand, and THIS TIME HE DOES NOT SUPPLY THE REST, and the reason is that a man who has been wrong in public in a market once about the word *chain* does not get to be the man who supplies the rest of a sentence about a boat with people in her.** Four of the five in a bight of the *Sallow Reach's* warp come up to the gunwale and stand on end in about three feet of water with the lid against her own rail. **AND AT ABOUT TWENTY PAST THREE THE GRAPNEL COMES TIGHT AND SHE GOES OVER ANOTHER THREE FOOT AND A BOY OF SEVENTEEN GOES OFF HER FOREDECK INTO SEVEN FATHOMS. He shouted, and for about a second and a half Calder Marr could not tell whether the shout came from the water or from the deck, and that second and a half is the whole of what is entered against him for it.** **HE LET GO OF THE LINE. The case went down straight and the grating lid was the last of it. One of Ollett's men said a word in his own language and the other said nothing. He was in the water in about a second and a half.** Nineteen minutes; the *Hearthwake* rolling a foot and a half on a seven foot list; the boy twice under the *Sallow Reach's* counter; the woman on her own doorway with a boathook holding him on while Ollett's man takes his own line off his own bow and gets a bight round the boy's chest from the outside; the fourth man off her on his own account with a line round his own waist that the boy of seventeen had put there before he went over the rail. **Nessa Pike never said the word, and it is entered, and the reason is that a man of thirty-two going to a boy of seventeen was not going to be stopped by a woman in a boat in about nineteen minutes, and if it had been twenty-five she would have pulled it, and she has entered that too.** On the Wednesday the *Sallow Reach* takes four people off at about two, the pump handle left in the pump, and they come up the mole's steps at about six in the afternoon in front of about forty people with the mole head's light not on behind them. **THE SECOND COLUMN IS FILLED ON THE WEDNESDAY NIGHT WITH THE WORD NOT IN IT, and the reason for the line is that a man on a mole cannot check which of his own ears a sound came in through, and the party has made that line twice now and is not going to make it a third time for the same thing and is not going to stop making it.** And nothing was raised: a basket half out of a hatch in a boat is not a raising, the clause does not reach that ground, there is no fifth return, and a dated line says the party was past the outer marks on its own account, raised nothing, told no office and will tell no office. And the third column was not filled on the eighth, and that is entered in the first column with the reason, and with the further fact that the party does not know who will fill it next and has not appointed anybody.

### Chapter 148 — What a Bell-Archive Is, and the Limit a Person in a Doorway Is Wearing

Thursday the tenth and Friday the eleventh of August, on the mole's steps, in the market office, and in a shed. **Four of the Sill come up the steps and stop, and the woman names them and herself without being asked: Nessa Vale, thirty-eight, Awn Proude, Tom Kittow, and Ewan Kittow, seventeen, who went into the water on the Tuesday and a man of this yard came after him, and she says it once in front of forty people and will not say it again and will not say it to him.** And then the thing this harbor has been looking at: **the Sill is the weir at the south end of this mole, its sill stone under four feet of stone at the south end, and they have been in this harbor since the second of June and put her over the shoal inside the four hundred yards because they had to, and they pumped her for nine weeks and did not put a lamp up and did not go to the officer and did not send a letter.** What a bell-archive is, in eleven minutes and a good question: **not a book; a thing you open. Five bells with a line cut into the lip of each, read out — not written down — by whoever has the turn, on a fixed day, in the hall, in front of the whole community, saying what they did, when, how many, who was in it and what it cost. The oldest is about a hundred and forty years by the sound of it. The other four are the ones they could not leave behind. The rest went in a fire in a year she will not name in a market, and one of their own saw that fire and she is dead nine years and there is nobody left who heard it and one of the bells that went into it. IT IS WORTH EVERYTHING THEY HAVE AND IT IS WORTH NOTHING WHATEVER TO ANYBODY STANDING ON THAT MOLE, AND BOTH OF THOSE ARE TRUE AND NEITHER IS TAKEN BACK.** **The limit is written by them, in their own words, signed by four people, and it is real: they will not say it in a shed, they will not say it into a book that goes out of this harbor, they will not say it to a man with a seal, they will not say one word about who took her off them, they will not give their names to any office in any city — because a bell-archive is not a thing that is read out, it is a thing that is struck, in a hall, in front of the people it is about, and there is nobody in this town it is about.** **AND THE HOLDER SAYS OUT LOAD, IN FORTY PEOPLE'S HEARING, THAT HE WANTED IT LIFTED, THAT HE CANNOT LIFT IT, THAT HE IS NOT GOING TO ASK TWICE, AND THAT THE WANTING IS ON THE PAGE WITH A DATE ON IT.** On the Friday the page is written out in the market office, which is open to anybody on a Tuesday and a Friday morning, and **Mara Venn is at the long table as a person who uses the room and is asked nothing by anybody, and gives six things about the bells — a maker's mark on the sound bow of each, the line and the lip cut with the same tool, two shallow cuts of different ages inside the lip of the largest of the same order as the two cuts at the second seam — and then says the thing that is the point: five bells at five and a half fathoms inside the four hundred yards under the after hatch of a vessel claimed whole by an office in a city, and no instrument here that reaches that water, no money here that reaches that water, no trade on this coast whose work it is, and she is not going and is not asking anybody to send her, and the not-saying costs her and not the boat.** Forty copies go out at no price, and a shilling changes hands for the *Sallow Reach's* sheet and is not this yard's income. **Nothing in the bag, and the reason is that a yard of two and a half that spent three days in a boat past the outer marks and three days writing down what it did there has not sold a day in ten days, and the men who hire a day want a Monday or a Tuesday and there has been no Monday and no Tuesday in the ten days.** And four people of the Sill have no boat, no wages and about nine marks between them, and have asked this yard for nothing, and are not going to be given anything, and the yard has entered that it would have liked to and had nothing.

### Chapter 149 — A Man in a Coat With a Paper, and a Question in a Market Nobody Answers

Saturday the twelfth and Sunday the thirteenth of August, in the hearing room over the tide-gang store, and on the boards. **THE HEARING ROOM IS USED ONCE. A man comes in who has not been in that room before and is not going to be again: Amos Trewhella, of an office in a city whose name the clerk wrote down and this yard has not repeated, with a paper, and he does not sit down for ten minutes.** He reads four things: **the *Vigil* is not to be employed upon this coast; Captain Veyra Holt is relieved of that vessel and of the command of this coast from the fifteenth of August and is posted from the first of September to the making up of the coast's wreck lists, in a city, at a table, with every vessel reported, claimed, entered and closed upon this coast for the year — SHE DOES NOT COME TO THIS HARBOR AND HAS NOT BEEN IN IT AND IS NOT GOING TO BE, and she is described from a distance and appears in no room; the second season of the writ of the first of May is not granted, refused or dealt with by the paper, is not before the office it comes from, and the holder will be told when it has been decided and not before, WHICH IS THE WRIT'S OWN LAST LINE GIVEN BACK TO HIM BY ANOTHER OFFICE; and he asks for the writ, the crew's book and the four returns.** **THE YARD PRODUCES ALL FOUR INSIDE ELEVEN MINUTES, WHOLE AND UNSHORTENED, and says out loud why it is opening the book, and the reason is the clause's own construction and has been since the second of May. The shipwright does not open the book. He points at the lead plug's line and says he is not going to say one word about that number to you or to anybody and never has. He names the eighth of August and the seventh of August out loud, with no softening, and the man in the coat reads the seventh twice and does not comment on it.** Four questions and four answers: they went for four persons and nothing else and are not going to say they nearly had it; no, they will not claim it; if it were raised it would go to four people of the Sill by their own hand and not by his, under a limit they wrote and he asked to have lifted; then produce the writ. **And the standing sentence is said out loud without softening — has not been told, has not asked, does not want to know this month, will read a paper about the second season, will answer it on the day it comes, and will not treat the man who brings it as a friend or as an enemy — and the yard adds that a man in a coat was let up the stairs BECAUSE of that standing arrangement, which is the point.** The clerk enters what a box can hold and says for the seventh time what he cannot enter: a person of no office having five bells at five and a half fathoms inside the four hundred yards on a vessel claimed whole, when the officer of this harbor authority has entered that he has nothing that will put a boat of his past those marks. **THE POSTING IS ENTERED IN THE YARD'S OWN BOOK WITH ITS DATES AND CHECKED AGAINST THE FOURTH THING OF THE FIFTH OF JULY, and the check is on the page: it is not a command in this harbor, not an inspection of or over the harbor authority, not a commission, puts nobody past the outer marks and nobody past the imperial line, and the check holds. And the second season is not granted and the writ runs to the first of March and its ground is not widened and its four names are four.** On the Sunday, in a market, in about forty people's hearing, **a man of a boat of the second lay, sixty, forty-one years on this water, asks the volume's question: WHO CHANGED THE RULE. Nobody answers for half a minute.** He does not want a letter, he wants the answer or the not-answering said out loud. **Calder Marr says he does not know, says that a man who has never heard the question says *what rule* and a man who has heard it says *I do not know*, says he has thought about it for five days and is not going to go round the side of a limit four people put on their own account, and cannot give a name because he does not know what the rule was called or what it changed from, and because in four years somebody would be standing on a mole in another town with the name in his hand and no lead in his water — and that is a thing this harbor has already had done to it once.** **And he is the one who says it out loud, in the yard's own hand with a date on it, so that a man can come up this hill and ask whether it was put and whether it was answered, and the answer is that it was put on the thirteenth of August in about forty people's hearing and was not answered and the not-answering is on the page with the reason against it. THEN ELIAS VOKE SAYS THE SENTENCE THE VOLUME CLOSES ON AND WILL NOT SAY IT TWICE: nineteen winters in a naval file because a reeve of this harbor sold a chart for them and did not have to and said afterwards in a letter of four lines that he would have asked and would have done it anyway — and that is one of them true and it is not that the other one is not true, both of those are true and neither of them cancels the other, and he has been saying that shape of sentence for forty years and has never had anybody write it down. WRITE IT DOWN. I AM NOT GOING TO SAY IT AGAIN. And Calder Marr writes it in the first column and Voke makes him read the last sentence back twice.** And nothing in that market answered the question, and the entry says so.

### Chapter 150 — Under a Naval Paper, and the Wage of the Fifteenth

Monday the fourteenth and Tuesday the fifteenth of August, in the market office press, on a market wall, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **THE VOLUME'S FINAL IMAGE. THE SHEET GOES UP ON A MARKET WALL UNDER A NAVAL PAPER, ON TUESDAY THE FIFTEENTH OF AUGUST AT ABOUT ELEVEN IN THE MORNING, IN A MARKET, IN DAYLIGHT, ON THE SAME NAILS, ON THE OUTSIDE OF WHATEVER WAS THERE — and what was there is the *Vigil's* notice of the fourth of August with the Crown stamp off by about a sixteenth of an inch to the right, which is over the *Sallow Reach's* printed sheet, which is over the sheet of the seventh of June, which is over the yard's tenth thing, and the yard's own fourth return of the twenty-second of July has been under four papers since the twenty-eighth of July and is still on that wall and can be read if a man stands at the side of it in the right wind. THE ARRANGEMENT IS THE ARRANGEMENT AND THE YARD DID NOT DECIDE IT AND THE SHIPWRIGHT SAYS SO OUT LOUD AND REFUSES TO PRETEND A SHEET UNDER A NAVAL PAPER IS A SHEET ON ITS OWN.** The sheet carries, in its own words: **the place of every copy of it** — copied at the foot of the dock at no price on the day, forty out of the press on the eleventh, one up the coast, about sixty boats, one copy in a city going to press that cannot be stopped, four of the eleven copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October not reached and not going to be; **the nine places, as this yard is able to state them, with a date against each, and it does not call them a chain and it does not call them a compact and THE WORD *HARBORS* IS NOT ON IT ANYWHERE** — six by a builder's own figures at this harbor's own second seam, forty, seventy-three, one hundred and forty-one, two hundred and ten, two hundred and seventy-nine and three hundred and forty-eight feet, the builder's name cut into a stone at the mole head, the day given and the year not on it because the book gives no year in any hand this yard can read; and three as the books give them, this harbor's own weir called the Sill with the third of the six lying a foot and four inches under its sill stone, a village not on this coast and not on any chart anybody here has seen with twenty-two children and nine named, and a sand and shell lay not on any chart in this harbor; **and it says that this yard does not know why the three are there and is not going to guess, and that one of the nine is under the south end of this mole and four of that nine were living in this town on Friday last, and says nothing else about them; and the two columns, nine lines of the first and five of the second, each of the five carrying its reason in full, both standing, neither scratched out, a request to strike one out refused; and the limit, and the five bells at five and a half fathoms, and the going past the outer marks and the broken sentence with the breaking entered in advance and the cost on the page for anybody who comes up the hill.** **AND THE WAGE OF THAT WEEK FELL ON THE SAME MORNING AT ABOUT ELEVEN AND WAS NOT PAID, and the bag was empty and had been since the evening of Tuesday the eighth, and the envelope was not made, and the whole of it is in the first column at about ten past eleven in the morning with the figure against it: four marks, four marks short, spent on a pot of air and on a man of Ashtide, not borrowed, not asked of the harbor authority or any office, not paid in part.** The third column is filled at about an hour's lateness — the shortest since the fourth of April — in the ship's hand, and **nobody reads it back to anybody, and it is the only entry in nineteen days that was not read back twice, and the entry above it says why and the reason is hers and was given in advance.** And the read-out runs at about five in the afternoon with about nine people in the street and the mole head's light not on, and **the chandler's note is said out loud, in nine people's hearing, as NOT re-written and going to stay un-re-written, with its three counts of weeks standing at twenty-two, thirteen and eleven as last written on the fourth of January, the refusal of the thirty-first of March unreversed, and the reason that re-writing a note with nothing in the bag is not a payment, said in a market on the third of May and said again today.** The delayed note has not moved, the delay is a hand's width of a second and was not taken and **nobody in this yard has said why and nobody in this harbor has ever said why and this party is not going to be the first.** And the last three lines: **in about four years a bell is going to be out in a boat at eight marks and a day of a boat then as it is now, and the whole of the craft is the going out, and it stands unreversed; and the boy who used to read the day back is not on the stone and has not been on it since the fifteenth of May; and there is a pencil in this yard tonight and it is in somebody else's hand.**

---

## Volume 04, Batch 0002 (Chapters 161–170) — complete. Movement 2, the work that cannot be sold while a book is being read.

**REVIEWED AND REPAIRED, 27 SEPTEMBER 2026. A reviewer pass over the ten chapters, the four state files and the volume outline returned FIX with eleven required repairs — of which one was a next-phase prompt the writing phase claimed to have created and had not, and two were state-file sections that did not exist — a list of prose and craft items, and five state-file figures. ALL ARE APPLIED IN PLACE. NO CHAPTER WAS RESTARTED, NO SCENE WAS CUT FOR LENGTH, NO COST WAS DROPPED, NO MOVEMENT BOUNDARY WAS MOVED AND NO PLANNED PLOT WAS ALTERED, and the full disposition is `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0002 (Volume 04) review repairs*, which is the authority for them.** **The five that would have cost the next writer most: `workspace/volume-04/batch-0003/PROMPT.md` DID NOT EXIST and this file and `state/current.md` both said it did — the one queued phase now exists and the two false claims are corrected; THIS FILE AND `state/open-threads.md` HAD NO BATCH 0002 SECTION AT ALL, which is why `state/current.md` was citing a section that was not there; the crew's book was being put into a file in a city on a *future* Saturday in Chapters 165 and 167, three weeks after Chapter 162 put it there on the ninth of September, which reversed the batch's own central beat; the money did not add up in two chapters — a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence against a wage of four is a shortfall of two marks and twelve pence and then of twelve shillings and fourpence, and the batch's two statements of the second of those figures contradicted each other; and the Tranter–Rorke paper chain had three steps whose dates could not all be true, including a refusal reported to a surveyor on a Saturday three days before the yard made it.** Also corrected: three instances of the author talking about the book inside the book; a wage-count in Chapter 161 that contradicted the chapter's own first-column entry eleven lines below it; the read-back ledger in `state/continuity.md`, which claimed seven chapters and named the wrong six; the far-bench count, which was six in one state file and seven in another and is **five** in the chapters; and three checkable figures in dialogue with nothing behind them.

**Actual word count: 29,289 words across ten chapters as re-measured after this batch's review repairs, and 28,941 before them** (Ch161 3,214 · Ch162 2,426 · Ch163 3,259 · Ch164 2,447 · Ch165 2,601 · Ch166 2,800 · Ch167 2,777 · Ch168 3,150 · Ch169 2,722 · Ch170 3,893), measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Every chapter is above the floor. Seven are inside the guideline and three are past it — Chapter 161 by fourteen words, Chapter 163 by fifty-nine, and Chapter 170 by six hundred and ninety-three — and all three ran because a cost could not be dropped: 170 is the batch's close and is one complete room in a market office with a shed coda, and 163 is three rooms on two days carrying the batch's only sitting, and 161 is past by a margin no reader will meet. CHAPTER 170 IS THE LARGEST CHAPTER OF THE VOLUME SO FAR AND IS LEFT LONG ON THE RECORD AND NOT EXCUSED.** The hearing room is used **once**, on **Saturday the sixteenth of September**. The market wall is used **not at all** in this batch. The market office is used **once**, on **Friday the twenty-ninth of September**, and the three rooms share no day.

**THE MONEY, AND IT CLOSES CHECKABLE ON THE PAGE: A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE in a bag on the morning of Monday the eleventh of September, and A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE in it on the evening of Saturday the thirtieth. A mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, so the whole of the arithmetic in this batch hangs on those two figures and both of them are checkable in the prose: 308 pence in, 788 pence after a three-mark day, 148 pence after a wage, 948 pence after a five-mark day, 308 pence after a wage.** The chain across the five days money moves is **1m 12s 4d · 4m 12s 4d · 12s 4d · 5m 12s 4d · 1m 12s 4d**: a day at the second seam's own face on Thursday the fourteenth, gross fifteen and the yard's three, the eighth and the share off the top; a wage of four on Tuesday the nineteenth, which leaves twelve shillings and fourpence and not one mark and twelve shillings and fourpence, and that other mark and twelve shillings and fourpence has been in the bag since the twenty-ninth of August; a day at the same face on Friday the twenty-second, gross seventeen and the yard's five; a wage of four on Tuesday the twenty-sixth. **Four wage Tuesdays fall in this batch and TWO OF THEM WERE NOT PAID: the fifth of September and the twelfth. The fifth was entered unpaid on the twelfth and not before, with the reason against the line, and the number of weeks fallen due this year with no envelope standing against them is TWO, being the fifteenth of August and the fifth of September, entered in the same breath in every place it is given.** The column goes **eighteen lines and seventy-two marks · nineteen lines and seventy-six marks · twenty lines and eighty marks**, the fifth of March the oldest throughout, and **thirteen envelopes · fourteen · fifteen**. The note is not re-written, its three counts of weeks unmoved since the fourth of January, nothing in a tin since the twenty-eighth of April, three marks owing to a man of Ashtide unmoved since the eighth of August and he is not named again, seven pence owing to this yard's own shipwright and not paid, five marks of gland standing unpaid and useless, eight marks and a day of a boat to the bellwright. **No money came into this yard from any office in this batch and none went out to any person except two wages.**

**THE READ-OUT IS THE DEVICE AND IT WAS USED ONCE IN THIS BATCH, AND IT FAILED.** The read-out — a man reading figures out of the yard's own book aloud to people who can check them — happens on **Monday the twenty-fifth of September at the head of the dock, in daylight, in about forty people's hearing, off the yard's own book, off the bar of gravel entry whole with the reason against the line, read twice**; two men of that market said afterwards to each other that it was the whole of it, and it did nothing whatever for the man it was done for, and the yard entered both halves. **THE READ-BACK IS NOT THE READ-OUT**: the ship's hand reading the day's entries back from the top of the bench and the holder reading them back after her, and nobody else in that shed, **runs in five of the ten chapters — 161, 163, 164, 167 and 170 — with a variant of it in 168, where the man who asked for the certification is handed back the line in the second column that belongs to him.** A later batch may use the read-out once and may use the read-back on any night it likes without spending anything. **A byte-comparison of every paragraph of twelve words or more across the ten chapters, run after the repairs, returns ZERO duplicates, and there are six exact repeated sentences of eight words or more and every one of them is this yard's standing inventory.**

### Chapter 161 — The Nineteenth Monday, and a Wage That Does Not Go In

Monday the eleventh and Tuesday the twelfth of September, on the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **THE WALL BEHIND THE REEVE'S BENCH CAME DOWN BARE AT EIGHT AND THAT WAS NINETEEN MONDAYS SINCE THE FIRST OF MAY, and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and this party has not put anything on that wall in five months and has entered twice that it is not going to and did not go on on the nineteenth.** The two objects of nobody's are about a hundred and seventeen days up on the crown and neither of them moved and one tarpaulin still has a corner off it since the seventeenth of June. **THE WHOLE OF THE MORNING GOES INTO THE SHED WITH A SLATE: A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE IN A BAG, three hundred and eight pence, written on the slate twice and got the same both times, and a wage of four on the Tuesday, and Nessa Pike says the whole of it out loud and it took about forty seconds.** Then the arithmetic she says next is the batch's opening problem: **that bag is two marks and twelve pence short of a wage, and there is no day of work booked between the Monday and Wednesday, and what he is going to write on the Tuesday is *it was not paid*.** A woman of the third lay books Thursday the fourteenth and says before anything else that she will pay twelve of seventeen at the mole, and that is the first money this yard has earned since the twenty-eighth of August. **ON THE TUESDAY THE WAGE OF FOUR DID NOT GO IN AND THE ENVELOPE WAS NOT MADE, entered in the first column with the figure and the date and the reason against the line, and it is the second Tuesday in a row on which one has fallen due and has not gone in, and the week of the fifth of September is entered on the twelfth and not before, with the reason: a party which begins to enter a wage late and then enters it on a day of its own choosing is a party whose column has stopped being a column and become a diary.** The shipwright puts **seven pence** of her own postage on the book with the date and **her name on it**, at her own asking, because *if it is in the second column it is a feeling and it is not a feeling, it is seven pence and a woman is out of pocket seven pence*. **And the thing they did not go and ask for is entered and refused: the ninth acknowledgement is the last entry in that ledger and a tenth may not be entered, the officer said out loud on the mole's steps on the twenty-seventh of August that his office has been asked twice and has paid twice and cannot pay a third time, and this is entered as a decision and not as a virtue and nobody is going to thank this party for it.** Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days and is asked nothing and Calder Marr does not go over to him.

### Chapter 162 — Three Marks, and a Cistern Charged by Strangers

Wednesday the thirteenth and Thursday the fourteenth of September, on the second seam's own face, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The pump comes off the bench at seven and the pot of air does not come off it at all, because there is no pot in this harbor with air in it that this yard has not paid for out of its own bag at two marks apiece, and the day is going to be worked on the face and not under it.** On the Wednesday morning, in front of about nine people on the crown, the two of them have the argument the whole of this fortnight is made of, and it is not about a figure: **on Saturday morning he is going to open that book in front of a man with a pen and he is not going to read the second column out, and a man in a room writes down the line and not the reason against it, because reasons are long, and reasons do not go into a copy, and the copy went into a file on the ninth of September in about two minutes with a pen — and in four years those five lines are going to be the only thing in forty-seven weeks that this yard knows and will not say.** Thursday is a day at the second seam's own face, landward of the third seam's mark, about five hours, nobody in the water, the hand lead's line over and back four times and not past the outer marks. **Gross fifteen. The eighth two, the share two, the victuals two, a hired hand three, a second hired hand three. Twelve out of fifteen at the mole and the yard's being three, and three went up on the mole's steps in daylight in front of about eleven people and he read all three figures out and said which of them he would rather have had and did not say it.** **AND AT THE SAME HOUR FOUR MEN THIS YARD DOES NOT KNOW CHARGED THE CISTERN BEHIND THE ENGINE HOUSE at no figure, because it is on a rota and a Monday and a Thursday and has never been this yard's rota, and this party walked it thirteen times between the end of May and the third of August and has not walked it since, and the reason is a working one and is on the page.**

### Chapter 163 — A Gland, a Note, and a Sitting at Ten in the Morning

Friday the fifteenth and Saturday the sixteenth of September, at the mole's toe off the second seam, in the shed, and in the hearing room over the tide-gang store. **Five marks of compressor's gland fitted to this yard's own pump on the nineteenth of May, on a creditor's page, unpaid, and of no use whatever because the thing after the gland is the hose; this harbor has no delivery hose and the longest thing of hose in it is about nine fathoms and belongs to a man of the second lay and is lent.** A man of the second lay books a day for the Friday following at his own figure; the work is ballast shingle against a wet stone at the toe of the second seam and it wants a hose, and at eight in the morning he is told it cannot be done, **the booking is refused, no money changes hands either way, and he says he had known since Tuesday and had come to have it on the book.** Then the note, because a shilling did not go in on it: **then we do not re-write it — nine words and a reason — and thirty-one stands, and twenty-two and thirteen and eleven as last written on the fourth of January, and a creditor's page in this town would rather say a true thing about this yard than a new one.** **SATURDAY, TEN IN THE MORNING, THE HEARING ROOM, AND THE PARTY IS IN A ROOM FOR THE SECOND TIME IN A WEEK, UN-REPRESENTED, WITH THE BOOK IN ITS BAG.** Ambrose Sell, clerk of the circuit office and clerk of that sitting, says his name and his office before he reads it, and reads a direction of three things: the inquiry stands directed to a sitting **in the week following the twelfth day of October next**, a paper naming the day to be served and not left; the party may apply in writing **at its own cost** for a copy of any record, and that grants no copy and makes none and releases nothing and no date attaches; and the construction of the second paragraph of the notice of the tenth stands and nothing touches the second season. **Then three things that are not on the paper: the power to seat one of two disputants is the inquiry's and is NOT THE WRIT'S POWER and did not come out of the writ; the party is not represented and has retained nobody; and there is a record in a city in a file and he will not say what is in it and will not say why, and in October he will give the same answer.** All six items of the fourth paragraph are produced, **the crew's book a second time, whole and unshortened, in about nine minutes, and not one line of it is read aloud in that room and no copy of it is made in that room**, and a form of four sentences goes on the table face up and is not read out either.

### Chapter 164 — A Man Who Came Up the Hill and Was Not Asked Anything

Sunday the seventeenth and Monday the eighteenth of September, on the mole's crown, in the shed, and at the head of the dock. **DENZIL HARE, of the third lay, about fifty, a boat of his own and no crew of his own since the spring, comes up the hill in the rain and says where a boy was — a fore berth from about the sixth to about the twenty-first of June, paid out of his own pocket and not out of a wage — and says he has been standing in the yard for four minutes waiting to be asked one question, and he is not asked one, and the yard enters that it wanted to say thank you and did not and why.** **THE THIRD COLUMN, which has not been filled since the first of August, and the shipwright says in the first column that she did not fill it and thought about it for about a minute and a half and is the only one here who could have done it with two strokes and nobody would ever have known, and the reason is that it is not hers and not a column of facts, and a column kept by a shipwright in a shed on a Sunday is a column about a shipwright.** And in the doorway the holder answered the man of the third lay twice, and the second answer was not the answer to the second thing, and **the one naming Volume 04 was allowed is spent here: in the second column, in his own left hand, with a reason against the line, on land, that he cannot always tell which of two things he said in the last minute was the one he had decided to say, and it is a tide-sickness and has been got since the fourteenth of October of last year and is not going off.** The four nameless lines above the named one of the twentieth of July are still four, are still nameless, and are not struck out. The wall came down bare on the Monday for the twentieth time. Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days.

### Chapter 165 — A Wage That Goes In, and a Mooring That Is Not This Yard's

Tuesday the nineteenth and Wednesday the twentieth of September, at the head of the dock, in a doorway, and in the shed. **THE WAGE OF FOUR GOES IN AT ELEVEN IN THE MORNING into an envelope in the holder's own left hand with the week on the outside, and the column is nineteen lines and seventy-six marks and the bag is twelve shillings and fourpence and there are fourteen envelopes on the bench and two weeks fallen due this year with no envelope standing against them.** The whole of it goes up on the mole's steps in daylight in front of about eleven people and takes about two minutes, **and includes that this is a yard of two and a half people whose book went into a file in a city on the ninth of September and cannot be got back out of it, and that he is not going to say one word about that.** A man of the first lay asks on the Wednesday whether the mooring at the ninth seam would take a boat of about ninety tons, and the yard says no in about eleven sentences: **that mooring is harbor ground, the office that has power over it has had an empty repair account since the middle of February, and what this yard can do is tell him what three things in its own book say and who said them, and take nothing for it, and be told not to be thanked.** The three things are the end of it entered on the third of July, the four walks out to that mooring by **Tam Rundle** of the tide gang and **Danno Fisk** of the third lay on their own time, and the finding of it in service on the first of September at about four feet on the flood. **The favor belongs to the man of the third lay who did the forty minutes, and the yard is not going to be thanked for it and has not been, and a man of the first lay is sent to a tide gang that is four men short, where a day and a half of the gang is the whole of the price.**

### Chapter 166 — A Sheet Off a Stall in a City, and Two Figures That Cannot Check Each Other

Thursday the twenty-first and Friday the twenty-second of September, on the second seam's own face, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **WIL TRANTER, of the first lay, fifty-one, master of a coaster of about a hundred and ninety tons of his own, comes up the hill on the Friday afternoon with a printed sheet bought off a stall in a counting office in a city for a penny, and has read it twice on the boat coming up and is sick about the second time.** He wants to know which of two figures on it is right, not for the paper, for him, and he has crossed that bar on twenty-two springs and put her on it once. **The yard refuses to tell him and refuses to say it does not know, because it does know that it does not know; a figure made once is a figure and not a certificate, and a printed thing cannot be un-printed.** A man with a brush says somebody ought to go and look at five miles of that coast, and the yard says it is on the face of the sheet in its own words and **that a yard of two and a half people that has a book in a file in a city is not the thing to ask, and that hiring a boat is, and that is about four days with a man on a lead and none of them can pay for it.** **And then the other half: he has a paper of his own that came on the Saturday boat, a man's own paper about a boat of his own, in which a line says a vessel of a hundred and ninety tons could not have crossed that bar on the second of August with eleven feet six aft, and the man who wrote it has never been on this coast and is asking him whether he will put his word under his line. He has not said yes and has not said no, and he came up here first, and he is told: nothing — come back on it.** The cistern is charged on the Thursday, **the sixth rota day of this month**, by strangers, and the yard worked about four hours and forty minutes on the Friday for a gross of seventeen and its own five.

### Chapter 167 — Thirty Marks, and Four Hundred Feet of a Woman's Warp

Saturday the twenty-third and Sunday the twenty-fourth of September, in the shed, in a doorway, and at the head of the dock. **A piece of paper folded in two in the back of the writ of the first of May comes out for the only reason that puts a man's hand on it, which is a bag with twelve shillings and fourpence in it, and the back of it is nine sentences refusing thirty marks in coin for a license to go and look at a road.** He reads it out slowly, twice, because it is in his own hand and he wanted to know whether it still said what he thought it said, and it did. **And the number behind it, said out loud once in four volumes: about four hundred feet of a woman's own warp came up off the bottom of this harbor on Friday the twenty-eighth of July at twenty-six marks and a day off a man of Ashtide, and the thirty marks refused in June would have paid for about four hundred feet of Sella Brigg's warp, and this yard owes her eight shillings and tenpence of it and has not paid it and is not going to this month — and a refusal once made is a thing you are carrying, and it is not going to be in a column and a figure in October either.** The paper goes back in the back of the writ and the book in the bag is forty-seven weeks of this yard's own weather and its own shame **and is in a file in a city and is not coming back out of it.** On the Sunday **Sella Brigg comes up the hill to say three things and to ask for nothing: that the figure is true and she would rather this yard did not pay her a shilling of it to stop a man talking, because a man in a market who is owed something is a man somebody will be angry at, and she would rather he was angry at the yard than at her; that she is not going to be a name on this yard's public sheet and will take her own warp off it in front of people if one goes on; and that she is not going to ask the yard to say anything about her in a market. And her name and her figure go into the FIRST column at her own asking, with the hour against it.**

### Chapter 168 — Asked to Certify, and a Figure Read Out at the Head of the Dock

Monday the twenty-fifth and Tuesday the twenty-sixth of September, on the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, in a doorway, and in the shed. **The wall came down bare for the twenty-first time and no sitting has been held in that room since the sixteenth.** Tranter comes up at half past ten with **the paper he told them about on Friday** and it is not the penny sheet: **Ailsa Rorke, surveyor, Fourteen Wharf**, reporting on the total loss of the coaster *Ardent* on the second of August, and finding that a vessel of eleven feet six could not have crossed ground barred to two and three-quarter fathoms at twenty past two, and recording that **she has applied in writing to the yard and has had no answer, and records the yard as declining to certify the figure, and records on the face of that entry that she has not had it from the yard and has it from a man of the first lay who crossed the water to tell her so.** Tranter says he told the surveyor **in a letter in the first week of September, before he came up the hill on the Friday**, that a yard which has printed a figure of its own cannot be got to swear to it afterwards, **that the surveyor had that in her hand when she wrote the line, and that he has not sent this party one word of that letter and is not going to, and has not asked the yard yet and is going to now.** **And the yard says no, and gives four working reasons: a certification is a conclusion with a man's name under it; the figure was made once on the second of May in a quarter of an hour with a hand lead's line and a slate; there is a man of fifty-four who has made two and a half on the same bar four times in nineteen years and it is not the yard's to decide which is right; and a figure about water is not an answer to a question about a boat and a cargo of eleven hundred tons. And the fourth: it is in print already, on purpose, and a printed thing cannot be certified afterwards without becoming a different kind of thing, and he has found out what his name is worth this month and it is a sheet of paper at a shilling for forty copies.** **THE READ-OUT, USED ONCE IN THIS BATCH: at about half past twelve on the Monday, at the head of the dock, in daylight, in about forty people's hearing, the holder reads the whole of the bar of gravel entry out of his own book — the place, the hour, the lead, the slate, the four times over, the flood and the ebb — with the reason against the line, and reads it twice. Two men of that market say afterwards to each other that it was the whole of it. IT DID NOTHING FOR THE MAN IT WAS DONE FOR, and the yard entered that it did not and that it had not lied to him, and that those are one fact.** The wage of four goes in on the Tuesday.

### Chapter 169 — What a Refusal Became in a City

Wednesday the twenty-seventh and Thursday the twenty-eight of September, in a doorway, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **Wil Tranter comes up with a letter in a coat pocket having not written to this party, and says so first: he wrote to the surveyor on the Monday night, he did not send a copy, and he wrote back on Tuesday night and it came up on the Wednesday with the morning boat.** The letter enters: *The yard of Morrow Quay, whose sheet is set in type in this city, gives the bar of that harbor at two and three-quarter fathoms on the flood; declines to certify the figure; and will not be held to it.* **FOUR WORDS IN THAT ENTRY WERE NEVER SAID BY THIS PARTY, the surveyor says so himself, asks whether they are the yard's own words, and gives his reason for entering them: a man who will not certify and will not be held to is a man standing behind a figure and does not wish to be examined, and he would rather put it down and be wrong than leave it off and be taken apart in a room he has not been in.** Calder Marr says the thing that had been sitting at the back of the fortnight, and it is the first time since the second of May that he has said anything he had not written down first: **a refusal is a sentence in a market, this yard has spent five months learning to publish one and is on the receiving end of one, and a party that publishes itself is not a party that gets to be sorry in a street and mean it — and he does not get to write to the man, and he would like it noticed that the man is the one who came.** And Tranter gives the rest of it: **there are three people on this coast who can put a trade at the foot of a line, there are two in this town who do it for money, and there is one who does it for nothing and is owed forty marks first after food by this harbor's own work since the fifteenth of October, and she is not on this crew, and you are not going to pay her — and it is not because you are a liar, it is because there is nobody in your name at all.** Nessa Pike then says, once, in the yard, that one of those two numbers is the depth of that bar and she does not know which and neither does anybody in this harbor and neither does anybody in the city, **and that she is not going to say that again in a shed for about four years and is not going to have either of them improve on it in a market.**

### Chapter 170 — The Price That Is Not Six Marks and Two Days

Friday the twenty-ninth and Saturday the thirtieth of September, in the market office of Morrow Quay, and in the shed. **HE WENT IN. That is the whole of the change and it took twenty-one days to happen and nobody arranged it.** On the eighth he had stood on the steps of that office for eighty minutes and not gone in, and he entered both halves of that with the date and the hour. **On the twenty-ninth he comes in at about ten past ten into a room open to anybody with the counting going on and about eleven people in it, and she is there with a book of her own, a pencil and her own brass calipers, and the first thing she says is *you stood on the steps*, and the answer is *for eighty minutes, and I did not go in, and I did not send anybody in, and I did not ask you afterwards what you had said, and all three of those are in my own book with the date on them and I have not taken one of them out*.** He asks for **one line, in her own trade's words, with her name on it and her price on it, saying what a lump of green glass off a mole's face is and what a chip of worked glass is, and whether the two of them are the same thing** — and he says he has not got her price and is not going to pay it and wants to be told it anyway. **She prices it before she answers it, and the price is not money and is not six marks and two days: it is an undertaking, in his own book, in his own left hand, in the first column, with a date on it, that this party does not ask her for anything again — not at a figure, not at nothing, not for a line, not for a name, not in a room, not in a shed, not in a market, and not in about two years when he will have had a bad month and need her to say that a thing is what it is.** And the reason, in her own words: **a conservator you cannot pay is a conservator you have to keep asking, and asking me to be useful is asking me to be cheap — and in about two years there is a page in a city with a trade on it and a name on the trade, and then somebody says *the conservator* in a room, and by then it does not mean a trade, it means a woman, and a woman can be applied to, and asked, and produced, and made to answer for what a yard found and for what a yard did not find.** **THE ANSWER IS NO, at a figure and at nothing, and she says out loud in eleven people's hearing that she has not done a thing at nothing since the fourth of August and will not start on a Friday morning because the man who stood on the steps has come in at last. No money changed hands, no sheet was asked for at the press, no shilling was paid, and the slab was not shifted.** He puts the whole of it in the first column including the fact that he came in, and does not thank her and does not say he is sorry. **AND IN THE SECOND COLUMN, THE ONE THING THAT IS NOT ENTERED: he has NOT entered in the first column that he intends never to ask her again, and has entered why he has not — that a man who undertakes not to ask is a man who has decided that a person is a door.** The third column was not filled on the thirtieth. The month closes on a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence in a bag, the same figure it opened on, two wages paid, two wages not paid, the cistern walked on none of its eight charges, and a working harbor poorer on the last page of the month than on the first.

## Volume 04, Batch 0003 (Chapters 171–180) — complete. Movement 3, the woman whose work is being used, and the file with a page out of it.

**34,984 words across ten chapters**, Thursday the first to Tuesday the twentieth of October 2026, measured with `wc -w` including headings, against the 2,200–3,200 guideline. **Five chapters are inside the guideline and five are past it, and all five ran because a cost could not be dropped: 176 carries the discovery, the break with the Registry and the whole of the doorway in two days; 178 carries a paper served in a doorway and a sitting in a room on two days; 171 carries two days, a new named working person and a refusal posted; 175 carries a day of work and the volume's central document at the same hour on the same Friday; and 180 carries the twenty-fourth wall, a wage and the batch's whole close. THEY ARE LEFT LONG ON THE RECORD AND NOT EXCUSED, EXACTLY AS CHAPTER 170 OF BATCH 0002 WAS.** The hearing room is used **once**, on **Friday the sixteenth of October**. The market wall is used **not at all** in this batch. The market office is used **once**, on **Tuesday the thirteenth of October**, and no two of the three rooms share a day.

**THE MONEY, AND IT CLOSES CHECKABLE ON THE PAGE IN PENCE: A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS AND FOURPENCE in a bag on the morning of Thursday the first of October, and A MARK AND TWELVE SHILLINGS in it on the evening of Tuesday the twentieth. A mark is a hundred and sixty pence and a shilling is twelve, so the whole of the arithmetic in this batch hangs on those two figures: 308p · 304p · 304p · 1,104p · 464p · 944p · 304p.** **THE BATCH CLOSES FOUR PENCE LIGHTER THAN IT OPENED, AND THE FOUR PENCE IS THE POSTAGE OF ONE LETTER IN THE ORDINARY POST, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE OF WHAT THIS YARD HAS SPENT ON ITSELF IN TWENTY DAYS.** The chain: a wage of four on Tuesday the sixth **NOT PAID**, envelope not made, column twenty-one lines and eighty-four marks, **and the number of weeks fallen due with no envelope standing against them is THREE and not two**; a day at the second seam's own face on Friday the ninth, gross seventeen, the yard's **five**, the eight and the share off the top; a wage of four on Tuesday the thirteenth, column twenty-two lines and eighty-eight marks, sixteen envelopes; a day at the same face on Wednesday the fourteenth, gross fifteen, the yard's **three**; a wage of four on Tuesday the twentieth, column **twenty-three lines and ninety-two marks**, seventeen envelopes. **THE WHOLE OF THE MONEY THAT HAS COME INTO THIS YARD SINCE THE FIFTEENTH OF AUGUST IS TWENTY-SIX MARKS; THE WHOLE THAT WENT OUT IN THIS BATCH IS EIGHT MARKS AND FOUR PENCE, BEING TWO WAGES OF FOUR AND ONE LETTER AT FOUR PENCE, AND NOTHING ELSE WENT OUT OF THE BAG AT ALL.**

**THE READ-OUT DEVICE WAS NOT USED IN THIS BATCH AND ITS ONE USE PER BATCH IS CARRIED FORWARD.** **THE READ-BACK — the ship's hand reading the day's entries back from the top of the bench and the holder reading them back after her, nobody else in the shed — RUNS IN SEVEN OF THE TEN CHAPTERS, 172, 173, 175, 176, 177, 179 AND 180**, and is not a use of the device and may be used on any night without spending anything. **A byte-comparison of every paragraph of twelve words or more across the ten chapters returns ZERO duplicates; a comparison of every exact sentence of eight words or more returns TWENTY-SIX repeats and every one of them is this yard's standing inventory**, which ran in all ten chapters of the two prior batches also. The full result and the reason none of them is repaired are at `state/continuity.md` § *The duplication audit for Batch 0003*.

### Chapter 171 — A Rota Day, and a Woman With a Counter Off the Fish Market

Thursday the first and Friday the second of October, on the mole's crown, in a doorway, and in the shed. **The cistern behind the engine house was charged at no figure by strangers and this party did not walk it, and that is the first rota day of this month and the fifteenth since the third of August, and this party has walked none of the fifteen.** Alden Marr is at the far bench on both days and is not asked for anything on either. **THE PLATE NOTE STANDS OPEN, AND THE PARTY ENTERS AGAIN THAT WHATEVER CLOSES IT WILL CLOSE IT IN AN OFFICE AND NOT IN A SHED AND THAT IT DOES NOT KNOW WHAT THAT IS.** Then, at about eleven on the Thursday morning, **Pernel Saye, fifty-eight, of this harbor, a copyist, with a counter in a room off the fish market that is open to anybody, where a sheet is a penny and forty sheets a shilling, comes up the hill unasked and at her own cost with a board of papers and asks a man who reads a page for one thing only.** The head of her own sheet says: *Job 4 — Fri. 8 Sept — Morrow circuit office, copying room. From the file of the ASTER GANNET. Leaf 62. One leaf, copied entire, eleven sheets, returned with the file.* **The yard reads those four lines twice and does not read the rest of it and does not ask to, and the number sixty-two goes into the first column in the holder's own left hand with the date against it and her name on the line and a reason against the line, and the reason is that the party cannot check the number, cannot see the file, has not asked for the file and is not going to, and that she has been up those stairs twice in her life and is not going a third time, which anybody may come up this hill and ask her.** And Calder Marr says the thing that makes this the batch's hinge: **he has got a book of his own in a file in a city that cannot be got out of it, and he has not got that file and is not going to ask for it, and there is nobody in this town who can get it for him and if there were he could not pay her.** And on the Friday a **letter went into the letterbox for FOUR PENCE, paid at a counter in the holder's own hand**, restating four working reasons for refusing a man of the first lay the figure of the bar of gravel, and Nessa Pike says the shape of that out loud and is heard, and he posts it, and the whole of his reason is that a man refused in a doorway is going to say so in a market whether the yard writes to him or not, and the third time this yard has published something it has cost four pence and he does not know what the fourth will cost and is not going to find out by not posting a letter.

### Chapter 172 — A Man Reading His Own Book

Saturday the third and Sunday the fourth of October, in the shed. **A man of this yard sat down on Saturday morning with his own crew's book and read it, and it took him until the light went, and nobody had ever asked him to and he had never done it.** Forty-seven weeks, two ruled columns and a third at the head of a page in a boy's hand, written in three hands. He reads the first column all day and knows what every line of it means. **At about half past four in the afternoon he gets to the fourteenth day of August and stops, because he can bring up the first four words of a line he wrote in the second column at about nine that evening and cannot bring up the rest, and the four words are *a man in a boat*, and the rest of it is in his own left hand.** He can say exactly what he did that day on the face of the second seam. He cannot remember writing the line. **Nessa Pike will not read it out to him, and gives the reason: that a person reading a man's second column to him at his own table to be kind is how a column stops being a column and becomes the person who was in the room.** And on the Sunday night he enters that he had decided on the Saturday to tell Mara Venn that he wrote down in the second column on the eighth of September a sentence she told him on the seventh of August not to write down — **and that he did not tell her, and that the reason is not a reason against the telling and is this: that on Saturday he found out he could not remember one line of his own second column, and a man who has found that out is not a man who is going to be able to tell a woman on a mole's steps that he kept something back from her for six weeks and be believed about it.** And she says **you are not going to ask her to be the woman who checks your page.** And the first column carries the Alden days, the third column not filled, the far bench empty on both days for the first time in a month, and the standing decision not to go to the harbor authority for anything, with the ninth acknowledgement named as the reason.

### Chapter 173 — The Twenty-Second Wall, and a Wage That Does Not Go In

Monday the fifth and Tuesday the sixth of October, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall behind the reeve's bench came down bare at eight and that was twenty-two Mondays since the first of May, and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and the cistern was charged by strangers at no figure and this party did not walk it.** And on the Tuesday **the wage of four marks did not go in and the envelope was not made, the third Tuesday in a row, and the number of weeks fallen due this year with no envelope standing against them is now THREE — the fifteenth of August, the fifth of September and the sixth of October — entered in the same breath because a yard which is asked how many weeks it has paid has to say all three at once rather than the one that is easier.** And at about half past two on the Tuesday afternoon **Wil Tranter comes up the hill with a letter he wrote to a surveyor in a city on Sunday night and did not send to this party, and says so first, and reads it out.** The letter refuses to set his word under her finding and gives the reason in full: he did not make the figure, and a man's name under a line about water is the only thing a man of the first lay has that a man of no lay does not have, and he is fifty-one and has a boat and four men. **He says the yard's letter of the second instant reached him in his own wheelhouse on Monday morning and that he read it in four lines in the holder's own hand and did not know what to do with it for four days.** And he says the whole of what it costs: **that a man with a page cannot act on a name that will not go on a page, and that this party has a document from him and cannot put it in front of anybody, and that a copy of a record is a thing nobody in this harbor can check.** And he is not thanked, is paid nothing, is offered nothing, and says out loud that he would rather it was on the page that the yard offered and he would not have it, because in about four years a man in a market is going to say the yard sent a man of the first lay to a city with a question, and both halves are going to be true and only one is going to be the whole of it.

### Chapter 174 — A Day That Was There to Be Booked and Was Not

Wednesday the seventh and Thursday the eighth of October, in the shed, in the market office, and at the head of the dock. **A day of work on the second seam's own face was there to be booked on the Wednesday and was not booked, and the reason is a fact about this harbor and not about this yard: the third lay is short and has been since August, the tide gang is four men short, one herring berth of two is out, and her vessel has a started plank.** Calder Marr will not enter it as *no work found* — **that would be a lie and a flattering one and this column is not the place for either** — and he takes the Friday instead at her figure, and she says she would have taken the Wednesday if she had had it. And on the Thursday, a rota day, the cistern is charged by strangers and this party does not walk it, and **the holder goes down to the market office, buys a penny's worth of nothing, is in a room where eleven people are not talking to him, and asks the press-keeper for nothing** — and she tells him, unasked, that she has set about nine hundred sheets since the fifteenth of August, has never been asked what is on one and has not read one, and has been asked three times this month by men with money to bring the slab forward and has not. **Then the plate note, for about forty minutes, and it does not move: the party entered on the eighth, against itself and with the hour against it, that it does not know whether the removal of a leaf from a file in a room on a second floor of this town is the same thing as the change of politics in an office it has been waiting for since the seventeenth of January, and that it is not going to guess, and that a number in a book is not a change of politics in an office and is not going to be made into one by anybody in that shed.**

### Chapter 175 — Five Marks, and a Counterfoil in a Box Under a Counter

Friday the ninth and Saturday the tenth of October, on the second seam's own face, at the mole's steps, in a room off the fish market, and in the shed. **The yard worked about four hours and forty minutes on the face of the second seam with nobody in the water and a hand lead's line over the side and back four times, and gross on the board was seventeen and the yard's was five, and the five went up the mole's steps in daylight in front of about eleven people and no figure was argued.** And at about half past ten the same morning **Mara Venn, thirty, of no office and of no party, sat down at a copying counter in a room off the fish market and asked for a fair copy of her own November paper at a penny a sheet, and then said the rest of it before it was asked for.** Pernel Saye put her pen down — which she does not do in the middle of a morning — went down and came up with a grey card about the size of a man's hand, and put it on the counter flat and turned it round. **THE CARD IS THE BATCH'S CENTRAL DOCUMENT:** *Morrow circuit office — copying room. Second day of September. Withdrawn: file of the ASTER GANNET, leaf 62. One leaf, to the copying room, copied entire, returned with the file. On the application of a commissioned officer of the Crown Navy, by letter of the first instant, a copy of which letter is on the file. This office agrees to the withdrawal. **Reason entered by this office: risk management.** The leaf is not to be produced. A copy is held.* **And then, in her own trade's words, THE WHOLE OF THE MOVEMENT'S SECOND HALF: "That is a form, and the form is the thing. A page is a thing a person wrote. A form is a thing an office wrote about the page. And when the office writes the form, the form goes in front of the page, because that is what forms are for, and a man in a room in October who is handed the form will read the form. He will not turn it over."** And then the second half of it: the foot of the copyist's job sheet in her own hand says **the copy of leaf 62 is to be read with leaf 88**, and Mara has never seen leaf 88, and leaf 62 is her own refusal of the eleventh of November on four grounds with her trade against her name and the office's stamp on it because she would not let it leave without one — **and the first of the four is that the office will not countersign an instrument that puts a person inside a closure that the same office's own notice created.** And her last line, which is the whole of her position: **"I have got a number that two people in this town can check and neither of us has seen the page. I have got a counterfoil in a box under your counter that is going to be waste paper in four years. I have got my own copy of a page that is in a city. And I have got a slip that says the copy of my page is to be read with a page I have never seen. Three things that all agree with each other and not one of them is the page."** And she pays the penny a sheet and says she has not done a thing at nothing since the fourth of August. And on the Saturday a man of the third lay offers a wharf day for the Monday and **is refused, and the whole of the reason is said out loud and it is a bad one and it is a working one, and he says he has had a Monday out of a month this year as well.**

### Chapter 176 — A Letter With a Leaf Number On It, and a Woman Who Came Up the Hill

Sunday the eleventh and Monday the twelfth of October, in a room off the fish market, at the mole's steps, and in the yard. **On the Sunday a woman of thirty sits at her own table, reads her own writing of the eleventh of November last, and cannot remember the fourth of the four grounds she wrote, and has to look; and she writes that down unsigned, with the reason she has never signed anything since the seventh of April.** Then **the break with the Registry, in her own hand, in four lines, in the ordinary post at half past two, at a penny out of her own pocket: that she will not be cited, quoted, produced, or asked again by that office or by any office of it; that no form, sheet, notation, hand or date of hers is to be used to support an account she did not write and has not read; and that she is not resigning anything, because there is nothing to resign from, and that she has been waiting eleven months to find out what to call the thing and it turns out that it has a name and the name is a form.** And on the Monday **the wall came down bare for the twenty-third time, and the cistern was charged by strangers, and Alden Marr is at the far bench.** And at about four in the afternoon **Mara Venn comes up the hill and sits down in the doorway, and she was not asked and nobody sent her, and the party had not known she was in the harbor**, and she says the three things out loud before she starts: that she is going to say it once in about nine minutes, that she is not going to answer a question, and that she is not going to be helped with anything. And she tells the whole of the card, and the four grounds, and the closure — and then **THE SPINE OF THE BATCH, TWICE OVER: "I am not going to give you the line. On the twenty-ninth of September I priced you a line in my own trade's words with my name and my price on it, and I said no, and the price was not six marks and two days and it was not money, it was an undertaking in your own first column in your own left hand that this party does not ask me for anything again. And the price has not changed. A file has come to us. That is not a reason and it is not a hardship."** And: **"if I wrote that line out again on this bench this afternoon in this yard's own book, in my own hand, with my name and my trade and a date on it, and you had it a week before the sitting, it would be worth precisely nothing and you would know it and so would I, and the reason is that a line of mine in a book of yours is a document, and the Registry's page is a document, and a man in a room in October holds two documents and they are the same instrument and only one of them has a seal on it, and the one without the seal is mine.
