That is how a person of no office becomes a thing a person with an office can use."** And he does not thank her and does not say he is sorry and does not ask her one question and says why: **a party which has been refused once and then comes up a hill with a second errand is a party which has decided that a person is a door.** And she says: **good, and I am not going to ask either of you for anything, and I want that on your page, because in about two years one of you is going to want something.** And she tells them, last and out loud, that **the first of her four grounds is about a plate in eleven feet in this mole, and that she came up this hill four days in advance of either of them wanting to ask her, and that neither of them is going to ask.** And Nessa Pike says the sentence the batch closes on: **"This yard has a number in its first column off a copying job sheet. It has a woman in a room off the fish market it cannot pay. It has a file on a second floor it has not asked for and is not going to. And it is going into a room on Friday morning with a book, and it is not represented, and it is going to be told what it means. Four things and not one of them can be put in front of anybody."** And **he did not tell her about the seventh of August, and the second column says so with the reason and the date, and the reason is the reason of the fourth and there is nothing new to add to it and he is not going to invent a second one.**

### Chapter 177 — A Wage That Goes In, and a Man Who Came Up the Hill Asking the Wrong Yard

Tuesday the thirteenth and Wednesday the fourteenth of October, at the head of the dock, in the market office, in the yard, and in the shed. **The wage of four marks goes in at about eleven in the morning into an envelope in the holder's own left hand with the week on the outside, and the column is twenty-two lines and eighty-four marks, sixteen envelopes on the bench, three weeks fallen due, and about nine people in the street say nothing at all about the third in a row, which is what about nine people say about a wage in a hard month.** And at about ten that morning **in the market office, which is open to anybody, a man of the first lay named Oswin Rede, about fifty, comes in with a letter from a city and asks the room, in about nine people's hearing, what a form says, and there is a person in this room on a Tuesday and a Friday morning who would know and she was not in it on the said morning, and had said at the counter that she is not coming on Tuesdays or Fridays any more, that the reason is her own and is not going to be given, and that anybody who wants it can ask her and she will not answer that either — and nobody in that room could tell him anything, and about nine people heard a man say out loud that a thing he had paid for was being explained to a city by a woman he had never met and could not pay, and nothing whatever was done about it.** And on the Wednesday **he comes up the hill with the letter anyway, and is told the whole of it in about nine sentences: that the form is not this yard's and is not in this yard's book and is not in this yard's hand, that the woman is not on this crew and is owed forty marks first after food since the fifteenth of October, and that this party is not going to tell him where she is and will say why, and the why is about her and not about him.** And he is sent down the hill and says he is not owed anything by this yard and that nobody in it has asked him for a thing and that he is not going to be a man who was helped. And the yard works about four hours and fifty minutes on the second seam's own face and the board is fifteen and the yard's is three, and the eight and the share come off the top.

### Chapter 178 — A Paper Served in a Doorway, and a Sitting at Ten in the Morning

Thursday the fifteenth and Friday the sixteenth of October, in a doorway, and in the hearing room over the tide-gang store on the west basin. **Ambrose Sell, clerk of the circuit office and clerk of that sitting, serves a paper of six paragraphs in the doorway of the shed at about eleven in the morning in front of three people, and says his name and the name of his office before he reads it.** The paper appoints the sitting for **Friday the sixteenth instant at ten in the morning**; requires the four parties to attend; requires the six things of the fourth paragraph of the notice of the tenth again and says that producing them is not a concession and is not an admission; says a schedule is read into the record and no copy is given to any party; **states that the registry file of the ASTER GANNET is before the inquiry, that one leaf is called for and is not produced, and that the reason of the non-production is on a form of the Registry's own dated the second of September which is not before the sitting and is not to be produced to the party**; and says nothing touches the second season. **And the magistrate's name is on the original and not on the copy: Halven Rook, magistrate of this circuit, who has not been in this harbor and is not going to be, and the clerk says the truth about it, which is that it is on a paper and not in a room.** And he says two things that are not on the paper: that he has read what the Registry's form says and will not say it **and will not say that he has read it**, because in about four years a man in a city is going to be able to say that a clerk of this circuit knew what a page said and could not deny it; and that the party is not represented and he is going to have to do that again in about forty minutes. And on the Friday **the wall behind the reeve's bench is bare and has been since Monday the twelfth, and about nine people are in the room, and the party is not represented, and it produces the six things whole and unshortened in about eleven minutes, and NOT ONE LINE OF THE CREW'S BOOK IS READ ALOUD, and no copy of any record is made in that room.** Then **the clerk reads the schedule into the record in about four minutes, and a list is a list: the file of the ASTER GANNET fourth on it, leaf 62 called for, leaf 62 not produced, the reason on a Registry form of the second of September, the form not produced, and the two words are not in the schedule and are not read out in that room.** And at about twenty minutes to eleven **the clerk asks one question, and it is the batch's cost: *is there any person in this harbor whose hand is on a page of that file and who is not an officer of any office in it* — and Calder Marr stands up in front of about nine people and says yes, and that she is not on this crew and is not of this yard and is not a holder and is not on the four names, and that he is not going to give her name and is not going to say that she is in this harbor, and that he has entered both with reasons, and that he wants two things said: that there is a person, because there is, and that a party which published itself in July is now going to have a line in a record in a city saying there is a woman in this harbor the yard of Morrow Quay will not name.** And the clerk writes it down and reads it back and enters that too. And nobody asked about the second column, nobody asked about the third, nobody asked the question the holder has had in his own second column since the tenth of September, and nobody mentioned the word.

### Chapter 179 — A Tin Cup on the End of a Bench, and a Column Nobody Fills

Saturday the seventeenth and Sunday the eighteenth of October, in the shed, and on the mole's crown. **Nothing happened on the Saturday and the party entered that, and the reason against the line is that a day on which nothing happens still happened and the only way it gets into a book that can be read in a room in a fortnight is by somebody writing it down on the day.** A man of the first lay of about ninety tons asks about a thing on his stem and is told that a man of this yard does not turn a thing over for nothing and is not taking it on in October, and says he had not expected it in October and will come again in the spring. A man of the third lay says in passing that **the cistern was charged on the Thursday and not on the Monday**, and the party enters that it did not know that and does not know it now. And at about half past four in the afternoon **Nessa Pike says the thing she has been carrying since Monday: she could fill the third column today, she has got nine sentences, they are what a woman of thirty said on the bench on Monday the twelfth in about nine minutes in about six people's hearing, and she is not going to write them — because the third column is a page where a man wrote down what he said when he was not in the boat, and that is the only reason it has ever been worth anything, and if those nine sentences go in it then this yard is holding a page about what a woman said to it in a doorway, and in about four years there is going to be a page in a city that says what a yard of Morrow Quay knew in October, and anybody can read it.** And then the second thing, **and it is the spine moving again and it moves because of her: "You are going to tell me or you are not. I am not going to ask you. I am not going to ask you tonight and I am not going to ask you in a market and I am not going to ask you in a room in a city in four years, and I want it on your page tonight in your own hand that you had a week to ask me and that you did not."** He writes it down. And on the Sunday at about eleven in the morning **he puts a tin cup of water on the end of the far bench and goes back to his own bench and says nothing; the man does not turn round and does not drink it and does not refuse it and says no word, and the cup is still there on Monday morning at nine and is not there at four in the afternoon, and neither brother saw who took it and neither brother has said a word about it.** And at about four he says out loud that he has done that once and is not going to do it again, and why: **that a cup of water on the end of a bench is a question in a language he made up, and he does not get to make up a language for him.**

### Chapter 180 — The Twenty-Fourth Wall, and a Reason Against the Line

Monday the nineteenth and Tuesday the twentieth of October, at the mole's steps, at the head of the dock, and in the shed. **The wall came down bare at eight and that was twenty-four Mondays since the first of May, and the clerk kept his own page and did not mention it, and a sitting was held in that room on the sixteenth and nothing has been put on that board since.** The cistern is charged by strangers — the sixth rota day of the month and **the nineteenth since the third of August, walked on none of them**. The tin cup is on the far bench at nine and is not on it at four. A man of the third lay comes back about a wharf day and **it is booked for Friday the twenty-third and the figure is not entered because the board is not written until the day, and a figure written on a note is not a figure paid until somebody puts the money on the counter.** And on the Tuesday the wage of four goes in at about eleven, the column is **twenty-three lines and ninety-two marks**, seventeen envelopes, three weeks fallen due. And at about half past nine on the Tuesday night in the shed **the whole of the month is said out loud once, in about four minutes, and it is in the book, and it is the five things the party can do about it, and four of them cost money it has not got**: go to the second floor and ask for the page, which is a request to an office of a third party about a document in that office's own file, and it would be asking a woman of fifty-eight to go up a third time; write to a city, which this party has not done since the third of May; go to the harbor authority and ask for a tenth thing in that ledger, and the ninth is the last and a tenth may not be entered; go to the woman, whose price on the twenty-ninth of September has not moved; **and be in a room and say it — and the party was in a room on Friday and said a different thing, and the thing it said is in a record in a city now and it says there is a woman in this harbor the yard will not name.** And Nessa Pike takes the fifth apart: **"A wall has nothing in it you can be made to answer for. A room has a person in it and a clerk with a pen, and a thing you said forty minutes after you have said it."** And Calder says the other half, and the shape of that sentence is said once, about something new, and nobody in that shed remarks on it and neither of them improves on it: **a hearing is a room this party can stand in for nothing, and what comes out of it goes on a page in a city and cannot be got off, and the room is open and the page is not, and he has been using one of those to talk himself out of the other for five months.** And the first column carries the wage, the envelopes, the three weeks, the pence, the booked day, and **the entry that the reading of this yard's own book in a room HAS NEVER HAPPENED, did not happen on the sixteenth, is coming, and the week of it is not in this party's keeping and is not going to be.** And the second column carries the month's whole account, the four reasons and one figure, and the **seven days Alden Marr was at the far bench — the first, the fifth, the eighth, the twelfth, the sixteenth, the eighteenth and the nineteenth — and neither brother went over and neither brother has said why.** And the last line of the batch: **a working harbor is poorer on the last page of this month than it was on the first page of it, and a yard of two and a half people is poorer with a mark and twelve shillings in a bag than it was with a mark and twelve shillings and fourpence, and both of those are this yard's own figures, and a party that has found out what an office says about itself is not a yard that has found out a thing it can do, and this party has not found out a thing it can do, and it is going to be in a room about the middle of November, and it does not know when.**
